# Concepts

Glossary of forge terms. One paragraph each, with a concrete example from `run-litellm-eval`.

## Run

A single workflow invocation. Created by `forge new`, given a unique id like `run-litellm-eval-96a1da`. Recorded in the `runs` table with status `active` | `complete` | `failed` | `abandoned` (`complete` = settled successfully; `failed` = settled with a required phase that failed or became unreachable; `abandoned` = operator-cancelled), plus the `projectDir` of the directory it was created from. SQLite is the resume state — a run that's parked at a gate survives reboots.

Example: `forge new research-synthesis "litellm-eval" --question "Does LiteLLM solve provider routing?"` creates one run.

## Project

The directory mounted at `/project` in the agent container. Recorded on each run as `runs.project_dir`. Implementer agents see it read-write; red agents see it read-only at the OS level (FORGE-DEC-006).

On `forge new` / `forge invoke`, forge resolves the mount target before launching any container (FG-374, FORGE-DEC-022):

- **Not inside a git repo** — mounts cwd (or `--project <dir>`) unchanged.
- **Inside a git repo, no `--project` flag** — resolves up to the repo root (requires `.forge` or `package.json` there); prints an informational notice. Hard-fails if no confident root is found.
- **`--project <subdir>` pointing inside a git repo** — hard-fails in automation (no TTY or `--json`); warns-and-honors in interactive sessions. Pass `--allow-subproject` to override in both contexts.

Override with `--project <dir>` to target a different repo from your cwd. Pass `--allow-subproject` when you intentionally want a subdir mount (records `explicitSubproject: true` in the task manifest).

Example: a run created via `cd ~/code/my-app && forge new feature "add login" --brief "..." --ticket FG-123` has `projectDir = /Users/you/code/my-app`. Every container spawned for that run mounts that path at `/project`.

## Workspace

The cwd of the human running `forge`. For most runs the workspace equals the project (you're in `~/code/my-app` and the run drives changes to `~/code/my-app`). They diverge when an orchestrator session in one directory drives runs against a different project — e.g. `~/code/audit-workspace` orchestrating runs whose `projectDir` is `~/code/forge`. `forge new` and `forge invoke` stamp the workspace into `metadata.workspace` (default: cwd; override with `--workspace`). `forge status` filters by workspace (matching either `projectDir == cwd` or `metadata.workspace == cwd`) by default; use `--all` to see runs across every project on the host.

## Operator adapters (`/orient`, `/handoff`)

Start-of-session orientation and end-of-session handoff, defined **once** as provider-neutral data in
`src/v2/operator-workflows.ts` (the bounded-CLI rule, the probe set, the report shape, the reconciliation rules,
the notes-block apply discipline) and **rendered** onto each provider surface that has one — never hand-authored
per provider, so a rule added to the definition can't exist on one surface and not the other:

- **Claude Code** (`src/v2/render-claude-commands.ts`) — `/orient` and `/handoff` slash commands, materialized at
  `.claude/commands/orient.md` and `.claude/commands/handoff.md`. Typed explicitly as `/orient` / `/handoff` inside
  a session.
- **Codex CLI** (`src/v2/render-codex-skills.ts`) — the same two workflows as repository-scoped skills at
  `.agents/skills/forge-orient/SKILL.md` and `.agents/skills/forge-handoff/SKILL.md`. Invoked explicitly by name
  (`forge-orient`, `forge-handoff`) or from natural language ("orient me", "hand off", …). **That the file exists
  is not evidence the running Codex build loaded it**: repository-scoped skill discovery is version-coupled, and
  an older build ignores the directory silently. Forge runs no positive activation probe, so every rendered skill
  says activation is **unverified**, never claimed, and names the CLI fallback below.
- **Every other provider** (the generic fallback) — Forge writes **no files**. This is a documented absence, not a
  missing feature: the `forge` CLI (`forge backlog notes show`, `forge backlog list --status active`, …) is the
  interface either way, and it's what the rendered adapters themselves fall back to when activation doesn't happen.

`/orient` — start-of-session orientation. Runs the probe set (`forge backlog notes show` + `forge backlog list
--status active` + `forge backlog list --status done` + git state + `forge projects show <project>` + `forge ops
check`) as one parallel batch, reports a compact state-of-play, ends with exactly one question — "What do we forge
next?" Never re-states the orchestrator role (the CLAUDE.md block already does); performing the start-of-session
protocol IS the demonstration.

`/handoff` — end-of-session ritual. Drafts the backlog notes block in the forward-looking shape
(where-we-left-off / picked-up-next / external state / decisions worth not relitigating / shipped-for-reference),
applies it immediately via a quoted-heredoc `forge backlog notes replace -` (no review pause — the quoted
delimiter is what lets backticks/`$`/quotes in the block land verbatim), and asks before pushing any unpushed
commits rather than pushing automatically.

Both workflows hard-code "use the `forge backlog` CLI, do NOT read backlog files directly." The CLI is the bounded interface that protects orchestrator context cost; projects store notes at `backlog/notes.md` and tickets under `backlog/{stories,epics,ideas,done}/` (in the default markdown storage mode — see **Backlog storage mode** below).

`forge backlog close <id>` marks a ticket done and records a `closed` date. Pass `--commit <sha>` to also stamp the closing commit, tying the ticket to the exact commit that shipped it. In markdown mode that means moving the file to `backlog/done/` and setting `status: done` / `closed:` / `closed_commit:` in its frontmatter; in db mode the same fields are columns on the ticket row and no file moves.

**Ownership is decided from the bytes on disk, never from a record of what forge previously wrote** — projects get
cloned, and forge gets reinstalled at new paths, so an install-time memory would mis-fire on the copy. Every
rendered file opens with an HTML-comment ownership marker (`renderAdapterMarker` / `parseAdapterMarker` /
`isForgeOwnedAdapter` in `operator-workflows.ts`) carrying a release/content **stamp**: a file that carries it is
forge's and is freely refreshed; forge's own pre-FG-253 artifact — an **absolute, dangling** symlink into
`scripts/claude-commands/<name>.md`, on the Claude-command surface only — is recognized by that shape and migrated
to bytes in place. A symlink that still resolves is pointing at real bytes somebody maintains, not forge's deleted
migration source, and a relative link or one found on the Codex surface was never forge's to begin with (forge
never wrote a symlink there); anything else at an adapter path — a regular file with no marker, a foreign symlink —
is a **project override**: reported, never overwritten, never a failure. `forge init`
and `forge upgrade` both install/refresh the whole set (Claude commands and Codex skills alike) from the same two
renderers, so the two commands cannot disagree about what "installed and current" means, and a stale rendering
(the stamp on disk names a different release) is detected the same way — see `forge doctor` and
[Durable launch](#durable-launch).

**Installed bytes are not active bytes.** Nothing forge writes proves a provider loaded it — Claude Code runs no
discovery probe either — so "installed" and "active" are always reported as two separate questions, never
conflated into one.

**Portability convention (per-developer, gitignored):** `.claude/commands/` and each of
`.agents/skills/forge-orient/` / `.agents/skills/forge-handoff/` hold machine-local, per-developer artifacts, so
`forge init` adds them to the project's `.gitignore` **individually** — never the shared `.agents/skills/` parent,
which may hold skills forge knows nothing about. `--no-install-hooks` bypasses installation of all of them. Each
contributor runs `forge init` once after cloning to bootstrap their own copies — same shape as `npm install`
reconstructing `node_modules/`. The `commit-msg` git hook `forge init` installs alongside them is pinned
differently: it symlinks *through* `$FORGE_HOME/current`, so a new commit runs the currently promoted release's
bytes (a check already in flight stays anchored to the bytes it started with) and the installed hook follows a
promotion without re-init — falling back to the dev checkout only when no `current` pointer exists, with an
idempotent re-install that re-points only a provably Forge-owned hook and never clobbers a regular file, a foreign
symlink, or any non-Forge-owned hook. That symlink-through-`current` installer covers only the operator's primary
checkout; a Forge-provisioned task clone gets the same hook through a structurally different installer, covered
under [Private writable Git for mutating agents](#private-writable-git-for-mutating-agents).

## Backlog storage mode

Which store is **authoritative** for one project's tickets: `markdown` (the default — one file per ticket under `backlog/`) or `db` (rows in the host `~/.forge/forge.db`). Introduced by FG-607 as the seam that lets a project migrate off Markdown without every consumer changing: `forge backlog` and every internal caller of the ticket seam (seventeen modules today) share one dual-mode implementation behind unchanged signatures, so the mode alone decides where reads and writes land.

The mode is stored **host-side in the DB, keyed by `project_key`** — never in a worktree's `.forge/config.yml`. That config file is git-tracked and therefore per-branch, so two linked worktrees of one project would otherwise be able to disagree about which store owns the tickets; keyed by `project_key`, they cannot. (The config's `project_key` is a durable cache/seed for identity resolution, not the authority; the DB project registry is.) A project with no `backlog/` directory at all still has a backlog in db mode, which is why `projectHasBacklog` is a resolver call rather than an `existsSync`.

**Authoritative means authoritative.** There is no read-through blending of the two stores and no silent fallback from one to the other — that is exactly the split-truth bug the DB-backed backlog exists to eliminate. A ticket that exists only as Markdown is invisible to a db-mode project, and vice versa; a mutation that fails in db mode is never rescued by a file on disk. Resolution is memoized per (process, project dir) with a few-seconds TTL, so tight `listTickets`/`readTicket` loops pay for it once, and a long-running reader (the dashboard) picks up an operator's mode flip without invalidation plumbing.

Operators see and change the mode through `forge backlog mode [--set markdown|db]`, and every store-touching `forge backlog` verb names the store it read on stderr. Both flips refuse rather than strand tickets, and `--set db` requires a prior `forge backlog import` to seed the `(project_key, prefix)` id sequence — in db mode ids are allocated from that sequence *inside* the write transaction, so concurrent `file` calls in two worktrees cannot collide or reuse an id. See the `forge-backlog` skill (`seeds/skills/forge-backlog/SKILL.md`) for the operator procedure and the full refusal list.

The default has **not** flipped: every project keeps reading its own `backlog/*.md` until an operator opts it in per-project, with `forge backlog migrate`. The additive tables the DB store needs appear machine-wide on the next writable open of `~/.forge/forge.db`, but nothing reads them for a markdown-mode project.

### The cutover (FG-608)

`forge backlog migrate` is the per-project cutover, and it is **one atomic operation** — import → validate the DB shadow equals this checkout's Markdown set → flip the mode. The flip is last, so any failure leaves Markdown authoritative and the mode unchanged; there is no half-migrated project. `--dry-run` reports the plan and writes nothing, not even the import. It refuses over the **target** project's own active runs or running campaign (activity in unrelated projects never blocks it), because the mode is read through a short-lived cache and campaign guards re-resolve the store on every drive step — a mid-run flip would split truth within one run.

The cutover is **one-way, and enforced rather than advised**. There is no Markdown export, so a migrated project's `backlog/*.md` freezes; the first DB-only edit is recorded, and `mode --set markdown` refuses afterwards rather than silently discarding every edit since (`--accept-frozen-markdown-loss` is the explicit acceptance-of-loss override). Two adjacent operator verbs exist because the cutover makes their failure modes reachable: `reidentify --confirm` re-points the registry's repository evidence for a key an operator asserts — needed when the source-dependent evidence key moves (`git remote add origin` on a repo registered without a remote) rather than the project — and `forget-source` releases a permanently-removed checkout so the tickets only it still claimed become prunable. Runbook: `docs/how-to-backlog-db-cutover.md`.

Import stopped being append-only at the same point, because a DB that is authoritative must reflect removals: a ticket, relation, or blocker marker absent from Markdown is deleted — but only when **no live source claims it**. A source is one physical checkout, identified by a random id minted once into that checkout's git admin dir (never git-tracked, moves with the repo, distinct per linked worktree), so a removal in one worktree cannot delete a ticket a sibling worktree still has. It fails closed in three directions: rows no membership has ever claimed are never pruned, an import that observes no live source refuses outright, and a row whose DB content diverged from its import basis is skipped with an `import_conflict` event rather than clobbered (`migrate --force` overwrites, recording before/after evidence).

### Container ticket authority

A task dispatched from a **db-mode** project gives its container a **read-only, project-scoped, backlog-only snapshot database** mounted at a fixed path — `forge backlog list` / `show` inside that container resolve it, never the mounted checkout's (now frozen) Markdown and never the host store. A markdown-mode dispatch publishes no snapshot and its container reads the checkout's files as it always has; what it gets instead is the marker below, saying so. The model is push: the host publishes the snapshot on every authoritative ticket write and fans out to every live container registered for that `project_key`, so an amendment made *after* a container started is visible to its next read. The containing directory is mounted (not the file) because a file bind pins the inode, and publication is a write-temp + rename inside it, which allocates a new one.

Three properties are structural rather than advisory. **Project isolation:** the published file physically contains only that project's tickets, relations and blocker evidence — no runs, tasks, events, gates or verdicts tables, and the full `~/.forge/forge.db` is never mounted. **Read-only:** the `:ro` bind is kernel-enforced, which matters because agents have root in their own container, so the CLI's refusal of every mutating verb is the polite half only. **Authority is asserted by the mount:** an unforgeable marker the host writes before the container starts says what this task's ticket authority is, and a container with no marker refuses to read rather than falling back to `$HOME/.forge` — inside a container that path is a shared named volume belonging to no project, so a fallback would answer plausibly and wrongly from another project's store.

Dispatch records the ticket revision and body hash a task was built from; the live snapshot reports the current one. The two are separate records and neither overwrites the other, so `forge backlog show` in a container names both when the ticket has advanced. A publication that fails after bounded retries marks its target STALE durably, and host `forge backlog` output warns about stale targets — an unpublished amendment is visible as stale, never indistinguishable from an up-to-date container.

**Which directory the identity question is asked of (FG-666).** Authority resolves against Forge-owned durable run state — the run's recorded `projectDir` — never the workspace the task happens to run in. A per-task workspace is a disposable materialization of one run's tree at a recorded base SHA: it answers *mount* questions, and asking it which project it owns is a category error. The two were indistinguishable while dispatch used linked worktrees, which resolve through their parent's git common dir and so derive the same repository evidence; a per-task private clone (`git clone --shared`, the FG-621 substrate) gets its own common dir, and its filesystem-path `origin` does not normalize as a remote at all, so it derives *different* evidence — and the cross-repository guard above then refused every clone-dispatched task's backlog read. That failed silently, because a refused read only degrades the marker the agent sees. The repair is which directory is asked: the guard is untouched, no path under the clones directory is trusted by prefix, and the project directory is carried forward as data. The distinction also matters for reds, which are dispatched with `projectDir` pointing at the publisher's *candidate worktree* — a tree whose `.forge/config.yml` is content the agent under review committed. Resolving identity from it would let an agent commit a `project_key` that makes its own reviewers refuse. Reds still **mount** the candidate; only the identity question moved.

An authority that does not resolve still dispatches — with an `unknown` marker, exactly as before — but it no longer does so quietly: the host prints what went wrong and logs a [`container.backlog_authority_unresolved`](SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md#fg-666-dispatch-authority-degradation-audit-only) event against **both the run and the task**, so the degradation is on the run's record rather than only in the prose of an agent whose reads refuse. The run id is load-bearing, not decoration: every run-scoped operator surface (`forge show <run-id>`, the JSONL/OTLP exports, the sanitized bundle, run metrics) selects on `run_id`, and `forge status --json` derives its event-based task fields only for *failed* tasks — while a ticket-blind task completes successfully. An event carrying the task alone would therefore have reached only `forge show <task-id>`, which you have to already suspect the task to run.

It fires on both paths where a declared `project_key` degrades to an `unknown` authority, told apart by the payload's `stage`: `"resolve"`, where the key could not be honoured at all, and `"publish"`, where the store resolved `db` cleanly and the snapshot publication then failed (a busy DB under a second forge process, a snapshot that would not build, an artifact another task's release had already unlinked). Both end at the same ticket-blind container, so both are said out loud. A marker that cannot be *written* also dispatches `unknown`, but it has its own record, `container.backlog_authority_marker_failed` — there the authority resolved and what failed was asserting it. A project that declares no `project_key` resolves `markdown`, reaches none of this, and says nothing, because a signal on every normal project reproduces the silence it exists to end.

## Operator queue

The durable answer to "what should be worked on next, in what order" — introduced by FG-609 as four verbs: `forge backlog rank <id> [--position <n>|--clear]`, `forge backlog enqueue <id>`, `forge backlog dequeue <id>`, and `forge backlog reorder <id> --to <n>` (or `--order <id,id,…>`). It is **host-side and db-mode only**: all four refuse by name on a markdown-mode or unkeyed project rather than writing into the DB shadow nothing reads there. See the [`forge-backlog` skill](../seeds/skills/forge-backlog/SKILL.md) for the operator procedure and the full refusal list.

It is **three orthogonal facts**, deliberately not collapsed into one. **Rank** (`tickets.priority_rank`) is where a ticket sits in the operator's stack rank. **Queue membership** is whether the operator selected it for execution. **Lifecycle status** stays exactly `active | done | deferred` and is untouched by these verbs. The **executable queue** is the intersection — queued *and* active *and* ranked, in rank order. Collapsing rank and membership is precisely how a "backlog priority" and a "queue position" drift into disagreeing; there is one canonical rank and one membership set, so they cannot.

**Rank is a stack rank, not a score.** Dense contiguous integers 1..N per project across the ranked set, with a full renumber inside one write transaction on every rank-changing verb — not sparse gaps, not fractional keys, whose cheap single-row moves buy a rebalance path that is itself a concurrency hazard, for a set that is a few hundred rows. The consequence to hold onto: **a rank value is not stable across moves**, so nothing durable may key off one, only off the ordering. There is deliberately no `UNIQUE` on the column (SQLite has no deferrable unique index, so an in-transaction renumber would transit duplicate states and abort, and the index would pin the column undroppable). Ties are therefore possible and are made harmless instead of impossible: every ordering read is `rank ASC, ticket_id ASC`, which is *total*, and the next rank-changing verb renumbers the tie away. An unranked ticket is a perfectly valid backlog item, not a deprioritized one.

**Enqueue is refused unless the ticket's current revision is ready.** It reuses [`evaluateReadiness`](#readiness) verbatim — no second readiness vocabulary — and `exploratory` counts as queueable, so a spike enters the queue without acceptance criteria. `evaluateReadiness` is **never** invoked on a ticket write: it is pure over an already-hydrated ticket, so freshness never needs the write path, and binding it there would write assessment rows machine-wide for tickets nobody will ever queue. It runs on exactly two paths — an enqueue attempt, and an explicit recheck. The outcome is persisted **either way**, so a refusal is auditable.

The stored assessment is a **cache plus an audit record and never the authority**: enqueue re-evaluates the current revision and stores *that*, so a refusal reason can never be quoted from stale content and a stale assessment is never a dead end — **enqueue is itself the recheck**. Staleness has one definition: the assessment is bound to the ticket's existing content hash (`ticketContentHash`, reused rather than reinvented — a second canonical definition of "ticket content" is the drift FG-608 spent a reopen eliminating), so any edit to the body, title, type, lifecycle status, created/closed dates or epic makes it read back `STALE`. Frontmatter overflow keys and the import provenance columns are not covered, which is a stated blind spot rather than an accident; the hash's field set is a strict superset of what the evaluator reads, so an assessment can never be stale-but-considered-fresh.

**`blocked` and `in_progress` are computed, never stored** — they are not a second mutable status vocabulary beside the lifecycle one. `in_progress` derives from dispatch evidence whose task has not reached a terminal disposition. A blocked queued ticket **keeps its rank and its membership** and stays in the queue, visibly blocked; resolving the blocker returns it to the identical position. A done or deferred one leaves the executable queue while its rank, membership and queue history all survive, so reactivating it restores the identical order.

**Blocked is two different predicates, deliberately** (FG-609 D7). The *ticket status* one is legacy-only and stays that way: only `import-legacy-blocked` evidence may make a ticket read back as status `blocked`, on the host seam, the container authority, the published snapshot and the dashboard alike, through the single leaf predicate in `src/store/blocked-source.ts`. The *queue* one (`isQueueBlocked`, `src/store/queue.ts`) is wider and reads **all** supported evidence kinds: a queue entry is blocked when the ticket carries legacy status-bearing evidence **or** any enriched dependency / campaign / run-derived row whose stored `queue_projection` is `blocked`. So a dependency blocker shows up in the operator queue, where it is actionable, and never silently reinterprets what a containerized `forge backlog show` reports as the ticket's status. A `queue_projection` of `not_ready` is a readiness hint and does **not** block — readiness is reported on its own. Queue-blocked is *reported, not enforced*: the entry keeps its rank and membership and stays executable-queue-visible. Deferred is the execution-ineligible case.

Every enqueue, dequeue, rank, unrank, reorder and readiness transition appends a row to an **append-only `queue_events`** table, in the *same* transaction as the change it records — so the order and the history cannot diverge, and a crash mid-reorder leaves the previous order intact rather than a partially renumbered one. It is deliberately not `ticket_events`: that table is import-reconstructable and idempotent by a deterministic event key, and collapsing two identical operator actions into one row would destroy exactly the history this one exists to keep. It carries no foreign key to `tickets`, so a removed ticket's queue history stays auditable.

Queue state has **no container consumer** in this slice, and queue writes therefore never mark a project's backlog dirty: a rank nudge does not fan a snapshot republish out to every live container on the host. It also does not appear in `list` or `show` output — it lives behind these four explicit verbs, so upgrading changes nothing for a project that never opted in.

## Queue claims

The durable answer to "who is executing this queue entry, and are they still alive" — FG-610's claim / lease / recovery primitives over the [operator queue](#operator-queue) (`src/store/queue-claims.ts`, table `queue_claims`). Like the queue itself it is **host-side and db-mode only**: every entry point, the reads included, refuses by name on a markdown-mode or unkeyed project, because a recovery caller that reads an *empty* claim set out of an unresolvable project concludes "nothing to recover", which is exactly the fail-open the guard exists to prevent.

This slice is **primitives only**. Nothing here polls, wakes, launches or drives; nothing reads configuration; no CLI verb, API route, dashboard payload or container reaches it. The dispatcher that consumes it is FG-591, and the operator controls it will need (`max_active_runs`, what a release outcome may say, a durable read surface for scans that granted nothing) belong to that ticket rather than to a silent default here.

**A claim is a scheduling reservation, not evidence of execution.** "This owner intends to execute this ticket and holds the right to until its lease lapses" — deliberately not collapsed with dispatch evidence, because both halves of the gap are real: a claim can exist with nothing running (the crash window before the launch), and a launch can still be running under a claim whose lease has lapsed. So a release `outcome` is a fact about the *reservation*, never about the work: releasing writes the claim row and only the claim row, never `tickets.status`, and `blocked` / `in_progress` stay computed and unstored.

**Claim-next is two phases, on purpose.** Phase 1 is an ordinary unlocked read that walks the canonical `(rank ASC, ticket_id ASC)` order and names every exclusion it applies — `unranked`, `not_a_queue_member`, `deferred`, `not_active`, `queue_blocked`, `readiness_ineligible`, `already_claimed`, `capacity`, `incompatible` — so "nothing was eligible" is never the answer. Phase 2 is a short `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction that re-validates and inserts. The caller's compatibility predicate is **pure and I/O-free by contract and is evaluated in phase 1 only**, never while the machine-wide write lock is held.

What phase 2 re-validates is the whole decision, not just the chosen row. The scan's claim is "the first eligible entry in canonical order, compatible with the active claims it was shown", so all three legs are re-checked inside the lock: the candidate's own durable facts (revision, membership, rank, status, blocked, readiness, holder, and the **capacity count, recounted in the transaction** — nothing in SQLite can enforce a `COUNT`, so a ceiling counted outside it over-admits silently); that no strictly better-ranked queue member became eligible while the scan ran unlocked (an un-defer, an enqueue, a reorder, an unblock, a lapsed lease); and that the active-claim set the predicate was shown is unchanged (otherwise two dispatchers both read "no conflicting claim" and serialize their writes into two incompatible grants). Any of the three having moved refuses `lost` — the *scan* is stale, so the caller re-scans — and **a refused claim writes nothing at all**. The predicate itself is still never re-run under the lock: detecting that the active set changed is a store read, and that difference is what keeps the hardening from costing the purity contract. Two exclusions are deliberately exempt from the rank re-check, because the store cannot re-derive them and treating them as intruders would deadlock the queue rather than harden it: an entry the predicate rejected, and one the ceiling turned away.

**The lease is on the store's own clock, and the TTL floor is derived rather than chosen.** Every lease value is written and compared against `storeNowMs` — never a process clock, which would let two forge processes with skewed clocks disagree about expiry. The floor (`MIN_LEASE_TTL_MS`) is three heartbeat intervals plus the whole-database write-lock wait, and it is enforced by refusal: a heartbeat is itself a write queued behind that lock, so a shorter lease converts ordinary lock contention into a self-inflicted takeover of a controller that is still running.

**An expired lease authorizes nothing, and proves nothing about the container.** Expiry proves that heartbeating stopped — not that the process stopped. So every owner-authorized entry point (the pre-work fence, the heartbeat, the pre-launch launch stamp, and the release) carries the store-clock lease term beside its owner+generation fence, and the two takeover paths carry the mirror term. At any instant a claim is therefore either drivable by its owner or takeable by a recoverer, never both and never neither — the recovery surface and the pre-work fence can never contradict each other over one row. That an expired owner cannot *release* is the point rather than a side effect: releasing retires the row, which would delete the recovery record and let the next claimant be admitted with no predecessor and no prior launch identity — a second container for work the first may still be doing. The way back in is a takeover, which bumps the fencing generation and keeps the retired row as history.

**Recovery adopts rather than duplicates, and it is capacity-neutral.** The launch/run identity is made durable *before* the physical launch, so a takeover discovers the prior container directly instead of inferring it from names, argv or timestamps; the successor is handed the retired predecessor (`takenOverFrom`) carrying that identity. A takeover retires and inserts in one transaction, so the slot count does not move — which is what lets a host sitting at its ceiling with crashed controllers still recover, instead of the ceiling becoming a permanent deadlock. Deciding what to do with an adopted launch (attach vs relaunch) is FG-591's policy, not the primitive's.

**Evidence is bound to a decision, not to a poll.** A grant persists the scan that produced it (`scan_evidence`) and appends one `claim` queue event in the same transaction; a scan that grants nothing, and a claim attempt that loses the re-validation, write nothing at all — an idle dispatcher must not become a continuous O(queue depth) writer on the machine-wide store. The accepted cost, stated rather than discovered: an idle scan leaves no durable explanation of why it selected nothing (the per-candidate reasons are returned to the caller on every call; a durable read surface for them is FG-591's). Nothing on this path marks a project's backlog dirty, so a claim never fans a snapshot republish out to every live container on the host. Schema: `docs/SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md` → **queue_claims**.

## Queue dispatch

The durable answer to "who decides what runs next, and why is nothing running" — FG-591's operator surface over the [operator queue](#operator-queue) and the [claim primitives](#queue-claims). `forge queue` is the CLI: the planning verbs `forge queue list`, `forge queue enqueue`, `forge queue dequeue`, `forge queue rank-before`, `forge queue rank-after` and `forge queue reorder`; the execution-authorization group `forge queue dispatcher arm`, `forge queue dispatcher disarm`, `forge queue dispatcher set`, `forge queue dispatcher status` and `forge queue dispatcher run`; and `forge queue cancel`, the one verb here that stops a container. `GET /api/queue` is the dashboard's read of the same durable state, and the four `POST /api/queue/*` routes are its planning writes, each shelling exactly one named `forge queue` verb (FORGE-DEC-015). Like the queue and the claims beneath it, all of this is **host-side and db-mode only** and refuses by name on a markdown-mode or unkeyed project — a board rendered from a write-only shadow reads as "nothing is queued" rather than as "this project has no queue", and those are different answers.

**The queue is a continuously evaluated scheduling policy, not a campaign.** A [campaign](#campaign) is a human-selected, planned, approved and reported *program* of work, with its own items, ordering, lifecycle and report. The queue has none of that and needs none of it: it is a standing preference — this rank, this selected subset — that a dispatcher re-evaluates every time the world moves. A claimed item launches through the **ordinary single-ticket run path** (`forge new <workflow> "<title>" --ticket <id> --project <dir>`, under [durable launch](#durable-launch) ownership) and never implicitly creates a campaign, a campaign plan, or an entry in a running one. FG-370 remains the explicit path for a planned program of work.

**The five views are projections over orthogonal durable fields, not five competing statuses.** Exactly three durable facts are read per ticket — the lifecycle `status` (`active | done | deferred`), the nullable `tickets.priority_rank`, and queue membership — and every column is derived from those plus evidence. **Backlog** is every non-done ticket, ranked or not, with deferred ones visible and execution-ineligible. **Queued** is the selected subset in canonical rank order. **In progress**, **Blocked** and **Done** are DERIVED on every read from run/dispatch evidence, blocker evidence and lifecycle status respectively, and are never manually toggled — nothing in this ticket stores `in_progress` or `blocked`, and no column exists to store them in. A queued ticket that becomes blocked keeps its rank and its membership and returns to the identical position when the blocker clears. There is a **sixth** state, rendered first-class rather than as a gap: `executing_not_queued`, an item that is not a queue member and is executing anyway. That combination is created on purpose (see *Dequeue is not cancel* below), so the board names it.

**Within Queued, a blocker and a scheduling wait are different facts.** A genuine blocker is durable, per-ticket, partly container-visible (`blocker_evidence`, read through `isQueueBlocked`), and *someone has to clear it*. A temporary scheduling incompatibility — "waiting for FG-123 to finish" — is per-evaluation dispatcher scan evidence, evaporates the moment the active set changes, and *nobody has to do anything*. A ready item that cannot safely overlap the current active set therefore **stays Queued with its explanation and is never labelled Blocked**, and nothing on the dispatch path ever writes a `blocker_evidence` row: a scheduling wait written there would silently reinterpret that ticket's status for every agent container on the next snapshot publish, with no code change in the container path.

**The capacity policy, stated.** `max_active_runs` is a hard bound on **queue-owned concurrent runs only**. It is counted as live `queue_claims` rows **inside `claimNextEligible`'s write transaction**, which is the only place in this system where a count actually bounds admission — SQLite cannot enforce a `COUNT`, so a ceiling checked in a dispatcher loop or subtracted from a read-phase headroom is advisory, and over-admits with no constraint violation and no trace. The ceiling is **host-scoped** and its value is host-wide (one `dispatcher_policy` singleton), because the resource being protected is the host — one Docker daemon, one machine's CPU and memory, one shared auth profile and its rate limits, one tmux server, one `~/.forge/forge.db`. A per-project ceiling of 2 across three registered projects permits six concurrent container runs on one laptop, which is the overload a ceiling exists to prevent. Rank, membership, scan domain and dispatcher identity all stay per-project.

Everything else Forge runs — operator-initiated runs (`forge new`, `forge invoke`), campaign items, review loops — carries **no claim row**, is structurally invisible to that count, and is **REPORTED beside the ceiling and never counted against it** (`capacity.notCounted` on the board payload, and the same figure on `forge queue dispatcher status`). The tempting alternative, deriving a count of other live runs and subtracting it as dispatcher-side headroom, is deliberately not done: the subtraction would happen outside the transaction that admits, so it could not bound anything, and presenting an uncounted number as though it did **reads as a guarantee while being a guess** — worse than not counting it at all. If host-total bounding is wanted later it belongs *inside* the claim primitive as a second counted set, never as a dispatcher-side subtraction. Under a host-scoped ceiling a refusal in one project is caused by another, so every capacity surface names the **holding project** rather than reporting an unexplained stall.

**Queue membership is planning intent; authorization is a separate, explicit act.** Enqueuing a ticket says an operator wants it done next. Arming autonomous dispatch (`forge queue dispatcher arm`, which records who and when) is what authorizes unattended, repo-writing containers to start. The two are never collapsed, and arming plus the ceiling are **CLI-only** in this story: the dashboard's four `POST` routes can reach `enqueue`, `dequeue`, `rank-before`, `rank-after` and `reorder` and nothing else, enforced by a closed exported route table and verb set rather than by prose. An unauthenticated localhost `POST` that authorizes unattended execution is a materially larger capability than reordering a list.

**Disable and capacity reduction are admission tests, never reapers.** Disarming withdraws authorization for NEW claims. It does not terminate a container, does not release a claim, and does not stop the dispatcher process — a disarmed dispatcher keeps heartbeating its lease and keeps releasing claims as their work reaches terminal. Stopping the process instead would drop heartbeats, let leases lapse, and let a later takeover launch a duplicate: the exact defect the claim primitive exists to prevent. Lowering `max_active_runs` below current usage behaves identically — it changes no live row and prevents the next claim until usage falls below the limit.

**Dequeue is not cancel.** An operator dequeue, unrank or defer is a PLANNING mutation and never touches a live claim, because releasing a reservation while its container still executes re-admits the ticket to the scan and lets a second dispatcher start work that is already running. Cancellation is a separate, explicitly named act with an order that is not symmetric: `forge queue cancel <id>` stops the work first (through the existing cancel seam), removes its launch record so the tmux-owned wrapper cannot keep running, and only then is the reservation retired **by its own lease owner**. If the launch record cannot be removed, cancellation refuses outright (`stop_failed`) and the reservation is deliberately HELD rather than retired — the container may still be alive, and releasing over live work is the same duplicate-execution defect D4 exists to prevent. The refusal names the launch id; the recovery is `forge launch rm --force <id>` followed by cancelling again. If the owning dispatcher is dead, lease expiry plus takeover is the recovery path, and the successor discovers the prior launch directly through the retired predecessor's recorded identity rather than finding an empty slot. The state this creates on purpose — not a queue member, still executing — is the board's `executing_not_queued` column.

**The dispatcher is a long-lived process outside the interactive model turn.** `forge queue dispatcher run` adopts or creates a fenced durable identity (`dispatcher_leases`, `campaign_controller_leases`' shape, every timestamp on the store's own clock), consumes durable at-least-once wake records, and refills capacity until no compatible candidate remains. It wakes on queue changes, run terminal transitions, blocker and readiness changes, capacity and policy changes, and recovery events; a low-frequency **watchdog** is the lost-signal and dead-controller bound — a health bound, never an estimate of a ticket's duration, and never the only correctness mechanism. A wake is durable and idempotent by construction, so a restart cannot lose one and a duplicate poke is harmless. Physical liveness belongs to tmux ownership (`forge launch run -- forge queue dispatcher run`), never to an interactive session's process tree: the reproduction this ticket exists to close is a promised next action that vanished with the model turn. **Nothing restarts the dispatcher after a host reboot** — a stale or dead lease is surfaced loudly and the operator re-arms; a login agent is a host-install concern.

**Compatibility is a recorded decision, not a semantic guess.** The parallel-compatibility predicate is pure, I/O-free, deterministic and total, and is evaluated in the scan's unlocked READ phase only — running caller code under the machine-wide write lock would stall every forge process on the host. It is *given* the facts a real decision needs (each active claim's repo/worktree lane and branch, its run's touched paths, the candidate's dependency and explicit ordering/exclusivity relations, duplicate-ticket execution, durable resource locks) and refuses on ambiguity: a bypass costs a cycle, a bad overlap costs a corrupted worktree. Bypassing an incompatible or ineligible candidate **leaves its durable rank byte-identical** — the dispatcher never reorders the operator's queue — and the candidate is reconsidered as soon as capacity or compatibility changes.

**"Why did nothing start" is a durable record, not an inference.** Every evaluation pass writes one `dispatcher_evaluations` row *including the passes that granted nothing*, carrying a top-level reason (`granted | disabled | no_capacity | no_eligible_work | lost | incompatible_only`), the capacity scope, limit, usage and holder list as enforced, and the per-candidate scan evidence in queue-claims' own `ScanReason` vocabulary verbatim — never a second vocabulary. That is what lets `forge queue dispatcher status` and the board's dispatcher panel keep the no-run answers **distinguishable** instead of collapsing them into "nothing is running": disarmed, no capacity (naming which project holds the host slots), no eligible work, blocked, temporarily incompatible, and a dead or stale dispatcher are six different facts with six different operator actions. A dead or stale lease **outranks** the last evaluation on every surface — an expired lease means nobody is looking at this queue, and reporting the last pass's reasonable-sounding outcome there is exactly how a correct-looking board hides a dead controller.

**The dispatcher never authors its own eligibility.** A readiness assessment that went stale after an operator edit is an operator-actionable board state, not something the scan silently re-evaluates. Enqueue is itself the recheck (see [Operator queue](#operator-queue)); an autonomous process that re-decided its own admission criteria would be a different trust posture than this ticket asks for.

**Reservation and execution are two records and stay two.** `queue_claims` is authoritative for the RESERVATION, the run/launch record for the EXECUTION; they are never joined in one query to decide something and neither is derived from the other. The visible consequence is the board's `launching` execution state: `ticket_dispatch_evidence` is written when a spawned *task* carries a ticket id, so a queue-launched run only reads as In progress once its FIRST task spawns, and between the claim's launch stamp and that spawn `launching` is what keeps a starting container from rendering as nothing at all.

Decisions D1–D13 — the dashboard-mutation premise, the capacity scope, the cancel path, disable semantics, durable idle evidence, readiness authorship, the lease TTL, reboot behaviour, predicate inputs, reorder concurrency, and the two constraints on the falsification test — are recorded in [FORGE-DEC-033](../learnings/decisions/2026-08-06_queue-dispatch-capacity-and-authorization.md). Schema and payload shapes: `docs/SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md` → **dispatcher tables**, **`GET /api/queue`**, **`POST /api/queue/*`**. Operational walkthrough: `docs/quick-start.md` §14.

## Orchestrator heartbeat

A liveness signal for the Claude Code session that's currently acting as a project's orchestrator. `forge init` installs three Claude Code session hooks (SessionStart / Stop / SessionEnd) into `<project>/.claude/settings.local.json` (per-developer, gitignored — see Operator adapters above for the portability rationale); those hooks invoke `scripts/claude-hooks/orchestrator-heartbeat`, which maintains a JSON file at `~/.forge/orchestrators/<session-id>.json` containing `{sessionId, projectDir, startedAt, lastSeen}`. SessionStart writes it, Stop touches `lastSeen` after every assistant turn, SessionEnd deletes it. A heartbeat whose `lastSeen` is within the last 15 minutes is considered live; older ones are stale (likely a crashed session). `forge projects list` shows live projects with a ● and floats them to the top.

Projects installed before this convention shipped had forge hooks in `<project>/.claude/settings.json` (the committed file). `forge upgrade` migrates those automatically — strips the forge entries from `settings.json` (preserving any other user keys + hooks) and writes a fresh `settings.local.json`.

## Durable launch

A long-running host command whose owner is a tmux server rather than the shell that submitted it (FG-535). `forge launch run [--name <n>] -- <command...>` starts the command in a detached, uniquely named tmux session (`forge-launch-<name>-<suffix>`, `remain-on-exit` on) and returns immediately; the submitting shell — typically a Bash call inside an interactive Claude Code session, which SIGTERMs its own registered background tasks on internal sweeps — can exit without touching the work. Requires `tmux` on the host.

Everything about the launch is persisted under `~/.forge/launches/<id>/`, so a later session can read what happened without having watched it:

- `meta.json` — command argv, tmux session name, launcher identity (`launcherPid`, the forge process that submitted the launch; `ownerPid`, the tmux pane process that actually owns the command and the pid to inspect or attribute a signal to later — `null` if tmux could not report it), start time, cwd, log path, and a `control` field: the **R1 provenance record** (FG-569). R1 is the runtime of the forge CLI that *submitted* the launch — its own `execPath`, `abi` (`process.versions.modules`), `nodeVersion` (`process.version`), and a trusted `releaseId` derived from that CLI's OWN release manifest (a dev CLI running from `bin/forge` records `null`, never a manufactured id). It is captured *inside* the submitting CLI at submission time, independently of R2 (`runtime.json` below) — the two are distinct runtimes and are never merged, since the CLI that submits a launch can run under a different interpreter than the recorder that runs the command. The two surfaces expose R1 differently. `forge launch show <id>` (human text) *always* renders a `control:` line, and a launch written before FG-569 shows `not recorded (launch predates R1 capture)` there rather than a value inferred from R2. `--json` serializes the `LaunchView` verbatim: the `control` object is present with `execPath`/`abi`/`nodeVersion`/`release` when recorded, and for a pre-FG-569 record the optional `control` key is **absent entirely** — the JSON never carries the human "not recorded" fallback string, so a consumer keys off the field's presence.
- `out.log` — the command's combined stdout+stderr.
- `exit` — the command's exit record, `{"code":<n|null>,"signal":<"SIGTERM"|null>}`, written by the wrapper as its *last* act. The wrapper is a node runner, so the record carries the OS's own verdict: `signal` is set only when the kernel actually killed the process (`WIFSIGNALED`), separately from the numeric `code`. An exit file existing is therefore proof the command itself finished, not that the wrapper was torn down.
- `runtime.json` — the launch's **R2 provenance record** (FG-569), written by the exit-recorder (the wrapper) as its *first* act, before it spawns the command. Its three runtime fields — `execPath`, `abi` (`process.versions.modules`), and `nodeVersion` (`process.version`) — are evaluated *inside* the recorder process, never copied from the forge CLI that submitted the launch. That independence is the point: the recorder can run under a different interpreter than the launching CLI, so R2 records what actually ran the command rather than assuming it matched the control runtime. The fourth field, `releaseId`, is the exception by design: it is **not** read from the recorder's ambient `FORGE_RELEASE_ID` (a dev caller could forge that). Instead the submitting forge CLI derives a trusted id from its OWN release manifest (`readReleaseManifest`) and bakes it into the recorder wrapper as a JSON literal — so a real release launch records its true manifest id even under a poisoned ambient env, and a dev launch with no release manifest records `null` regardless of any `FORGE_RELEASE_ID` in the environment. `forge launch show <id>` (and `--json`) surfaces it once written; if the record is absent or malformed it is simply omitted — R2 is never guessed.
- `workload.json` — the launched workload's **R3/R4 provenance record** (FG-555), written by the recorder at spawn time, *before* the spawn (so R3 is recorded even if the spawn itself fails). R1/R2 are forge's own two runtimes; R3/R4 are the *other* side of the launch boundary — the four runtimes together (BD-14) are the whole picture. `forge launch run` is an argv launcher: the recorder spawns `argv[0]` **directly** and never synthesizes a `bash -lc` login shell (a caller may still supply one intentionally), so argv is a *string* and these two records are its honest resolution:
  - **R3** — the launched top-level executable (`argv[0]`) resolved to a real path *as resolved at spawn time*, recorded as honest fact: **captured** (`argv[0]` was already a path), **derived** (a bare name resolved on `PATH`), or **unresolved** (not found — recorded as fact, never guessed). It is resolved *inside the recorder*, in the environment the command actually ran under: tmux interposes its own shell env, so resolving `argv[0]` in the submitting CLI would give a plausible-but-wrong answer.
  - **R4** — how a caller-supplied **nested shell** (`bash -lc <chain>`) later resolves `node`/`npm`/`forge` *inside* the launched command. That happens at runtime against whatever `PATH` the shell builds, so forge declares it **`unknowable`** explicitly. R4 is classified on the *effective* command — forge first skips leading `VAR=VAL` assignments and recognized non-PATH-mutating exec-prefixes (`env`/`nice`/…), so a shell hidden **behind a wrapper** (`env PATH=… bash -lc …`) still records **`unknowable`**, never a false `not_applicable`; a wrapper form forge cannot fully parse also prefers `unknowable` rather than pretend certainty. **`not_applicable`** is reserved for the one provable case — an effective `argv[0]` that is a terminal Node interpreter (basename `node`/`nodejs`), where the interpreter *is* the runtime and R3 fully captures it; nothing resolves a different Node later. Any other direct non-shell command (e.g. `true`) is **not** `not_applicable` — a later shebang/PATH resolution could still select a different Node, so it too records **`unknowable`**. R4 is never implied to be covered by argv.

  `forge launch show <id>` (human text) renders a `workload:` line (R3) and a `nested:` line (R4); a launch that predates FG-555 — or one whose recorder has not run yet — reads `not recorded`, never inferred from argv. `--json` serializes the optional `workload` object on the `LaunchView` when present; a pre-FG-555 record simply omits the key, and a malformed `workload.json` is dropped rather than half-parsed — provenance is never guessed.

**Refuse-before-execute (the control-toolchain contract, pinned-PATH trust).** A **Forge-owned unattended caller** — a verification chain the orchestrator submits, not an operator at a terminal — can require forge's own control toolchain for the launched workload with `forge launch run --require-control-toolchain -- <command...>` (FG-555). The contract **pins the workload's `PATH` to forge's control-runtime node dir (the control node first)** and, before anything is written or any tmux session exists, decides from the *effective* `argv[0]` — computed after skipping leading `VAR=VAL` assignments that do **not** mutate `PATH` and a bounded set of non-PATH-mutating exec-prefixes (`env`/`nice`/`nohup`/`time`/`stdbuf`/`setsid`/`timeout`) — whether to run or refuse. It **allows**: a `node`/`nodejs` (resolved **by name** *or* an explicit path) whose probed ABI (reusing FG-570's `checkAbi`) equals the required ABI — that is the interpreter the recorder spawns directly, so a wrong-ABI one cannot slip through; and a `forge`/`npm`/`npx` **resolved by name anywhere on the pinned `PATH`** — because the pin puts the control node first, a name-resolved control tool runs under it by construction (its `#!/usr/bin/env node` shebang resolves the pinned control node). This is the operator-blessed *pinned-PATH trust*, and it is what makes the primary caller `forge launch run --require-control-toolchain -- forge review-loop …` — and equally `-- npm run …` or `-- node …` — work: a Forge-owned unattended verification is submitted as a **direct** `forge …` / `npm …` / `node …` command, with no shell and no env/PATH wrapper. Everything else is **refused** with one named, actionable message: a leading assignment that mutates `PATH` (`env PATH=…`, which defeats the pin); a **login shell** (`bash -lc`, `zsh --login`, any `-l`/`--login`) that re-sources profile scripts and resets `PATH` *after* the pin; a directly-named interpreter whose ABI differs; a control tool given by **explicit path** (not name-resolved, so `PATH` order does not govern it); and any **other shell** (even non-login — `bash -c`, `sh -c`), script, or unknown wrapper the contract cannot place in the trusted set. The refusal advises resubmitting the verification as a direct `forge …` / `npm …` / `node …` command on the pinned toolchain. This fails a toolchain skew *here* (e.g. Node 23/ABI 131 vs Node 24/ABI 137) instead of surfacing as hundreds of downstream opaque `ERR_DLOPEN_FAILED` false-red test failures. The guard only *probes* the ABI; it never rebuilds or replaces a native dependency. Being **allowed** under the flag and being **recorded** as R4 `unknowable` are independent and both correct: a name-resolved `forge`/`npm` launch is allowed *and* still records R4 `unknowable`, because its shebang resolves the runtime only later. This is the deliberate boundary of the pinned-PATH-trust contract (operator-decided): a name-resolved `npm`/`npx` is trusted to **start** under the pinned control node, but the contract does **not** deep-verify how an `npm run` lifecycle script later resolves `node`. `npm run` prepends the project's `node_modules/.bin` to the lifecycle-script `PATH`, so a project-provided `node` there could resolve a *different* ABI *after* the gate has passed — that later resolution is what R4 records as `unknowable`, not a guarantee. For a strict ABI guarantee, launch `node`/`forge` **directly** rather than through an `npm run` lifecycle script. Absent the flag, `forge launch` behaves exactly as before: it inherits the launcher's environment, runs and records the workload, and makes no toolchain claim.

Status is **derived at read time**, never stored. `forge launch list` and `forge launch show <id>` (both `--json`-able) classify each record:

- **running** — no exit file, tmux session alive, pane still live. With `remain-on-exit` a live session is not by itself proof the wrapper is alive, so a pane tmux cannot classify also reads as running (fail-safe: `rm` keeps refusing it).
- **exited 0** / **exited N** — the command finished on its own.
- **terminated by `<SIGNAL>` (signal sender not recorded — origin unknown)** — the record carries signal evidence (`{"code":null,"signal":"SIGTERM"}`), so the kernel proved a signal landed. *Who* sent it is not recorded anywhere, so forge names the signal and stops there — it never claims the kill was external.
- **exited N (signal-range code, no signal evidence — origin unknown)** — a bare exit code in the signal range (`{"code":143,"signal":null}`) with no signal evidence. A command may deliberately return 143, so this is never upgraded to a kill claim; the code is reported as the code it is.
- **owner gone without an exit record (wrapper killed, or failed before recording — cause and sender not recorded)** — a live session holding a *dead* pane with no exit record. The wrapper writes an exit record even for a signaled child, so this shape is durable evidence that the wrapper never completed its last act — but not that it was killed: a wrapper that died on an I/O failure *writing* the record leaves exactly the same evidence. Cause and sender are both unrecorded, so forge names the shape and asserts neither. Terminal by evidence, so `rm` takes it without `--force`.
- **unknown (no exit record, owner gone)** — no exit record *and* no live session (e.g. a host reboot took the tmux server with it).

The through-line: forge reports only what the record proves. Signal evidence names a signal, a signal-range code names a code, and a missing record names nothing — attribution is left unknown rather than inferred.

`forge launch show` also prints a log tail and any forge `run-`/`task-` ids it can extract from the log, which is how a launched `forge next` hands its run id back to whoever checks on it later. `forge launch rm <id>` removes a finished launch's record and its tmux remains; it refuses a running launch unless `--force` is passed, so removal is never the thing that kills work.

**The completion contract (`forge launch wait`, FG-552).** A controller — an orchestrator or campaign runner — that needs to be notified when a launch reaches a terminal disposition uses `forge launch wait <id> [--json] [--timeout <seconds>]` rather than fixed-estimate polling. It is a **blocking** primitive: it reads the authoritative launch record, installs a filesystem watcher, rereads *immediately* (so a launch that became terminal during subscription is still caught — there is no check-then-subscribe gap), runs a bounded internal reconcile tick, and returns exactly one structured observation once the launch is terminal. It returns *immediately* if the launch is already terminal. It blocks without waking a model, and it does not re-enter the harness's tracked-background set — the waiter owns none of the work.

The observation is **record-first and evidence-honest**, on the same "report only what the record proves" through-line as status classification above. A terminal observation rests on a committed **atomic** exit record (written temp-file-then-rename, so a reader never sees partially-written JSON as a result) *or* on **mandatory reconciliation** for the two no-artifact dispositions — `owner_gone` and `unknown` produce no filesystem artifact, so a watcher alone structurally cannot see them, and the reconcile tick re-reads owner evidence (tmux liveness / dead pane) on a bounded interval. The waiter **never fabricates an exit record.** An empty or unparseable record is a bounded-retry signal, not an *immediate* terminal disposition: while it stays unreadable the launch reads as `running`, and a transient corruption that clears reclassifies from the now-readable record (F11) — so a launch that in fact exited 0 settles to `exited 0` and is never flipped to `owner_gone`/`unknown` on a passing glitch, and next to a *live* owner it stays `running` indefinitely (no terminal is manufactured over a demonstrably-running command). What the waiter will not do is block forever on a record that never becomes readable: once owner evidence is itself terminal (no session, or a dead pane), a record still unreadable past a bounded invalid-record window (`DEFAULT_INVALID_BOUND_MS`) is promoted to the reconciled terminal disposition — `unknown` or `owner_gone` — rather than a `0` it cannot prove. This bounded internal re-read is a plain blocking wait, not a fixed-estimate model poll. The reconciled dispositions and every other terminal state are rendered through the **one canonical status classifier** — there is no second status vocabulary.

The wait path is a **minimal observer** in the sense that matters operationally: its module graph does **not** load the eager command registry or the native `better-sqlite3` binding (the store is reached only by lazy `require`s inside function bodies, never at import time), so it still observes and reports a launch's terminal disposition under an incompatible or absent native runtime. It is not import-free — it uses `node:fs`, `node:path`, `node:child_process` (to shell out to tmux), `node:url`, and project modules — but nothing on that path forces the native binding or the registry to load. The guarantee is native-binding / command-registry isolation, not a literal `node:fs`-plus-tmux import set.

Delivery is advisory and cleanly separated from the work. The waiter's **exit status** is distinct from the launch's own exit code (which is *data* carried in the observation): `0` = it observed and rendered a disposition (including `owner_gone`/`unknown` — a successful *observation* of an indeterminate launch is still exit 0); `1` = unknown launch id (distinct from a *known* launch whose status is `unknown`); `124` = `wait_timeout`; `130` = `wait_cancelled` (a usage error or a malformed id is exit `2`). A `wait_timeout` is an explicit waiter result, never a fabricated launch terminal state. Interrupting `forge launch wait` (SIGINT/SIGTERM) cancels the **waiter only** — it exits `wait_cancelled` and never signals the tmux-owned work, so the launch keeps running (OQ-4). A per-launch `wait.log` line records the observed/timed-out/cancelled wait as a distinct audit trail, separate from any work-level cancellation. Post-reboot, a known launch with no exit record and no live owner reads the safe default `unknown` rather than a fabricated record (OQ-5); refining it to a more specific disposition requires independent durable evidence and belongs to the durable-continuation consumer, not the waiter.

**The continuation consumer (`forge continue`, FG-563).** The waiter delivers; it does not advance. The durable **continuation consumer** is the first wired reader of the completion contract above (and of the FG-562 phase-bound claim): on a completion wake it advances the orchestrator's phase exactly once, driven by the launch actually finishing rather than a guessed wakeup or a fixed-interval poll. `forge continue` is the command the controller invokes on each wake; it carries the **full phase-bound continuation identity** (`--continuation-id`, `--source-launch`, `--phase`, `--next-action`, and `--consumer-kind orchestrator`) — a launch-id-only form (`forge continue <launch-id>`) is rejected before any store touch, because the claim is phase-bound and a launch id alone cannot bind it. On every wake it re-reads the **authoritative** launch record itself (`readLaunch`/`classifyExit`, BD-3) and advances only on a disposition that record supports: a wake hint, a cached status, or a caller-supplied disposition never advances a phase, and a still-running launch — even one that has outrun any duration estimate — re-arms without advancing. It claims the single next-action transition through the FG-562 compare-and-set, so a duplicate wake or a watchdog re-fire produces exactly one claim and one advance; and before spawning physical work it looks the run up by its deterministic dispatch receipt and **adopts** an already-created run rather than duplicating it — which is what lets a controller restart replay every in-flight dispatch (`forge continue --recover`) without spawning a second run, even across the crash window between spawn and recording the run's ids.

`ScheduleWakeup` is demoted to a **fixed, health-bound lost-signal watchdog** — a 30-minute cadence, never sized from a job's duration (BD-9). It is not the normal completion path; its only job is to catch a *lost* wake (a swept session, a Monitor that died silently, a dropped event). When the watchdog — not the normal event — recovers a launch that reached a terminal disposition but was never advanced, it records one durable row in `continuation_lost_signal_recoveries` **before** the advance is observable, and `forge lost-signals` renders that recovery audit — which controller the watchdog recovered which launch for. It is a recovery ledger, not a delivery ledger: only watchdog recoveries get a durable row, so a launch with no recovery row was delivered normally (inferred from the absence of a row, not recorded per launch) and restart-replay recovery is not yet separately recorded — so "was this completion recovered by the watchdog?" is answerable from durable state, and "delivered normally" from the absence of a row, without transcript archaeology. A watchdog fire on a still-running launch records nothing and re-arms; a fire after the normal event already advanced records nothing and takes no action (no false lost-signal claim, F18). That table is distinct from `continuation_stale_observations`, which records a completion event that arrived for a launch the slot had already moved past — a different question. Schema: `docs/SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md` → **continuation_lost_signal_recoveries**.

**Reading the continuation evidence (`forge continuation`, FG-565).** Where `forge lost-signals` renders only the watchdog-recovery audit, `forge continuation show <id>` and `forge continuation list [--state blocked] [--consumer-kind <kind>] [--json]` project the durable continuation evidence itself — from the `continuations` and `continuation_stale_observations` tables **alone**, never joining the filesystem launch record or the runs table (those are a separate source of truth surfaced by `forge launch show` / `forge runs`). Between them they answer, without transcript archaeology, which controller claimed a completion and for which consumer (`claim_owner` + `consumer_kind`), the structured next action selected (`next_action`), the run/task id dispatched (`dispatched_run_id` / `dispatched_task_id`), any stale/duplicate completion recorded-and-ignored (`continuation_stale_observations`), and — for a `blocked` slot — the standing operator action required to unstick it (`forge continuation list --state blocked` flags each with `ACTION-REQUIRED`). It is **read-only**: no schema change and no write path. Schema: `docs/SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md` → **continuations** / **continuation_stale_observations**.

Operational guidance: `docs/quick-start.md` §13.

## Current activity

The answer to one operator question — *"is something happening, or is this stuck?"* — projected from durable state alone (FG-679). The dashboard's **In flight** surface and `forge status` consume the same exported derivation (`src/v2/current-activity.ts`) over the same persisted rows. The dashboard intentionally renders only in-progress host/CI waits from it; `forge status` retains the full diagnostic vocabulary.

It exists because the dashboard used to project Forge **tasks** and nothing else. Two load-bearing kinds of in-flight work are not task rows — host verification running under `forge launch run`, and the required CI checks running at the candidate sha — so a run with either in flight read as though nothing was happening. Observed 2026-08-04: the in-flight surface showed one stale `awaiting_gate` row while `npm run test:worktree` was actively running under a launch, so the card read "waiting on a plan gate" when the truth was "verification running, no gate outstanding".

### One visible dashboard owner

The dashboard has one visible owner for live work: **In flight**. It renders agent tasks from `/api/in-flight`, collapses orchestrators there, and adds compact rows for the two non-task waits below. The detailed durable projection remains available under Activity → **Diagnostics** and in `forge status`, but it does not render a second Agents section.

- **Host verification** — [durable launches](#durable-launch) with a persisted observation that **declared the purpose `host_verification`** (FG-700). A launch is *never* rendered under Agents, however long it runs and whoever submitted it.
- **CI checks** — configured shipping checks at the exact candidate sha, from observations the review-loop's existing CI observer persists. The dashboard label describes the operator question (are PR checks in progress?) rather than exposing Forge's internal “required CI” term.

Those two are the waits. A third section is diagnostic only — it is on `forge status` and under Activity → Diagnostics, and never among Home's compact rows:

- **Launch activity** — every other launch with a persisted observation, whatever purpose it declared. Its full diagnostic row is unchanged; only the heading it sits under is narrower.

### Where a launch appears, and what authorizes that

Launch-to-run association is **explicit structured metadata captured at submission** — `forge launch run --run <run-id> [--task <task-id>] [--ticket <id>] -- <command…>`. There are three placements, by what is actually known:

- **Declared** — the launch named a run, task, or ticket at submission. It appears on its run (and its project), whether that project directory was resolved from the declaration itself or, when the declared run carries no registered project home, from the launch's cwd. Either way it is never rendered `unassociated`.
- **CWD-associated** — the launch declared no run/task/ticket, but its cwd maps to a registered project home, so it may appear at **project level only**, labeled `unassociated` and marked as derived. It is never attributed to a run.
- **No registered project home** — appears in a host-level **Unassociated activity** bucket. This is the common shape for a launch run inside a per-task worktree under `~/.forge/worktrees`, and it is what lets `/status` (which enumerates every launch host-wide) and the dashboard agree without inventing ownership.

`association_kind` (`explicit` / `cwd` / `none`) is a narrower, separate fact recorded alongside placement: it names **which channel resolved the project directory**, not whether the launch is associated with a run. A declared launch whose association resolved the project reads `explicit`; a declared launch whose association resolved nothing and whose cwd supplied the project instead reads `cwd` — it is still Declared above, still on its run, and still not `unassociated`. When a declared association's project and the launch's cwd both resolve to *different* registered projects, the declaration wins (FG-684): forge's own dispatch of `forge launch run --run <id>` from the forge checkout, for a run that lives in a disposable clone, places the launch on the run's project rather than on forge's.

### What a launch IS, and what authorizes that label

**Purpose is a second declared field, and it is the only thing that authorizes the `Host verification` label (FG-700).** Association answers *where* a launch belongs; purpose answers *what it is*, and neither is derived from the other. `forge launch run --purpose <host_verification|agent_invoke|review|campaign|dashboard|generic>` declares it at submission, and an unrecognized value is refused before anything is launched or recorded. Only `host_verification` may render under **Host verification**; every other placed launch renders in full under **Launch activity**, the generic launch-diagnostics section on `forge status` and the dashboard's diagnostic surface. The two sections are disjoint by construction: a placed launch is in exactly one of them, and placement never decides which.

A NULL, empty or unrecognized purpose **fails closed to `generic`** — the weaker claim, always. The column arrived by ALTER, so every row written before FG-700 has a NULL one; those rows are generic launches and never host verification, and nothing is backfilled or inferred from history. A reader whose store predates the column resolves its SELECT list against the store in front of it and degrades to `generic` rather than failing (the dashboard's read-only unmigrated handle is the case this protects).

This exists because the projection had **no** purpose field to consult and used the presence of `--run`/`--task`/`--ticket` as a stand-in for one. Every associated `forge invoke engineer`, `forge invoke test-engineer`, `forge invoke documentation-maintainer`, `forge review start` and campaign launch therefore rendered as a host-verification wait — observed 2026-08-10 — duplicating work the agent-task projection was already showing under Agents. Narrowing the label cost no diagnostics: the rows moved, and none disappeared.

**Ownership is never inferred from a launch's name, its argv, or its log text.** This is not a style preference: FG-492 records that long-lived agent processes carry conversation text in argv and therefore falsely match unrelated role and ticket names — which is exactly how a launch gets attributed to a run it has nothing to do with. `forge launch show` / `list --json` still publish `forgeIds`, the `run-…` / `task-…` ids `extractForgeIds` regexes out of raw log output; that field is retained for compatibility and diagnostics and **authorizes nothing** — it never decides placement or ownership.

### What each launch status means, as printed

The [durable launch](#durable-launch) status vocabulary is projected exactly as `forge launch show` prints it, through the persisted observation rather than flattened into it. `statusLine` (`src/v2/launch.ts`) stays the one human rendering, so the same strings appear on the CLI and the dashboard:

| Rendered exactly as | What it proves |
|---|---|
| `running` | A fresh observation says the launch began and is believed live. |
| `exited 0` / `exited N` | The command finished on its own with that code. |
| `terminated by SIGTERM (signal sender not recorded — origin unknown)` | The kernel proved a signal landed. *Who* sent it is recorded nowhere. |
| `exited 143 (signal-range code, no signal evidence — origin unknown)` | A bare exit code in the signal range, with **no** signal evidence. |
| `owner gone without an exit record (wrapper killed, or failed before recording — cause and sender not recorded)` | A live session holding a dead pane and no exit record — durable evidence the wrapper never completed its last act, but not that it was killed. |
| `unknown (no exit record, owner gone — e.g. host reboot)` | No exit record *and* no live owner. |
| `unobserved since <time>` | The observation is stale or incomplete. See below. |

**Those are four different facts, not four spellings of one.** A SIGTERM-terminated launch, a bare signal-range `exited 143`, `owner gone`, and `unknown` each say something different about what the record proves, and **exit 143 alone is never attribution evidence** — a command may deliberately return 143, so the code is reported as the code it is and never upgraded into a kill claim. Collapsing any of them into a generic `failed` badge is a regression in honesty, and the surface is tested against it (FG-535 / FG-592 / FG-679).

### What the surface does not claim

**A persisted observation is evidence of what was observed and when. It is never a claim about the present**, and the absence of a fresh observation is a fact about the **observer**, not about the work.

So a row whose freshness has lapsed renders `unobserved since <time>` — never fabricated as `running`, and never fabricated as terminal. That is the honest shape and it stays reachable; a launch with no exit record on disk keeps it indefinitely.

The honest limitation that follows: a **hand-run** launch's freshness comes only from **opportunistic promotion**. `promoteLaunchObservations()` reads the exit record the launch wrapper's own recorder already wrote to disk, and it runs at the next `forge next`, `forge gate`, `forge continue` or `forge status` — there is no daemon and no resident observer, because the terminal record is already durable and one would buy nothing. A launch that reached a terminal disposition is promoted at that point, stops appearing as in-flight, and keeps the exact vocabulary above. Between promotions, a gap reads `unobserved since <time>` rather than `running`. Promotion never fabricates: no exit record on disk leaves the row exactly as it was.

### Required CI: no current candidate vs. not observed vs. not running vs. stale

The **section itself** is one of three states (FG-694), and the first two are a distinction the pre-fix surface could not make — it read both as `CI not observed`:

- **`no current CI candidate`** — nothing in scope could be waiting on required checks: no active run, no open review naming this work. There was never anything to observe, so the section renders no CI row at all rather than reporting an observation that was never owed.
- **`CI not observed`** — there IS current work in scope (an active run, or an open review) and no observation exists for it yet. Someone should have looked and hasn't. Only reachable when the first state doesn't apply.
- **`observed`** — at least one current observation, each carrying its own per-observation state, distinguished the same way as before:
  - **`CI not running`** — the newest observation resolved (`success`, `failed`, or `unavailable`) and enumerates no pending contexts. Someone looked, and nothing is in flight.
  - **`stale`** — an observation exists but is older than the freshness cutoff. It is labeled stale and presented as what it is: a past observation, not a current one.
