Read-only by default: it recomputes a fresh evidence view (worktree vs. shared-project-dir source, changed files, a valid `result.json` or a result recoverable from stdout — structured or FG-337-inferred, per [Recovering a result from stdout](#recovering-a-result-from-stdout)) and prints a recommended next command; `--json` is the full structured surface, where `recommendation` is the rendered line and `recommendationCommands` (FG-507) is that same recommendation as an ordered array of the individual commands to run — usually one, two for the cancel→retry sequence below. The `worktree:` line names the **recorded** path, which reconcile's workspace reaper may already have disposed of — it does that only against proof the work reached `projectDir`, so the changed-file probe correctly reports nothing there, and the task's timeline says which happened ([Reaping an orphaned workspace](#reaping-an-orphaned-workspace)). Recover itself is read-only and reconciles nothing on this path, so inspecting never triggers the sweep.

**Recommending against a still-`running` task (FG-507).** Recover is the command an operator reaches for when a task is stuck at `running`, and for that shape it used to recommend a bare `forge retry <id>` — which `forge retry` then refused, since retry only accepts a `failed` task. The guard is right; the recommendation was wrong. Recover already holds the evidence to say something better: when the task is `running` and its container `forge-<task-id>` is confirmed gone, it recommends the two-command sequence `forge cancel <id> && forge retry <id>`. The cancel settles the row to `failed` with `failure_kind: "cancelled"` — retryable per `retry-policy.ts`, so the retry that follows needs no `--force` — and the retry line is the one recover would otherwise have printed on its own. Recover never performs the cancel itself: killing a task stays an explicit operator act. It also never recommends cancelling live work. A `running` task whose container is still alive keeps the single-command recommendation, and because the liveness probe only reports "gone" on a genuine *no such container* (any other `docker inspect` failure is treated as ambiguous → assume alive), a docker outage degrades to that same conservative single-command shape rather than proposing a cancel it can't justify. A run-level `forge recover <run-id>` lists only `failed` tasks, so it never reaches the probe at all and stays docker-free.

- **`--continue`** adopts the orphaned task's persisted work and marks it complete, preferring a valid `result.json`, then a structured stream-recovered result (FG-540), then an FG-337 stdout-inferred result, then the raw diff as a last resort. That last resort is bounded by the same probe reconcile's workspace reaper is bounded by, and in the operator's favour: a workspace still holding an adoptable diff is retained, never reaped, while one the reaper did dispose of was clean with its commits already reachable from `projectDir` — so nothing that was adoptable here has been swept out from under this command ([Reaping an orphaned workspace](#reaping-an-orphaned-workspace)). `--json` names the source it took in `adoptedFrom`: `result_json` | `stream_recovered` | `stdout_inferred` | `diff_adopted`; a `stream_recovered` adoption also emits `task.result_recovered_from_stream` (`payload.source: "recover --continue"`). It's fail-safe: it refuses with no writes when the task isn't in a recoverable orphaned state (`orphaned` / `orphaned_work_may_persist` / `oom_killed`) or when there's nothing to adopt. Its refusals split into two categories. **Force-overridable**: when the only evidence is the ambiguous shared project directory (no dedicated worktree), `--continue` refuses unless `--force` is passed, since the diff there may include unrelated uncommitted changes. **Not force-overridable, ever** (FG-481): when the task's run is a **pipeline** run (any workflow other than `invoke` or `invoke_chain`, FG-486), `--continue` refuses regardless of `failure_kind` and regardless of `--force` — `--force` here overrides only the shared-project-dir refusal above, never this one. `orphaned_needs_finalize` (FG-479) was already excluded from the recoverable set for exactly this reason, since it only ever arises on a pipeline step; FG-481 generalizes the refusal to every pipeline-run task in any continuable kind, because adopting persisted work as complete would recreate the same trust-gate bypass (candidate merge → integration gate → reds → gates → publish never ran) whether the container died with `orphaned_needs_finalize` or with `orphaned` / `orphaned_work_may_persist` / `oom_killed`. The guidance is the same as `orphaned_needs_finalize`'s: `forge retry <id> --force` — a re-drive through the real finalize path, not an override of the `--continue` refusal. Single-step **invoke** runs and **invoke_chain** runs (FG-486) are unaffected and keep today's `--continue` behavior exactly — an `invoke_chain` task's container-gone recovery has no finalize to bypass, so adopting its persisted work is safe the same way an `invoke` task's is. Under the hood this uses `markTaskRecovered`, a compare-and-set that only ever fires from `status = 'failed'` — a distinct transition from the ordinary `markTaskComplete`, which deliberately blocks `failed → complete` to stop a completing container racing a `forge cancel`. An operator's explicit `--continue` is a different, gated decision.
- **`--re-drive`** re-dispatches a failed fanout wave in-run, in one of **two lanes** picked by the wave's durable shape. An **unordered** wave mints one fresh pending primary task in the step's phase (the same shape `dispatchFanoutStep`'s parent lookup already expects), leaving the old parent and children in place as an audit trail, and re-runs the full wave — there is no partial-index resume for an unordered wave. An **ordered** wave (FG-688) is instead reopened *in place*: the same parent row goes `failed` → `pending` keeping its children attached, so the wave adopts every item whose captured commit is already integrated and dispatches only the items that did not complete. Both lanes also return a `failed` **run** to `active`. It refuses if the parent is already `complete` or still `running`, if the parent's failure kind is not in the re-drivable enumeration, if the parent has been superseded, or if the run is `complete` or `abandoned` — see [Adopt-preserving re-drive of an ordered wave](#adopt-preserving-re-drive-of-an-ordered-wave).
- **`--force`** acknowledges an ambiguous shared-project-dir diff (or another refusal) and proceeds anyway.

`forge cancel` is fanout-aware and no longer abandons a run silently (FG-455 p2). Cancelling a fanout parent kills and fails every non-terminal child container too, not just the parent's. And when a cancel would leave a run with no dispatchable work, the run is abandoned only if `--abandon-run` is passed explicitly — without it, the task(s)/container(s) are still killed and marked failed, but the run stays `active` with guidance to either re-run with `--abandon-run` or recover/re-invoke to continue. This applies to both the single-task escalation path and cancelling a run id directly.

**Empty-result backfill (FG-455 p4, Mode A).** A separate recovery path from the `running`-task recovery above, since it acts on tasks already marked `complete`: a detached `forge invoke` whose wrapper process was killed can leave a task `complete` in the DB with an empty result — the structured output was never written back before the wrapper died. Reconcile now runs a second pass, scoped to `status = 'complete'` tasks with no result, that backfills it from the container's own `result.json` (the bind-mounted `/task/result.json`, which may have been written after the DB row was marked complete) or, failing that, from stdout — structured stream recovery first, FG-337 narrative synthesis second, the same precedence and the same guards as the `running`-orphan path ([Recovering a result from stdout](#recovering-a-result-from-stdout)). The no-contrary-exit guard applies here too: a recorded non-zero container exit or an OOM kill refuses stream recovery even though the DB row says `complete` — that row is exactly what this pass distrusts. A structured backfill emits `task.result_recovered_from_stream` with `payload.source: "reconcile_backfill"`. This pass never changes a task's status — it only fills in a missing result — and is idempotent: a task that already carries a result is left untouched, so re-running reconcile is always safe.

### Adopt-preserving re-drive of an ordered wave

An [ordered wave](#ordered-fanout-dependency-aware-build-execution) can reach a **terminal** failure after some of its items have already completed, been captured and been merged into the wave's candidate — `prerequisite_blocked` above all, where one item failed and every transitive dependent was blocked while independent ready work ran to completion. Before FG-688 the only forward verb minted a fresh parent, which by construction could not adopt the old parent's children ([above](#crash-recovery-is-a-resume-not-a-re-drive)), so recovering meant re-running work that was already integrated. `forge recover <parent> --re-drive` now recovers that wave without discarding it.

**The mechanism is the parent row itself.** The ordered lane reopens the parent `failed` → `pending` in place, keeping its children attached, and that is the whole change: nothing downstream needed teaching. `runOrderedWave` already recomputes readiness and adoption from durable state — two task rows and two git refs — at the top of every dependency group, already skips a merge whose commit is an ancestor of the candidate, and already adopts an existing candidate branch; `dispatchFanoutStep` already prefers a pending primary. So the invariant holds by construction: **an item whose exact captured commit is already an ancestor of the candidate it would receive is never re-dispatched, regardless of why the wave stopped** — git ancestry does not record why. This is the same recomputed-from-durable-state adoption the crash resume uses, reached through the operator's door instead of reconcile's.

**Which failures it accepts.** One enumeration (`RE_DRIVABLE_FAILURE_KINDS` in `retry-policy.ts`) is consulted by the mutation guard, by the recommendation `forge recover` prints, and by `forge retry`'s redirect, so the three can never disagree. Accepted: `fanout_wave_orphaned` (the unordered lane, unchanged), `prerequisite_blocked`, `integration_blocked`, and the three mid-wave no-verdict arms `integration_gate_timeout`, `integration_gate_crashed` and `verification_environment_unavailable` — in each case nothing was said about the code, or the captured work is integrated and retained, so re-gating the adopted candidate is the correct forward move. Refused: `plan_dependency_invalid` and `ordered_fanout_unavailable` (a re-drive re-reads the same plan and the same isolation setting and refuses in the same place), `integration_failed` (an end-of-phase verdict on the merged code — a re-drive would adopt everything, re-gate, and fail identically), `cancelled` (a human's decision, authoritatively terminal), and every per-task kind with its own remediation. The map is typed `Record<FailureKind, boolean>`, so adding a failure kind without deciding this question is a compile error, and it **fails closed at runtime**: `undefined`, or a kind string written by a build this one does not know, is refused. That is deliberately the opposite of `retryPolicy`'s permissive default for an unrecognized kind — `retryPolicy` is advisory prose, this authorizes a write — and the two defaults are never shared.

**Supersession is what keeps cross-parent adoption closed.** The re-drive refuses a parent that another parent-less primary in the same phase was created at or after, in any status. Such a primary owns the phase now — `gate request-changes` mints exactly that shape — so the older parent is a dead lineage, and reopening it would put two live parents in one phase and let one wave reach another's children. That is the cross-wave hazard the parent-scoped adoption filter closes on the dispatch side, refused here on the recovery side; reusing the parent row rather than introducing any lineage key is precisely what means the filter itself never had to move. The tie (two primaries stamped in the same millisecond) refuses too, since this gates a mutation. The old "a re-drive is already pending for that phase" check is subsumed by this guard and keeps its `forge next <runId>` advice.

**The run comes back too.** An accepted wave failure usually settles the *run* to `failed` as well — it does so as soon as nothing else is dispatchable — and both `forge next` entry points refuse a non-active run, so a reopened parent on a settled run would never dispatch. Both lanes therefore take the run-level `failed` → `active` transition, and it is bounded at the verb: `failed` reactivates, `active` is a no-op and proceeds, and `complete` or `abandoned` **always** refuses (an abandoned run is a human's `forge cancel`, authoritatively terminal). Reopening the run nulls `completed_at`, so the row afterwards carries no trace that it ever failed; a `run.reactivated` event (`source: "recover --re-drive"`, carrying the prior status, the cleared `completed_at`, the authorizing parent id and its failure kind) is the sole durable record. The earlier `run.failed` event is never amended or deleted — the readable history is `run.failed` → `run.reactivated` → (later) `run.completed` | `run.failed`. The parent reopen, the run reopen and both audit events commit in **one transaction** under the run lock, each write a compare-and-set: if either CAS matches zero rows the whole thing rolls back, and **a refusal writes nothing** — no task row, no run row, no event — on every arm, including a lost race.

**The recommendation can never name a verb the guard rejects.** `forge recover <parent>` used to print `forge recover <id> --re-drive` for every fanout parent regardless of failure kind, so on the `prerequisite_blocked` parent this exists for, following forge's own advice produced a refusal. The recommendation now routes through the same predicate the guard enforces, for *any* failure kind: an eligible parent gets the re-drive line, a refused kind gets the `forge retry <id>` / `forge retry <id> --force` line the retry policy actually accepts, and a superseded parent gets `forge next <runId>` naming the live primary. `--json` carries the answer as a `reDrive` object (`eligible`, a `disposition` code — `eligible` | `superseded` | `run_not_reopenable` | `failure_kind_refused` | `ordered_resumable_in_place` | `wave_in_flight` | `not_failed` — a `why` sentence, and the wave's `ordered` shape). A run-level `forge recover <runId>` lists these parents as recoverable and stays docker-free: the whole disposition is read from durable state, never from a container probe. `forge retry` on an ordered re-drivable parent is refused without `--force` and points here, mirroring the fanout-child refusal, and the `prerequisite_blocked` / `integration_blocked` retry advice names the re-drive rather than a retry.

**No schema change.** `runs.status`, `runs.completed_at` and the `run.reactivated` event type all already existed, so no migration runs and an aged `~/.forge/forge.db` is handled identically to a freshly created one.

### The pre-container crash window

`runContainer` marks the task `running` and logs `task.started` **before** the agent container launches, and the span that follows — image pull, auth staging, dependency provisioning — is minutes long. A crash inside it (FG-533) used to be a permanent wedge: the row is `running` with no `container.started`, and `container.started` is exactly what the container-gone sweep above gates on — as does the read-only candidate SQL behind `forge ops check` and `forge show` — the ready queue never re-admits a `running` phase, and `forge retry` refuses any status but `failed`. Reconcile now sweeps that shape to `failed` with `failure_kind: "pre_container_crash"` — retryable with a plain `forge retry <id>`, no `--force`, because no container ever ran and there is no work to clobber.

The sweep is deliberately narrow, so that every existing reason a `running` task legitimately has no `container.started` keeps its exemption. It fires only when **all** of:

- the task's dispatch provenance is `workflow` (the FG-512 `dispatchSource` marker). `invoke` rows, session/design rows, and legacy marker-less rows are left untouched — `forge invoke` survivability is its own scope (FG-536), addressed by construction rather than by sweep.
- `agentRole` is not `manual` — a runner-minted manual step carries the workflow marker but runs host-side and is never containerized.
- the task has no children. A fanout parent runs `running` with no container of its own for the whole wave; parent recovery belongs to the FG-455 p2 pass and the `awaiting_red` sweep above.
- no `forge-<task-id>` container is alive. This preserves the sub-window where docker did start the container but the process died before the `container.started` append — that work is real, so a later pass sees its exit through the ordinary evidence path instead of false-failing it here.
- no live foreign run-lock holder, of **any** age (stricter than the `awaiting_red` sweep's staleness default). Here the lock is the *only* liveness signal, and the pre-container span can legitimately run long under a lock acquired much earlier (a cold image pull), so a live holder blocks the sweep no matter how old it looks. A dead holder doesn't block it at any age, so a genuine crash is still swept on the next pass — and, as with the `awaiting_red` sweep, the caller's own pid never counts, so `forge next` reconciling under its own lock is free to sweep.

The FG-437 mid-provisioning branch (above) runs first and keeps owning its own sub-window unchanged — a task with `provision_started` and no `provision_succeeded` still lands as `verification_environment_unavailable`. Everything else in the pre-container span lands here. As with the other terminal landings, reconcile clears the task's staged auth and calls `removeWorktreeIfSafe` with `provenMerged=false` — outside `EPHEMERAL` mode a no-op, because that inline call discards nothing it was not handed a proven merge for. Disposing of the workspace is a separate, later decision made from the workspace itself rather than from the landing that produced it: see [Reaping an orphaned workspace](#reaping-an-orphaned-workspace), which runs over the task once it is terminal — including a task this very pass just landed.

### Reaping an orphaned workspace

Every other workspace cleanup in forge fires on the transition it follows — `removeWorktreeIfSafe`, inline after a step's merge-back — which is exactly why workspaces leak: the crash that strands a task is the crash that skips its cleanup, and the merged, now-redundant tree it leaves behind is one nothing ever revisits. Reconcile sweeps that shape by **state** instead (FG-356). On every pass, after the landings above, it examines each terminal (`complete` / `failed`) task of the run whose row records a `worktree_path` that is still present on disk. There is no filesystem scan — the recorded path is the only thing consulted, and a task that never had a workspace is never looked at. Every non-terminal status is deliberately out of scope, because `awaiting_gate` (including a validation hold), `awaiting_red`, `blocked_by_red` and `awaiting_recovery` are all states where the operator is being *asked* to look at the work, and the workspace is what they would look at. A run with no recorded `projectDir` — a legacy row predating FG-374 — is skipped outright: there is no repo to prove capture against, so nothing is disposed of on a guess.

**Disposal requires proof the work was captured. Anything short of proof retains.** A workspace is removed only when all of: it is one of the two substrates forge creates — a linked git worktree or (FG-621) a private `--shared` clone, read off the workspace's own `.git`; it holds no checked-out submodule, since the superproject's status does not recurse into one; its tree is clean, with no uncommitted, untracked **or git-ignored** files — agent output frequently lands on ignored paths (build artifacts, scratch dirs, logs), so a tracked-clean tree holding only ignored output is not proof of capture; every commit its branch (`forge/<runId>/<taskId>`) and its `HEAD` point at is reachable from **Forge-owned state**; and the workspace is **that task's**. Everything else is **retained**, including anything git declines to answer: an unreadable `git status` counts as "there may be work here", never as clean.

Two of those proofs read differently per substrate, and the difference is the whole safety argument:

- **Ownership.** For a *linked worktree* it is the shared git dir the workspace reports being `run.projectDir`'s, plus `refs/heads/forge/<runId>/<taskId>` checked out — and it is proved LAST, because where ownership and capture would both refuse, the capture reasons name the exact commits or files at stake. For a *private clone* the first half INVERTS (a `--shared` clone is a different repository from the parent by construction) and it is proved FIRST. Classification cannot help there: the operator's live source checkout has a `.git` *directory* too, so it classifies as a clone, and every capture check would sanction removing it — it is clean, and its `HEAD` is reachable from its own `HEAD`. What actually keeps it, and every ordinary clone, out of reach is the **alternates proof**: a `--shared` clone's `objects/info/alternates` resolves to the parent's object store, and no repository is ever its own alternate. Layout, branch name and remote are all imitable; that is not. The branch half still applies, so a stale `worktree_path` naming a *sibling* task's clone is refused as well.
- **Reachability.** `run.projectDir`'s `HEAD` is only a proxy — a `remote:<remote>#<branch>` publish target never advances it. The authority is the task's recorded publication receipt: a commit reachable from a SHA the publisher recorded as **published** is captured, whatever `HEAD` says. A clone's capture set is three de-duplicated tips (the parent's branch ref, the clone's `HEAD`, and the clone's own branch tip) rather than two, because a clone is a full repository whose branch can hold commits its `HEAD` is not on.

Only then is the workspace disposed of. A linked worktree goes through `git worktree remove --force --force` (after an unlock, which is what git actually requires for a locked tree); a private clone is a directory removal, because the directory *is* the repository and its private refs go with it — `git worktree remove` has nothing to act on and `git worktree list` never showed it. The branch is then deleted with `git branch -d` — the merged-only form, deliberately a second opinion on the one irreversible step, so a disagreement leaves the ref rather than forcing it.

On the **private-clone** path the ref is disposed of on *the same proof that authorized the reap*. `git branch -d` only knows about `HEAD`, and FG-621 widened the capture proof past it: a clone published to a `remote:` target is proven captured by a recorded receipt while `HEAD` never moves. Left on `-d` alone the tree would be reaped and the `forge/<runId>/<taskId>` ref stranded with nothing to retry it — the ref-stranding FG-356 fixed, reappearing on the new substrate. So `-d` runs first, and only when git declines *and* the surviving tip is reachable from the same Forge-owned state the reap already required is the ref forced. A tip nothing proves captured is never forced.

**A clone is never disposed of while the parent is repacking.** A `gc.pid` in the parent's common git dir is git's own "a gc is running" marker, and a `--shared` clone's alternates point into the very object store being rewritten. That is *transient*, so it produces a **deferral**, not a retention: the workspace is neither reaped nor retained, and the next pass asks again. It is checked immediately before the irreversible step, so a clone that would have been retained for a real reason still reports that reason.

A deferral is **bounded and observable**, because the transience is an assumption a stale lock file breaks. git leaves `gc.pid` behind when a gc is killed, so the file's mere existence is not evidence a repack is running — believing it would turn one abandoned lock into a permanent deferral for every clone in the project, un-reaped *and* (a deferral being no disposition) unreported. Forge therefore asks the same two questions git asks: the lock is ignored once it is older than git's own 12-hour staleness bound, and a lock naming *this* host whose process is gone is ignored outright. A lock naming another host cannot be probed, so it is believed until it expires. A genuine in-progress gc still defers. And a deferral that persists is recorded — a `task.workspace_reap_deferred` event naming the path, the branch and the reason, once per (task, reason), so it is visible in `forge show` without flooding the timeline or breaking the crash lane's fixpoint.

A retained workspace is not silently forgotten either. Reconcile records a durable `task.workspace_retained` event naming the path, the branch, the substrate and the reason — once per (task, reason), since reconcile runs on every lifecycle command and re-logging each pass would flood the timeline. The reasons an operator can encounter:

- **`uncommitted_work`** — uncommitted, untracked or git-ignored files in the tree; `details` lists them (or names the failed `git status` probe).
- **`unmerged_commits`** — a branch/`HEAD` commit that `projectDir`'s `HEAD` cannot reach, or a history git could not resolve at all; `details` names the uncaptured commits.
- **`retained_failure_kind`** — the task failed with a kind whose workspace is the evidence **by design**, and those short-circuit the git probe entirely: the kind states its own retention, so it is never left to be inferred from a probe result. `merge_conflict` (FG-352), `capture_failed` (FG-621 — the clone still holds the agent's work and nothing else does), plus the integration/publication kinds `integration_failed`, `integration_gate_timeout`, `integration_gate_crashed`, `publish_base_churn`, `publication_refused` and `dirty_publish_target` (FG-357/FG-425, the same no-discard contract [the publisher](#integration-publisher) documents for its candidate trees). `details` carries the kind.
- **`submodules_present`** — the workspace has at least one checked-out submodule, so the capture probe cannot see inside it: `git status --porcelain --ignored` at the top level reports nothing for a submodule holding the only copy of an agent's output. Forge's own workspaces never hit this — `git worktree add` records the gitlink but never runs `submodule update --init`, leaving an empty directory git reports as uninitialized — so a workspace that *does* have one is a shape forge did not make, and it is retained whole rather than probed piece by piece. `details` names the submodule paths (or the failed `git submodule status` probe).
- **`private_clone_substrate`** — **no longer produced.** It recorded a private clone the reaper refused to touch because clone reaping was unimplemented (FG-356 shipped it as an explicit, tested no-op rather than a silent fall-through). FG-621 implemented that disposal, so a clone is now proved or retained for a real reason — a clone forge cannot prove it owns retains as `workspace_not_owned`. The value stays in the contract because historical events still carry it.
- **`remote_target_uncaptured`** — the task's recorded publication receipt names a `remote:<remote>#<branch>` target, which never advances `run.projectDir`'s `HEAD`, and nothing else proved the workspace's commits captured. An explicit, named retain rather than a `HEAD`-relative "unmerged" verdict about a branch that was never going to reach `HEAD` — and, deliberately, not a silent forever-retain. `details` names the uncaptured commits and the target.
- **`unknown_substrate`** — a directory identifiable as neither substrate. Never guessed at.
- **`workspace_not_owned`** — the path is one of forge's substrates, but not *this* task's. For a linked worktree: it reports a different repository as its own, or it is checked out on something other than `forge/<runId>/<taskId>`. For a private clone: its `objects/info/alternates` does not resolve to `run.projectDir`'s object store — which is also how the operator's live source checkout, an ordinary (non-shared) clone, and a clone of an unrelated repository all stay out of reach — or it is checked out on another task's branch. A stale or misassigned `worktree_path` naming another clean workspace of the **same** repository would otherwise pass every capture check trivially — that tree is clean and its commits are already reachable — so ownership is read off the workspace itself before anything is removed. `details` names the repository or alternates target it belongs to and/or the branch it is actually on.
- **`removal_failed`** — git refused the removal and the workspace is still on disk. This is decided on the directory, not on git's exit status: a removal git declined whose tree turned out to be gone anyway has nothing left to retain, and naming a path the work is not at would be worse than useless.

A **deferral** records `task.workspace_reap_deferred` — path, branch, substrate and reason (`parent_repacking`), once per (task, reason). It is not a disposition: the workspace is neither reaped nor retained, and a later pass still has to settle it. It exists so a deferral that never resolves is visible rather than indistinguishable from a workspace nothing ever looked at.

A disposal records `task.workspace_reaped` instead, carrying the path, branch, substrate, whether the branch ref actually went (`branchRemoved`), how the tree went (`removal`) and `reason: "work_captured"`. `removal` is `"git_removed"` when `git worktree remove` completed the removal itself, and `"path_vanished"` when git *declined* it and the tree was gone regardless — a race, a partially-completed removal (git unlinks the working tree before its registration, so a failure at the second step leaves the first done), or a tree something else already deleted. That distinction is not cosmetic: git clears a worktree's `$GIT_DIR/worktrees` registration only when its own removal succeeds, so the second case leaves a **stale registration** that breaks later worktree operations on the parent repo. Every disposal path prunes it — `git worktree prune` runs whenever the removal did not cleanly succeed, and since it only ever drops entries whose working tree is already missing, a removal that genuinely failed keeps its registration and pruning never widens what gets disposed of. Both events render on the task's `forge show` timeline, and both render their **values** — the workspace path, the branch and the reason — rather than the generic key-name summary the timeline gives every other event type. These two are the record of where unrecovered work still lives; a line that named only the payload's keys would make that record unreadable at the surface operators actually use.

**A branch deletion that failed is retried, not abandoned.** The branch is deleted *after* the tree is removed, so a transient failure at that step used to strand a `forge/<runId>/<taskId>` ref permanently: the next pass found the workspace absent and stopped there. It no longer does. When the recorded workspace is gone but the task's own deterministic branch still resolves in `projectDir`, the pass retries the deletion and records a `task.workspace_reaped` with `reason: "branch_deletion_retried"`. The retry runs on **the same warrant the first pass did** — `git branch -d` first, and only when git declines *and* the surviving tip is reachable from the Forge-owned state the reap itself required (`HEAD`, or a recorded publication receipt) is the ref forced, exactly as on the clone disposal path above. A merged-only retry would be no fix at all for a clone published to a `remote:` target: receipts have nothing to do with reachability from `HEAD`, so every later pass would refuse the ref again and it would strand as permanently as if nothing retried it. The safety posture is unchanged where it counts — a tip nothing proves captured is never forced, stays exactly where it is, and the no-op records nothing. A task with no branch left is untouched and silent, as before.

**Where the evidence lives, and what is still guaranteed.** Reconcile runs on the ordinary lifecycle commands — `forge next`, a writable `forge status`, `forge show --reconcile` — so an operator triggers this sweep themselves just by driving or inspecting a run, and a workspace they were about to open may legitimately be gone by the time they `cd` into it. What cannot be gone is **unrecovered** work. The reaper's clean-tree check is the same `git status --porcelain` probe `forge recover` builds its evidence view from, run with `--ignored` (the reaper is the stricter of the two on both counts: recover reads an unreadable probe as no changes, the reaper reads it as unsafe, and recover does not count ignored output as work while the reaper does), so any diff `--continue` could have adopted retains the workspace rather than losing it, and a task sitting at `orphaned_work_may_persist` — changed files by definition — cannot be reaped while that work exists only in the workspace. Once you capture it, by committing and merging it into `projectDir`, a later pass reaps the now-redundant tree; that is the intended end state, not a loss. And a workspace that *was* reaped had its commits proven reachable from `projectDir`'s `HEAD` — `projectDir` is where to read them.

So the order of operations that holds: read `forge show <id>` or `forge recover <id>` (both read-only — recover reconciles only under `--re-drive`, and only for a still-`running` fanout parent) *before* running a lifecycle command, and let the timeline answer the question. `task.workspace_retained` means the workspace is still on disk and says why; `task.workspace_reaped` means the work is in `projectDir` and the tree was redundant; neither event means the work went anywhere unrecorded. The task row's `worktree_path` is never cleared by the reap, so a recorded path is a fact about dispatch, not a promise the directory still exists.

## Task retry

`forge retry <task-id>` takes a `failed` task and mints a fresh pending row for a second attempt: a new task id (so a new task dir — never a reuse of the failed attempt's `result.json` or staged auth state), the same phase/role/inputs, and `inputs.previous_failure` carrying the failure kind, the error, and the failed task's id. The failed row itself is kept as-is for audit. Whether the retry is allowed at all is `retry-policy.ts`'s call, per `failure_kind` (see [Orphaned task recovery](#orphaned-task-recovery)); `--force` overrides a non-retryable kind.

The new row is a **primary** task — `parentId` is deliberately left unset. `runNext`'s `dispatchStep` only reuses pending *primary* rows, so a row parented to the failed task would be skipped by the ready queue and a fresh contextless task created in its place, dropping the retry's `previous_failure` input. Lineage is therefore recorded two other ways: `inputs.previous_failure.failedTaskId` on the new row, and the `task.retried` event logged against the failed task (carrying `newTaskId`, `failure_kind`, and whether the retry was forced). Walk either to reconstruct a retry chain; don't expect `parentId` to describe one.

Who then *dispatches* that new row depends on whether the task is a workflow step or ad-hoc — a three-state question, since the answer is not always knowable (FG-507):

- A **workflow-step** task — since FG-512 it carries the runner's own provenance marker `dispatchSource: "workflow"` (below), which classifies it decisively; a pre-FG-512 row instead carried no marker and was recognized by its `phase` matching a declared step id of its run's workflow — is dispatched by the ready queue, so retry prints the `Next: forge next <run-id>` pointer and stops there.
- An **ad-hoc** task is one the workflow runner never scheduled. Two shapes count: a task on a synthetic `invoke` run or an `invoke_chain` run (campaign quick lanes chaining plain invokes), and a task attached to a *real* workflow run via `forge invoke --run <run-id>`. Both are invisible to `computeReadyQueue` — so the old `forge next` pointer stranded the new row forever, waiting on a queue that would never see it. `forge retry` now re-dispatches an ad-hoc row itself, immediately and in-process, with invoke semantics, and prints no `forge next` pointer.

That invisibility is bought by the provenance marker, not by walking step ids. Every `forge invoke` row carries `phase: "task"`, and a workflow may legally declare a step called `task`; a queue that indexed rows by phase alone would read the attached row as that step's pending primary and dispatch it — through the pipeline path, with worktree merge and gates and reds, which is precisely the semantics retry promised the row would *not* get. There is a real window for that: retry inserts the new row `pending` and only then dispatches it directly, and the run lock covers the row mutation, not the container's lifetime. So `dispatchSource: "invoke"` is what keeps an ad-hoc row out of the workflow runner's bookkeeping everywhere it enumerates its own primaries — `computeReadyQueue`, the per-step settle states behind `isRunSettled` (which reads those rows one level up instead, below), `dispatchSingleStep`'s pending-primary reuse, and reconcile's orphaned-duplicate-primary sweep (which would otherwise fail the pending row out from under the retry that is about to dispatch it). A marker-less legacy row in phase `task` on a `task`-declaring workflow cannot be placed on either side of that line, which is exactly why retry refuses it (`legacy_ambiguous_phase`, below) rather than let it reach the queue at all. The ad-hoc test asks **recorded provenance first**: `forge invoke` stamps `taskPackage.dispatchSource: "invoke"` on the row at creation, so a task it created says so itself, with no workflow load and no structural guess. The marker is control-plane only — deliberately not an `inputs` key, so it never renders into what the agent sees. Retry stamps it on the fresh row it mints for an ad-hoc re-dispatch too, so a retried row is never re-decided by inference on the next retry. Since FG-512 the marker is **total for new rows**: the workflow runner (`runNext.ts`) stamps `dispatchSource: "workflow"` on every primary, red (including a shipping-reviewer pre-fail), fanout parent/child (including an empty-upstream parent), and manual-step row it mints; `gate.ts` stamps it on the rows it recovers (`on_reject` recovery and request-changes replacements); and retry stamps it on the workflow-step replacement it re-mints — so a row created after FG-512 always carries either `"invoke"` or `"workflow"`, and `taskDispatchKind` classifies it decisively without ever loading the workflow. Only a **marker-less** row — one minted before the marker existed — falls back to the structural test, which is keyed on the workflow's actual step ids, never on `phase === "task"` alone, since a real workflow may legitimately declare a step called `task`.
- **Unknown** is the third state, and it is what keeps the other two honest. Only a marker-less row can reach it: a recorded `dispatchSource` settles the question before any workflow is read, so a marker-carrying invoke task now retries successfully **even when its run's workflow YAML is missing or unparseable** — its provenance needs no load. For a marker-less row the second ad-hoc shape can only be ruled in or out by reading the run's workflow, and two distinct states leave that unprovable. `taskDispatchKind` (`src/v2/run-kind.ts`) returns `{ kind: "unknown", reason }` for both rather than collapsing either into "workflow step" — that collapse is exactly what minted stranded rows, since retry would create a pending task and point at a `forge next` that never sees it. The two reasons:
  - **`workflow_unloadable`** — the workflow YAML has gone missing or no longer parses, so the step ids that would answer the question are unreadable. Carries the `loadError`.
  - **`legacy_ambiguous_phase`** — the row's phase is `task` (the phase every `forge invoke` row carries) *and* the YAML loads and declares a step with that same id. A legacy `forge invoke --run` row and a genuine step of that workflow are then structurally identical, and only the marker the row predates could have told them apart. For any other phase the workflow owns, the step id settles it decisively. Since FG-512 this fires **only** for such a pre-marker row: a genuine workflow step literally named `task` now carries `dispatchSource: "workflow"` at mint time and retries end to end, so the collision this reason guards is strictly a legacy shape.

  Only pipeline runs can reach either state; an `invoke` or `invoke_chain` run is decisively ad-hoc without a load attempt. `forge retry` is the one caller that interprets `unknown`, and it refuses (below).

The re-dispatch runs through `dispatchInvokeTask` — the same spawn → result-ingestion → run-finalization path `forge invoke` uses — so the two commands cannot drift on the `result.json` contract, the event sequence, or run finalization. As with an attached invoke, a run that already went terminal is brought back to `active` (`run.reactivated`, `source: "retry"`), since a run with a live task is not complete. Only the row mutation is taken under the run lock; the container itself runs outside it, so a retry can't block every concurrent `next`/`gate` on the run for the agent's whole lifetime. The re-dispatched task package gains a **`## Previous attempt (this is a retry)`** section naming the failed task id, its `failure_kind`, and its error — context the pipeline's task-package renderer already surfaced via `inputs`, and which the invoke renderer (which prints only the freeform task text) would otherwise have silently dropped. A plain `forge invoke` never carries the key, so its package is unchanged.

Leaving the container outside the lock is what makes the run *settleable* while an ad-hoc task is still alive, so settledness carries that half of the invariant itself (FG-507). `isRunSettled` answers `false` while the run holds any top-level ad-hoc row that has not reached a terminal status (`complete` or `failed`) — in practice `pending`, minted but not yet dispatched, or `running` — and it answers that before it classifies a single step. A concurrent `forge next` therefore reports the run `active` where it previously reported `complete`, and `gate`'s `finalizeRunIfDone` returns without writing, so neither can finalize a run out from under a container that is still writing its `result.json`. Only settledness widened: `computeReadyQueue` and the per-step settle states still skip ad-hoc rows entirely — admitting one as a step's primary is the pipeline-semantics bug above, not a fix for it — the run lock's scope is unchanged, and reconcile is untouched. A **terminal** ad-hoc row (`complete` or `failed`) blocks nothing; the workflow steps' own states decide the run exactly as they did before.

That check cannot tell a container that is a second from starting from one that will never start, and the honest cost is a **stranded `pending` ad-hoc row holding its run open indefinitely**. Minting one takes a crash inside the window between an ad-hoc row's insert and its direct dispatch — the window `forge retry` opens when it writes the replacement row. No container will ever finish that row and no queue will ever pick it up, but settledness still counts it as live work. `forge cancel <task-id>` fails the row — `failed` is terminal — after which the run settles normally on the next `forge next` or gate decision.

Every fact the re-dispatch needs is recovered from what was **recorded**, never guessed: the FG-350 control-plane receipt in the task's `manifest.json` plus the failed task's own row and events. `projectDir` comes from the receipt, falling back to the run's; the mount mode comes from the receipt, so a red-role row keeps its read-only `/project` mount (an OS-level invariant, not a preference) rather than being silently re-dispatched writable — with no receipt to read, it falls back to the same `red-` role discriminator the runner dispatches on; the runtime comes from `manifest.runtime.name`; the auth profile's *name* comes from the task's `auth.profile_applied` event, since the manifest only records that a profile was requested. The model is resolved fresh at retry time the way invoke resolves it, not copied from the stale prior attempt — with one exception: when `manifest.model.resolvedBy` records that the failed task's model profile came from an explicit `forge invoke --profile <name>`, that profile is replayed into the fresh resolution. An operator's explicit profile pin is a decision about which model/provider/auth runs the work; re-resolving without it would silently retry on whatever the ambient policy selects now. Every other `resolvedBy` is a policy rule (an agent override, an activity default), and those *do* re-evaluate against today's policy.

When that plan cannot be built, retry **refuses** with `AdHocRedispatchUnavailableError` rather than re-dispatching on a guess: a legacy row carrying no `inputs.task` text, a `projectDir` that is unrecorded or no longer exists on this host, or a task that ran under an auth profile whose name was never recorded (re-dispatching unauthenticated would silently change what the agent sees — #176). The refusal is fail-closed and happens **before any write**, so it can never leave behind the stranded pending row it couldn't have dispatched, and it prints a ready-to-run `forge invoke` command so the operator can re-dispatch the work by hand.

Retry refuses a second way, one step earlier, when the discriminator itself comes back `unknown`: `RetryDispatchKindUnknownError`, exit 1. Forge cannot tell whether the task is a step of the run's workflow — which `forge next` would re-dispatch — or a `forge invoke`-attached row, which `forge next` never dispatches and which would strand as a pending row, so it declines to guess in either direction. One error covers both unprovable states and carries the `reason` that produced it; the reason selects the rendered message, never the outcome. Like the refusal above, both happen **before any write**: no pending row, no `forge next` pointer, nothing to clean up, and the message says so. Each names the workflow and then offers both recoveries, since only the operator knows which task this was.

- **`workflow_unloadable`** quotes the workflow's load error verbatim, and its first recovery is to restore the workflow YAML and re-run `forge retry <id>`.
- **`legacy_ambiguous_phase`** — reachable only by a pre-FG-512 marker-less row — explains that the row records no dispatch provenance and that the run's workflow legitimately owns a step of that phase. No local fix makes the row provable, so its first recovery is to re-drive the step from a fresh run of the workflow: `forge next <run-id>` dispatches that run's ready queue, but it will not pick this phase up while its only row is the failed primary.

The second recovery is shared by both: if the task was ad-hoc, re-dispatch it directly. That's a ready-to-run `forge invoke` command when the row recorded `inputs.task` text; when it didn't, the command comes without a `--task` and says so plainly, telling you to supply the original task yourself rather than implying the row still holds it.

## Integration publisher

Worktrees (FG-351/352/353) turn same-file textual races into detectable git conflicts, but they do not catch semantic cross-file breakage: agent A changes a signature in `foo.ts`, agent B (own worktree) still calls the old signature in `bar.ts` — the two branches touch no overlapping lines, so `git merge` succeeds cleanly with broken code merged and no signal. FG-357 closed that gap by building+testing the MERGED tree, not just merging it. FG-425 fixed *where* that build+test runs.

Through FG-357/FG-424 the gate ran against the publish target *after* the merge had already landed on it, so a gate failure could only report a bad merge that was already published — and because forge's run lock is per-`runId` and never per-project, two runs on one project could interleave their merges and gates against a moving HEAD. The **integration publisher** (FG-425, FORGE-DEC-026) inverts that ordering: it validates a candidate commit in a throwaway worktree and publishes only what passed. The publish target now fast-forwards to a fully-validated commit or does not move at all. This is worktree-mode only — which since FG-345 is the [default](#workspace-isolation-worktree-mode); a run with isolation disabled (`FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1`, or a non-darwin host) bind-mounts the shared project dir, does no merge, and runs no gate, exactly as before.

Every publication attempt follows the same shape — single-primary (FG-352) and fan-out (FG-353) both converge on the one publisher. **The lane comes first**, before the candidate exists: it is what holds the base still for the whole of validation.

1. **Record the attempt and enqueue it on the project's FIFO publication lane.** The durable enqueue key is assigned here, at record time, before any contention — FIFO never derives from lock-acquisition order, which the OS grants in no order at all. This record is also the publication intent, durable before anything is touched.
2. **Once the attempt owns the active lane turn:** capture the target's current `baseSha`; create a fresh, per-attempt candidate worktree under `~/.forge/worktrees/publications/<attemptId>-r<rebuild>` (never pooled, never reused — a crashed attempt is abandoned and a retry gets a new one); merge the task branch (single-primary) or the children's integration branch (fan-out) there, producing candidate commit `candidateSha`; and validate that candidate **in that worktree, never against the target** — the integration gate (the project's own `npm run test:unit`), then the reds and any review gate. Publication is conditional on all of it: a red rejection means nothing ever reached the target, which is what makes a red a gate rather than a notification.
3. **Still inside that same lane turn, enter the short publication mutex window:** the final target checks, the compare-and-swap confirming the target is still at `baseSha`, the ancestry proof, and the fast-forward to the **recorded** `candidateSha` — never a branch tip, never current worktree state — followed, for a local target, by synchronizing the checked-out index and working tree. The mutex is released as soon as that window closes; the lane entry is released only once the attempt's disposition is durable.

**Two spans, two jobs.** The **lane** provides *ordering* — it spans candidate integration and the full validation set, so it is held for minutes. The **mutex** provides *mutual exclusion* over the target-mutating operations alone, so it is held for milliseconds. Validation is serialized (by the lane) but is never inside the mutex. Correctness does not rest on the lane: it rests on the short mutex, the CAS against the recorded base, the ancestry proof, and publication being bound to the immutable recorded `candidateSha`. That is why the lane is allowed to be approximate — a wrongly-skipped lane entry costs *fairness*, never a wrong publication.

The lane and the mutex are keyed on canonical project identity (a realpath-canonicalized `projectDir`), so two runs naming one project differently — via a symlink, a trailing slash, a relative path — still serialize against each other. Runs on genuinely different projects never queue behind one another.

If the target moved off `baseSha` before the compare-and-swap, the attempt rebuilds on the new base and re-runs the *whole* validation set (reds included) once — **within the same lane turn**, not by going back to the end of the queue. A second move parks with `publish_base_churn`. Because the FIFO lane means forge-owned attempts cannot move each other's base, repeated churn is evidence that something *outside* forge is writing to the target — find that writer; raising the rebuild bound only buys more rounds of losing the same race.

The target is the project's checked-out branch (`local:<projectDir>#<branch>`); a detached HEAD is refused with a message rather than published to. A remote target (`remote:<remote>#<branch>`, published via an ancestry proof plus an expected-SHA lease push) is implemented and tested but has no production caller yet.

A gate failure still classifies into FG-424's three `failure_kind`s, unchanged in meaning — only the tree the gate ran against moved:

- **`integration_failed`** — an ordinary non-zero exit (the test suite ran and failed on its merits), or any other case that isn't positively identified as a timeout or signal-kill. This is the fail-closed default: a non-signal, non-timeout infra failure (e.g. a corrupted on-disk cache) is indistinguishable from a real test failure here and intentionally still classifies as `integration_failed`, so broken code can never slip through unblocked as a false "infra" excuse. Non-retryable — retrying re-dispatches against the same broken merge; fix the break in code.
- **`integration_gate_timeout`** — the gate run hit its own timeout. Transient by nature, so it's retryable: a fresh attempt may complete.
- **`integration_gate_crashed`** — the gate run was killed by a signal outside the timeout path (e.g. `kill -9`, SIGSEGV). Non-retryable — the candidate worktree's state after an abrupt kill isn't trustworthy to blindly re-run — with advice to inspect the host for a broken or half-updated toolchain/cache, resolve it, then retry.

Known false-negative bound: this heuristic only catches infra failures that surface as a timeout or a signal-kill. An infra failure that exits non-zero through a normal code path (for example, a stale cache that makes the test *runner itself* report failures) is not distinguishable from a real test failure and still classifies as `integration_failed`.

**On all three, the publish target is untouched** — the failure happened in the candidate worktree, so there is no merge to undo and nothing to reset. (Pre-FG-425 guidance to run `git reset --hard HEAD~1` in `run.projectDir` no longer applies: `forge retry`'s advice for `integration_failed` now says to fix the code and explicitly warns against resetting the target.) `integration_gate_timeout` and `integration_gate_crashed` classify as `blockerKind: "infrastructure"` (SHARED, pauses the whole campaign) since a gate timeout or signal-kill reflects host/environment state, not this item's own changes; `integration_failed` is scoped to the item (see below). The candidate worktree and branch are retained for inspection in all three cases — the same no-discard contract as `merge_conflict`. Candidate worktrees are reclaimed only once a publication is durable; a failed or parked attempt keeps its trees as evidence. The default 10-minute gate timeout is overridable via `FORGE_INTEGRATION_GATE_TIMEOUT_MS` (milliseconds). If the project's `package.json` declares no `test:unit` script, the gate is a no-op (a project-config gap, not a merge defect, so it must not block every publication).

Four `failure_kind`s are specific to publication. All four are terminal, and on all four **nothing was published**. Each is classified deliberately, per kind, into a campaign `blockerKind` — three of them SHARED (a property of the shared publish *target*, so it would block every remaining campaign item identically) and one LOCAL:

- **`dirty_publish_target`** → `git_state` (SHARED) — the target has uncommitted tracked changes, or the candidate would collide with an untracked file. The collision test uses git path semantics, so an untracked `foo/bar` blocking a tracked `foo` (and the reverse) is caught too. Refused *before* any mutation: forge never stashes, resets, cleans, moves, or checks out over operator-owned state. It is SHARED despite being cheap to fix — the dirt is in the shared target, so every remaining item would validate a full candidate and be refused at exactly the same place. Commit or stash your changes, then re-run.
- **`publish_base_churn`** → `git_state` (SHARED) — the target moved off the validated base twice (above). Evidence is preserved; look for the external writer, whose writes hit every subsequent item's publication too.
- **`publication_refused`** → `git_state` (SHARED) — the candidate did not descend from the base it was validated against, or its checkout could not be applied and the ref advance was rolled back. The target is byte-for-byte where it started. The target's history moved or was rewritten under a validated candidate, and forge cannot reason about it until a human explains it.
- **`lane_taken_over`** → `scope` (LOCAL) — the attempt's lane lease lapsed and a later attempt claimed the lane. This is the lane *working*, not a fault: nothing was published, the target is untouched, and no other item is impaired. A retry simply enqueues a new attempt with a fresh worktree. Pausing the whole campaign for it would be a self-inflicted stall.

There is also one **non-terminal** publication outcome, and it is deliberately not a failure kind at all: **`recovery_pending`**. The publisher advanced the target ref and then lost the publication window (a deschedule longer than the mutex lease — a suspend, a SIGSTOP, a long stall), and could not take the window back within its bounded lease wait to settle what that means. The candidate **may already be on the target**, so no terminal claim may be made: the task parks in `awaiting_recovery` rather than failing. See [Publication visibility and recovery](#publication-visibility-and-recovery).

Because a red rejection now leaves work **unpublished**, `forge gate <task> advance --force` over a red no longer just marks the task complete — it re-enters dispatch and runs the publication step (no agent re-dispatch, no reds re-collected). The integration gate still runs on that re-entry: a human overriding a red is not a human overriding a broken build, and did not ask to.

### Publication visibility and recovery

The durable per-attempt record — `{baseSha, candidateSha, publishedSha, target}`, plus state, park reason, rebuild count, and candidate worktree — is operator-visible:

```bash
forge publish attempts                  # every attempt, with the four SHAs and the target
forge publish attempts -p ~/code/my-app # scoped to one project (any spelling of the path)
forge publish lane                      # the FIFO lane: who holds the publication window, who is queued
forge publish lane -p ~/code/my-app -a  # one project, including terminal entries
forge publish recover <attemptId>       # converge an attempt interrupted mid-publication
```

`forge publish lane` is the answer to "is this run hung, or is it just queued behind another one?" — it names the mutex holder and renders the queue in durable enqueue order (the enqueue key is assigned when the request is *recorded*, before any contention; FIFO never derives from lock-acquisition order, which the OS does not grant in any order).

An attempt interrupted inside the short publication window — between advancing the target's ref and updating its checked-out working tree — has a defined recovery: it is re-derived from `{baseSha, candidateSha, currentTargetSha}` alone, never from working-tree contents. Recovery runs automatically at the top of every `forge next` wave, so it needs no operator action; `forge publish recover <attemptId>` runs the same idempotent convergence by hand. If the ref carries the candidate but the working tree could not be brought up to it (usually an untracked file in the way), the command says so explicitly and exits non-zero: the publication is durable, but your checkout is not yet at it.

Note that an interruption mid-publish does **not** mean nothing was published. If the candidate already won the CAS, the publication is durable and recovery *completes* it (finishing the checkout) — it never rolls the target back. Do not hand-roll a `git reset` on a target you found mid-recovery; run `forge publish recover` and read what it tells you.

Only that convergence may settle such an attempt. A **lane** lease lapsing means the attempt's *owner* is gone; it does not mean its *ref advance* is gone, so the lane sweep abandons the lane entry (losing a turn costs fairness) but never the attempt record. Recovery is also idempotent with respect to attempts that already finished: a settled attempt is **reported** as recorded, never re-derived from the current ref — re-deriving one after a later publication landed on top of it would rewrite durable history, turning a `published` attempt into a churn park claiming nothing of it ever landed.

**`awaiting_recovery` — an unsettled publication (FORGE-DEC-027).** The same window can be lost without a crash: any deschedule longer than the mutex lease (laptop suspend, SIGSTOP, container pause, a long IO/GC stall) can leave a publisher having advanced the ref but no longer owning the window. From that moment it mutates **nothing** further — not the ref, not the index, not the working tree, not even a rollback of its own write, because a thief may already have synchronized the tree onto the candidate. It tries to take the window back for a bounded lease wait and converge itself; if it succeeds, it reports the disposition the ref proves and you never see this state.

If the window does not come free in that time, the attempt stays `publishing`, the publisher returns `recovery_pending`, and its task parks in **`awaiting_recovery`**. What that status means for you as an operator:

- **The task's work may ALREADY be on the target.** Do not retry it — a retry would publish it twice. `forge retry` and `forge gate` both refuse the status by name and say so; `forge show`, `forge status` (`⧗`), and the advisor all lead with the same warning and name the attempt.
- **The fix is forge's own, not yours.** Run `forge next <run-id>`: every wave converges unfinished publications from the ref (AD-5) and then reconciles the owning task onto whatever that convergence recorded — completing it through the gate its step declares if the candidate landed, failing it with the *converged* failure kind if it provably did not. `forge publish recover <attemptId>` runs **both halves** by hand — it converges the attempt and then runs that same reconciliation, reporting the task's resulting status — so it clears `awaiting_recovery` on its own and needs no `forge next` behind it. When that reconciliation *refuses* a task because a human already decided it, the command says so by name — `was NOT reconciled onto it and stays failed`, with the reason — rather than printing a status under "reconciled onto it" for a task nothing reconciled (FG-676).
- **A terminal refusal never stands over an attempt still `publishing`.** That reconciliation also repairs the inverse contradiction — a task recorded `failed` beside a `published` attempt, which a crash, or a database written by a build predating FG-425's AC5 fix, can leave behind. It repairs *damage*, though, never a *decision*, and the two authorities divide cleanly: **the publication record is authoritative for whether the candidate reached the target; the task row is authoritative for what disposition a human chose.** So a sweep walks a task onto its publication only where no human has already spoken for it. A `forge cancel`, a gate `reject` and a gate `request-changes` are decisions, not damage: the row stays terminal, keeps its error and its rejection rationale, and reconcile leaves it there however the attempt settled — the cancel carve-out that records `task.published_after_cancel` is this same rule seen from the cancel side, not a second one. A published-then-rejected candidate gets no announcement event of its own: `forge show` renders the task's publication attempts already, and that is where you see the target carrying work from a task the gate turned down. The no-evidence default is unchanged and load-bearing: a row `failed` with nothing on its timeline recording a human's decision is still reconciled onto the truth the attempt proves — that is the crash-stranded case, and it is the common one. (FG-676.)

**A row already resurrected does not clear itself (FG-676).** Before that guard existed, a reconcile sweep could flip a gate-rejected task back to `awaiting_gate` and NULL its error, leaving a phantom blocker: no agent will ever run for it, no gate decision resolves it, and the row contradicts its own event stream on adjacent lines of `forge show`. The corruption is **forward-only**. `awaiting_gate` is in neither of reconcile's selection sets — `awaiting_recovery`, or `failed` beside a `published` attempt — so no later wave revisits such a row, and shipping the guard does not clear one already written. `forge ops check` reports it as a `resurrected_gate_decision` incident naming the task, and `forge ops repair <task-id>` restores the terminal row from the event stream, which is the authoritative copy: the original failure kind and error text, the rejection rationale, and the task's `result` carried through unchanged, plus one `task.reconciled` audit event. Run `--dry-run` first — it writes nothing and says what it would do. The repair is **operator-invoked by design, and nothing self-heals it**: it rewrites a durable audit row, so a sweep doing that autonomously would fire on whoever drives a run next and silently overwrite rows an operator may be holding as evidence. Where the events do not prove the shape — the row is not `awaiting_gate`, there is no gate rejection on the timeline, or a later `task.completed` means the task recovered — it refuses by name rather than guessing.

Because recovery re-runs the checkout, it is a write to the target's working tree — so `forge publish recover` takes the same short publication mutex a publish does. If another attempt is inside its window, or the named attempt is itself still live (its lane lease has not lapsed), the command **refuses**, names the holder, and exits non-zero rather than running a second checkout in the same tree or marking a live attempt abandoned. Wait for the holder and re-run; the command is idempotent. An *expired* holder is taken over as normal — an expiring lease is a durable timestamp lapsing, never a liveness probe (AD-7).

The publisher's lifecycle is also on the run timeline (`forge show`, dashboard): `publication.requested`, `publication.merge_failed`, `publication.validation_failed`, `publication.base_moved`, `publication.published`, `publication.refused`, `publication.parked`, `publication.recovered`, and the run-level `integration.published`, whose payload carries the recorded `{target, baseSha, candidateSha, publishedSha}`. Three more cover the unsettled-publication path above: `publication.window_lost` (the ref advance landed and the window was then lost — recorded *instead of* `publication.refused`, which would claim the opposite of what happened), `publication.recovery_pending` (the window did not come free within the convergence bound), and `publication.already_published` (the publisher was handed a task whose work is already on the target, and republished nothing). The task side records `task.awaiting_recovery` and `task.publication_reconciled`.

## Workspace isolation (worktree mode)

**Isolation is the default (FG-345, 2026-07-28), not an opt-in.** A workflow-dispatched task runs in its own workspace — a private clone for a mutating agent, a linked worktree for a non-mutating one — rather than sharing the operator's checkout through a bind mount. ("Workflow-dispatched" is load-bearing, not a hedge: [Which dispatches provision one](#which-dispatches-provision-one) below is the scope, and `forge invoke` sits outside it.) `isWorktreeModeEnabled()` (`src/v2/worktree-lifecycle.ts`) resolves it in this order:

1. **`FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` → off.** The legacy escape hatch, and the highest precedence. It outranks an explicit `FORGE_WORKTREES=1`, at dispatch and not merely in the predicate.
2. **`FORGE_WORKTREES` set → honored explicitly.** `"1"` is on; any other value, `"0"` included, is off.
3. **Neither set → the platform default: on for darwin, off elsewhere.**

Rule 3 is platform-aware rather than a bare "on" because the preflight gate below **hard-fails on Linux, permanently by design**: forge's orchestrator runs on a macOS host, and "Linux" in these gates can only mean the agent container. A bare "on" would arm isolation on a Linux host and then throw on every dispatch, so a non-darwin host keeps the shared bind-mount path exactly as it behaved before. An explicit `FORGE_WORKTREES=1` still force-enables everywhere — and on Linux still hits that gate loudly, which is the intended behavior.

### Which dispatches provision one

**Isolation is a property of WORKFLOW dispatch** — `forge new` / `forge next`, primary steps and fan-out children alike (`runNext.ts`). Those tasks get a workspace, a recorded `worktree_path` and `base_sha`, and a Forge-owned publication path back to the project.

**`forge invoke` provisions no workspace, and default-on did not change that.** A single-agent dispatch mounts the resolved `--project` directory itself and records no workspace path or base SHA. `forge review-loop` inherits this for **both** of its dispatches — its red reviewer (read-only mount) and its fixer (read-write) run against the operator's checkout, which is also where the loop stages and commits the fixer's work host-side. So on a darwin host with no switch set, those agents are on the shared checkout while a workflow-dispatched task beside them is isolated.

That asymmetry is a **scope boundary, not a decided exclusion**: the invoke surface has no candidate/publication step to merge a workspace back through, and review-loop reads its fixer's output from the directory it mounts, so isolating it needs a capture path built first. Read every claim below — the workspace contract, the preflight gates, the substrate split — as describing workflow-dispatched tasks. Neither isolation switch changes what an invoke mounts: `FORGE_WORKTREES=1` provisions nothing there, and `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` disables nothing. The one thing `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` still does on this path is acknowledge the self-host refusal below.

### Self-host dispatch on a never-isolated path

FG-612's dispatch guard (`assertSelfHostDispatchAllowed`, `src/v2/self-host-guard.ts`) refuses a dispatch whose project directory overlaps the source root of the forge that is executing — in either direction — unless **that dispatch** provisions an isolated workspace. The hazard it names is specific: forge runs `src/` in-process (FG-569), so an agent's half-written file is immediately live for every forge process on the host, in every project.

**The guard asks what this dispatch does, not what the host default is.** The caller declares `isolated`, `not-armed`, or `never-isolated`; the guard never infers it from `isWorktreeModeEnabled()`. That distinction is what default-on created — before FG-345 "isolation is the default here" and "this dispatch is isolated" were the same question, and reading the first as the second let a self-host `forge invoke` through unrefused on a darwin host. Workflow dispatch passes worktree mode through and behaves exactly as before; the invoke path declares `never-isolated` and refuses.

So a self-host `forge invoke <role>` — and a self-host `forge review-loop`, whose red reviewer and fixer both dispatch through it — now **refuses before the container starts and before the task row is written**. It is not a verdict on the code, and no reviewer or fixer runs.

**Arming worktree isolation is not a way through this refusal, and the message deliberately never names `FORGE_WORKTREES`.** Setting it would not change what an invoke mounts, so an operator who followed that advice would land back on the live checkout believing they had fixed it. The refusal is structural. The two ways through:

| Way through | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| Dispatch against a disposable **clone** of the forge checkout | The fix. The mount no longer overlaps the executing forge's source root, so the guard does not fire and agent writes cannot reach the running tree. |
| `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` | The explicit, acknowledged override: proceed on the shared checkout anyway. Warns once per project on stderr and accepts the hazard; it isolates nothing. |

The `never-isolated` refusal is scoped to **self-host** dispatch. An ordinary project's `forge invoke` is untouched and still runs against its checkout — that surface is outside the isolation guarantee, but it is not the tree forge is executing from. The operator procedure for the meta case is in [Running forge on the forge repo itself](how-to-use-forge-across-projects.md#running-forge-on-the-forge-repo-itself).

### The workspace contract

**A task workspace is committed tracked content at the recorded base SHA, plus inputs explicitly supplied through Forge's own provisioning or environment mechanisms** — the dependency cache, the runtime's declared mounts and env, the task package. **Ambient local checkout state — uncommitted, untracked, ignored — is intentionally NOT inherited. That is the contract, not a gap in it.** A workflow that needs a local file supplies it through a Forge mechanism rather than depending on whatever happens to be lying in a working tree; `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` is the supported escape for anything the contract does not fit.

**The boundary is only partly visible, and the invisible half is the one that bites.** The workspace constructors (`createWorktree`, `createTaskClone`) collect what they did not carry with `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`, and dispatch prints the provisioned workspace's list on stderr — `[forge/worktree] task <id>: N untracked host file(s) not in workspace: …`, naming the first five. `--exclude-standard` deliberately omits **ignored** files, so the diagnostic covers untracked-but-not-ignored paths only: `.env`, local config and build caches are never listed. That leaves the highest-value case silent — `.env` and files like it are canonically gitignored, so a dispatch whose only missing input is an ignored file emits **no diagnostic at all**. Collecting ignored files is out of scope by decision, not oversight (an unbounded `--ignored` would enumerate all of `node_modules`); when an ignored local input is genuinely required, `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` is the operator's answer.

### Preflight gates

The gates are **unchanged** by default-on — but more operators now meet them, because more dispatches take this path. They run before any state is mutated, and `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES` does not bypass them (it works one level up, so the gate is never reached at all):

1. **Platform** — a Linux *host* is refused, permanently.
2. **Git** — the project directory must be inside a git repository. A non-git project hard-fails.
3. **Dirty tracked tree** — the tracked tree must be clean, because the agent sees a committed snapshot and a dirty tree makes the workspace stale by construction. `FORGE_WORKTREE_IGNORE_DIRTY=1` is the documented bypass.

Each refusal names `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` as the way back to the shared bind-mount.

### Operator environment variables

| Variable | Effect | When you'd reach for it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` | Disables isolation entirely; dispatch bind-mounts the shared project directory and records no workspace path or base SHA. **Highest precedence** — it wins over an explicit `FORGE_WORKTREES=1`. | The headline switch now that isolation is default-on: the supported way to get the legacy shared-checkout behavior for a workflow the workspace contract does not fit. |
| `FORGE_WORKTREES` | Explicit on (`1`) / off (any other value). Overrides the platform default, and is overridden by the kill switch. | Force isolation on a non-darwin host (it will then hit the Linux gate), or pin an explicit off without reaching for the kill switch — which is how forge's own test preload (`src/test-setup.ts`) makes the suite host-independent. |
| `FORGE_WORKTREE_IGNORE_DIRTY=1` | Bypasses gate 3 only. The tracked tree may be dirty; the workspace is still built from the committed base, so those changes do not reach the agent. | Dispatching from a checkout you deliberately keep dirty, having accepted that the agent works from the committed snapshot. |
| `FORGE_WORKTREES_EPHEMERAL=1` | Affects **cleanup only**: a workspace may be removed even without proof of capture, and its branch force-deleted. | Test and throwaway runs, where no output is at risk. Never on a run whose output matters — it is the one warrant that disposes of an unmerged workspace. |

These are host-side orchestrator switches, not agent configuration; forge withholds its whole `FORGE_*` namespace from host-verification children ([Host verification readiness](#host-verification-readiness)).

### Which substrate a task gets

The substrate follows capability, and default-on does not change the split: a **mutating** agent gets a private `git clone --shared` ([Private writable Git for mutating agents](#private-writable-git-for-mutating-agents)); a **non-mutating** agent — a reviewer, a red — gets a read-only linked worktree ([Worktree git access](#worktree-git-access)). Disposal of either is covered under [Reaping an orphaned workspace](#reaping-an-orphaned-workspace).

## Agent worktree dependency parity

Only on macOS hosts, and only when a task dispatches into a git worktree (worktree mode — FG-345/351), forge upgrades the container-local `node_modules` shadow (`#245`, darwin-only, exists to keep every dependency write inside the container instead of round-tripping through grpcfuse) from a single anonymous volume to one **named, lockfile-keyed** volume per npm workspace member — the repo root plus every literal entry in `package.json`'s `workspaces` array (glob patterns aren't expanded in this first cut). Volumes are named `forge-deps-<lockfileHash>-<member>`, where `lockfileHash` hashes the repo-root `package-lock.json`: an unchanged lockfile lets a later dispatch against the same commit (e.g. the Shipping Reviewer verifying what an engineer just built, FG-372) reuse an already-installed volume instead of paying for a fresh install, and an edited lockfile invalidates the cache automatically by changing the volume name.

The container entrypoint (`docker/agent-entrypoint.sh`) runs `npm ci` (lockfile present) or `npm install` (no lockfile) from the repo root before exec'ing the agent command, so workspace links and `@forge/*` aliases resolve the same way they do on the host. Only the dispatch that first populates an empty cache key installs; a concurrent dispatch for the same key blocks on a host-side lock under `~/.forge/dependency-cache/` until the first dispatch either marks the key ready or releases the lock on a failed install. Read-only dispatches (reviewers and reds, including the Shipping Reviewer) never install — the reviewer *container* never writes. Since FG-664 the **host** decides that lane's dependency environment before any agent container starts: on an eligible configuration it provisions a cold key through the same provisioner under the same lock, attests the result with a Forge-owned probe, and **refuses the dispatch** when it cannot — where it previously proceeded with no dependency mount at all.
