FG-678 widened that host-side decision to every dispatch shape that mounts a project — the writable `forge invoke` lane and every read-write workflow dispatch, worktree mode included. See [The dependency-environment gate](#the-dependency-environment-gate-fg-664-fg-678) below.

A failed install exits the container with a dedicated sentinel exit code (123) before the agent command ever runs. Forge classifies that as `failure_kind: "verification_environment_unavailable"` — a new terminal kind alongside `container_crash`/`model_error` (see [Container crash vs. agent failure](#container-crash-vs-agent-failure)) — so a broken or stale dependency graph is reported as an environment failure, not misread as a test failure or a generic crash. Exit code **122** is its peer, raised by the same entrypoint for an unusable git (see [Worktree git access](#worktree-git-access)).

Provisioning itself runs in a separate, short-lived `forge-provision-<cacheKey>` container that starts before the task's own container, and it durably logs `container.provision_started` / `container.provision_succeeded` events (carrying that container's name and cache key) so a host crash mid-install is still visible to reconcile after the worktree is gone. See [Orphaned task recovery](#orphaned-task-recovery) for how reconcile detects and recovers that crash.

Cleanup is deliberately **not** wired to individual worktree disposal: a cache-key volume is shared by every task that sees the same lockfile hash, so tearing it down when one task's worktree is removed would break other tasks still referencing it. `forge-deps-*` docker volumes therefore accumulate on disk across runs; `forge dependency-cache prune` (FG-434) reclaims them — it removes every `forge-deps-*` volume plus its `~/.forge/dependency-cache/` `.ready`/`.lock` markers, skipping (not erroring on) any volume docker reports as still in use by a container, and only clearing a marker/lock for a cache key whose volume was actually removed. `--dry-run` reports candidates without removing anything; `--json` emits a structured `{volumesRemoved, volumesSkippedInUse, volumesError, markersRemoved, dryRun}` summary. A pruned volume simply re-provisions (a fresh install) the next time a task needs that lockfile hash.

**Since FG-678 nothing is scoped to the worktree shape.** A non-worktree **read-write** dispatch — a writable `forge invoke`, or a workflow primary under `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` or on a tree that is not mountable as a workspace — used to be the one shape left out: it mounted the original single *anonymous* `node_modules` shadow volume, with no named cache, no lockfile hashing and no automatic install. That volume starts empty and masks whatever the bind mount has at the same path, so a host-side `npm ci` in the operator's own checkout could not reach the container, and whether an agent recovered by self-installing was decided inside the container rather than by the platform. Those dispatches now resolve host-side like every other shape and mount the same named per-member volumes **read-only**; the anonymous shadow survives only as the fallback for a read-write dispatch that resolved no environment (a not-applicable configuration). A read-only reviewer or red is unchanged — it mounts the named per-member volumes read-only whenever the cache key is ready, worktree mode or not. The whole mechanism is darwin-only (and switched off entirely by `FORGE_NO_NM_SHADOW=1`): on a non-macOS host no `node_modules` volume of either kind is mounted, since there is no grpcfuse round-trip to avoid.

**Every dependency volume binds at a path *inside* a read-only project mount, so the mountpoint directory must already exist in the source tree** — docker cannot `mkdir` a mountpoint on a read-only rootfs, and a missing `<member>/node_modules` fails the container at creation with `create mountpoint for /project/<member>/node_modules mount: ... read-only file system`. FG-627 created those directories at the isolated-workspace constructors (linked worktree, private clone), which covers two of the trees that can end up bound at the container's project path and misses the rest: a plain `--project <dir>` dispatch against an ordinary checkout that only ever ran a root-only install, and — the one that actually fires under worktree mode — the [publication candidate](#integration-publisher) worktree each phase publishes for its reds to review, whose fresh checkout can never carry a gitignored member `node_modules`. FG-628 moves the precondition to the point the read-only mount is **decided**, keyed on the tree that will actually be bound (`worktreePath ?? projectDir`) rather than on an enumerated list of constructors, so candidates are covered by construction; the two worktree-lifecycle calls remain as two callers of the same `createDependencyMountpoints` mechanism rather than the only ones. A ready cache key authorizes the *content* of the mount, the mountpoints authorize the *mount itself*, and both are now required.

**Operator-visible side effect: a dispatch may create empty `<member>/node_modules` directories in your own live, non-isolated checkout.** Whenever a dispatch binds a project directory rather than an isolated workspace, that directory is the tree being bound, so it is where the mountpoints are created — a read-only reviewer or red, and since FG-678 a writable `forge invoke` or a read-write non-worktree workflow dispatch too. This was accepted deliberately over the alternative (refusing at preflight, which would convert a crash into a permanent refusal for every project that ever ran a root-only install). It is safe and reversible: the only thing created is an *empty* directory under an already-gitignored path, and git cannot see an empty ignored directory through `status --porcelain`, `status --porcelain --ignored`, or `ls-files --others` — so capture stays clean and the FG-356 reaper's unrecovered-work probe is unaffected. `git clean` is the one exception, and it cuts the harmless way: a plain `git clean -fd` skips the mountpoint like any ignored path, while a clean that deliberately targets ignored paths (`git clean -fdX` / `-fdx`) *does* remove it — and may remove an enclosing directory that holds nothing else. Nothing depends on it persisting, so the consequence is harmless: a later dispatch recreates the mountpoint. Creation is idempotent under concurrency (a fanout wave dispatches children in parallel against a shared project dir with no host-side mutual exclusion), and it never deletes or rewrites anything. **npm's own workspace resolution is unaffected**, measured against a root-only install: node resolution walks *up* from the requiring file and an empty `<member>/node_modules` contributes no candidates, so a member's `require`/`import` still resolves to the root-hoisted copy; `npm ls --workspace <member>` reports the same tree it did before the directory existed; `npm install` reports `up to date` rather than reinstalling; and `npm ci` succeeds and leaves the empty directory in place. npm derives install state from the lockfile and the packages it actually finds, never from the presence of a directory. **Editor and tooling watchers see one directory-creation event and nothing after it** — the directory stays empty for its whole life (the container writes *inside* the mounted volume, not into the host directory it binds over), so there is nothing to recurse into and no recurring churn, and a watcher that ignores `node_modules` by default (VS Code's `files.watcherExclude`, `tsc --watch`, bundler dev servers) skips the path outright. The one tool class that *can* misread an empty mountpoint is one whose "are dependencies installed?" test asks only whether `<member>/node_modules` exists rather than whether it has contents — which is why [Host verification readiness](#host-verification-readiness)'s **non-empty** `node_modules` check is what keeps one of these from reading as an already-installed tree. Delete them with `rmdir` if you want them gone; the next dispatch recreates them.

Failure to create the mountpoints is never fatal and never a block **on the read-write lane**. On a read-only or permission-denied checkout forge records a `container.dependency_mountpoints_unavailable` event (carrying the repo root and the underlying error) and simply proceeds with no dependency-cache mount — the same "cache isn't ready" posture as an unprovisioned key. Mounting anyway is what crashes the container. **FG-664 superseded that posture for an eligible READ-ONLY dispatch, and FG-678 extended it to every dispatch that crosses the shared resolver**: the same diagnosis is recorded and the dispatch is then refused (`mountpoints_unavailable`), because degrading there means running an agent against a workspace whose dependency environment nobody established — on the read-only lane, the host's own `node_modules` seen through the `/project:ro` bind; on the writable lane, the empty anonymous shadow. FG-628's degradation survives exactly where no resolution happens at all: a not-applicable configuration (non-darwin, `FORGE_NO_NM_SHADOW=1`, a project declaring no dependencies) never asks the question, so it proceeds with no dependency-cache mount as it always did.

**This cache is for CONTAINERS only, and is never reconciled with host-side readiness.** [Host verification readiness](#host-verification-readiness) (FG-566) keeps its own state under `~/.forge/host-readiness/` and is prohibited from reading or writing any `~/.forge/dependency-cache/` marker, volume or lock — it reuses this store's *discipline* (temp-then-rename atomic markers, a narrow lock, pid liveness plus a timeout backstop), not its keyspace. The keys are deliberately asymmetric: the container key above is **ABI-free**, because the image pins the interpreter, while the host key **must** carry the runtime version and ABI, because the host interpreter varies between machines and across `.nvmrc` switches. Writing this key from the host would make a later container dispatch skip its install against an empty volume — the same readiness lie, pointed the other way. The container nested-volume mount mechanics above (FG-627, widened by FG-628) are adjacent to both and are a third, separate thing; the one place they touch is that host readiness treats an *empty* `node_modules` (a pre-created mountpoint) as absent rather than as an installed tree — which matters more since FG-628, because those mountpoints can now appear in the operator's own live checkout, the very tree host readiness evaluates.

### The dependency-environment gate (FG-664, FG-678)

Before an eligible agent container starts, the host **resolves that dispatch's dependency environment** and either authorizes the mount or refuses the dispatch. This is one gate over **all three dispatch shapes**, and all of them cross one resolver (`prepareDependencyEnvironmentForDispatch`):

- **Read-only** — `forge invoke`'s reviewers and reds, which is how the [review coordinator](#review-coordinator) dispatches its discovery lenses and its [rechecker](#review-rechecker) and how [`forge review-loop`](#review-loop-reviewer) dispatches its red, plus `runNext`'s workflow reds (`projectMode === "ro"`). This is FG-664's lane; fixing it alone would have left every pipeline-dispatched red able to start with no dependency mount at all.
- **Read-write into an isolated workspace** — a workflow primary or fan-out child dispatching into a [worktree or private clone](#workspace-isolation-worktree-mode). This is FG-376's lane: named lockfile-keyed volumes mounted `:ro` after a Forge-owned provisioner populates them.
- **Read-write over the project directory itself** — a writable `forge invoke`, and a workflow read-write dispatch with no workspace (`FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1`, or a tree that is not mountable as one). This is the shape FG-678 fixed. It is keyed to the dispatch **shape**, not to the `forge invoke` entrypoint, so a workflow dispatch that lands in it is covered with it.

The third shape previously had no dependency provisioning at all: it fell to an *anonymous* `node_modules` volume with no source, which starts empty, masks whatever the bind mount has at that path, and dies with the container. Two consequences, both measured. A host-side `npm ci` in the operator's own checkout could not reach the container — the shadow masked it — so the obvious operator remedy did not work and could not work. And whether an agent recovered by self-installing was left to the agent: two `test-engineer` dispatches identical in role, model, profile, runtime and workspace diverged, one reasoning its way to an install and validating the change, the other running the target suite twenty times, collecting 1020 `ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND`, and reporting honestly that it could not execute anything. Both behaved defensibly; the platform had handed them different worlds. That is platform nondeterminism, and the fix is a contract rather than better prompting.

The rule FG-376 protects is refined, not relaxed, and it now covers every shape: **agent containers never install and never race an install; the host may provision, under the existing per-cache-key lock, before the agent starts.** Every agent's argv carries dependency volumes `:ro` and only `:ro`, no read-write dependency volume, no `FORGE_NM_INSTALL_ROOT` and no install command, whatever its project mount mode. Only the Forge-owned provisioner container ever writes the shared cache. That is what keeps a *shared writable* cache from ever sitting over a live shared project directory — the FG-376 race — and it is why isolation here comes from read-only-ness rather than from a per-dispatch copy of the tree. **The bound environment is therefore immutable for the dispatch**: an agent cannot add a dependency mid-task, and it is told so in its task package rather than discovering it as an `EROFS` halfway through. The `node_modules` mask itself survives in every arm and is load-bearing for host safety — what FG-678 changed is what sits *behind* the mask, never whether the host's own `node_modules` is exposed to a container that could write through it.

There are **three outcomes, and "not applicable" is one of them** — it is not a quiet refusal. The discriminator runs host-side off the project's own dependency manifest plus its lockfile:

1. **No readable dependency manifest, or a manifest declaring no dependencies — *not applicable*.** The dispatch proceeds exactly as it did before, with no refusal and no degradation. A project that declares no dependencies is never refused on this ground and is never told a falsehood about its own workspace.
2. **Declared dependencies with a supported lockfile — *resolve*.** The pre-existing path, unchanged.
3. **Declared dependencies with no supported lockfile — *refuse*, `lockfile_absent`.** Forge keys the environment off the lockfile, so a project that declares dependencies but ships none it supports (`package-lock.json`) is unkeyable: there is no reproducible environment to bind, and no honest way to say the workspace has no dependencies. The refusal names the declaring manifest, the lockfile Forge supports, and adding one as the remedy.

The `FORGE_NO_NM_SHADOW=1` and non-darwin escape hatches short-circuit **ahead of** that discriminator, so they still return *not applicable* for every project, lockfile or not. That is the same eligibility the cache itself has, for the same reason — on Linux the host's modules already carry the container's platform, so the hazard does not exist.

**One resolver, one rule — and it reaches the read-only lane too.** There is no writable-only branch and no mount-mode parameter, which has a consequence worth stating plainly rather than discovering: a darwin read-only reviewer against a project that declares dependencies and ships no supported lockfile now **REFUSES where it previously dispatched**. It is bounded by the two short-circuits above, so a non-darwin host and `FORGE_NO_NM_SHADOW=1` are unaffected. Supporting intentionally lockfile-less projects would be an explicit, designed opt-out — never an implicit degraded path, which is the shape this gate exists to eliminate.

**Ready.** The lockfile-keyed volumes are mounted `:ro` and the dispatch records a **receipt**: the cache key, the probe image, and the `process.version` / `process.versions.modules` (node ABI) plus per-package name, version, resolved path, compiled artifact and `loaded` verdict the probe observed *inside the mount shape the agent will run*. Repeated identical dispatches against the same workspace and lockfile bind the **same cache key**, and that key is answerable from the manifest and the ledger rather than inferred from container logs. A receipt is evidence of a MOUNT, not a substitute for one — no dispatch records a `dependencyEnvironment` it did not actually receive. The receipt lands on the task's [`manifest.json`](SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md#manifestjson-structure) (`dependencyEnvironment`, authoritative), the review's `recheck` stage evidence indexes the same receipt, and the timeline records `container.dependency_environment_resolved` — so "which engine did this verification actually run against" is read back from durable state rather than assumed. `loaded` is the **exit status of a Forge-owned container that did nothing but `dlopen` that one artifact**, never a claim the loaded code made about itself: the reporting container executes no dependency code at all, and each artifact is loaded alone in its own short-lived container, so an attested artifact has no report stream to reach and no other package's verdict to touch.

**Refused.** No agent container is started — the gate is fail-closed, so no agent is ever dispatched into a workspace it cannot execute in. The task fails `verification_environment_unavailable`, the timeline records `container.dependency_provisioning_failed` with `stage: "environment_resolution"` and the classified reason, and on both `forge invoke` lanes the manifest is still written — carrying `dispatchRefused` instead of a receipt, because [`forge retry`](#task-retry) recovers a task's mount mode from that manifest and a refused reviewer with no manifest falls back to a role-prefix guess that re-dispatches it read-write. (A refused workflow red writes no manifest: it is not a task `forge retry` re-dispatches, and its step parks rather than retrying.) Seven reasons, each naming what could not be established: `lockfile_absent` (the project declares dependencies and ships no supported lockfile, so the environment is unkeyable — decided from the manifest read alone, before any provisioner or probe runs), `mountpoints_unavailable`, `provisioning_failed`, `probe_failed`, `probe_unparseable`, `dependencies_absent` (a declared package is in no mounted root, or declares a native build and ships no artifact), and `driver_unloadable`. `blocked_environment` stays a review *state* rather than becoming a task-plane failure kind: the review lane records the refusal as [`blocked_environment`](#review-coordinator) — a stop that dispatches no fixer and consumes no review cycle; a workflow red parks its step [`blocked_by_red`](#blocked-by-red), so an unreviewed artifact waits for an operator rather than advancing on a review that never happened.

**A disproven ready marker self-heals, once.** The `.ready` marker lives under `~/.forge` while the volumes it speaks for are docker objects, so a `docker volume prune` or a Docker Desktop factory reset leaves a marker whose volumes are empty. When the probe proves the install root empty for a key already marked ready, the marker is invalidated and the key re-provisioned **exactly once** under the same lock, then re-probed; the receipt records that repair (`staleCacheRepaired` — the disproven root, the invalidated marker value, and whether this dispatch or a concurrent one re-provisioned). A cache that is still empty after the repair, or whose declared drivers still will not load, is refused, and that refusal is final. Without the repair the refusal would be permanent, with no operator-visible remedy but deleting a file by hand.

**A self-declared failure is terminal (FG-678).** The gate above is only half of what made the original defect hard to see. The other half is at the ingestion seam: `forge invoke` used to complete a task row unconditionally once the agent's `result.json` parsed, without ever inspecting the `status` the agent had declared in it — so an agent that reported honestly that it could not execute anything still landed a `complete` row, the run settled, and the validation that never happened left no visible mark. A parsed result declaring `status: "failed"` now fails the task with `failure_kind: "agent_reported_failure"`, emits no `task.completed`, and returns `status: "failed"` from `forge invoke`; a result declaring `status: "complete"` is unaffected. This is deliberately broad — it changes outcome reporting for **every** invoke role, reds, research and architect dispatches included — and deliberately narrow in what it does not touch: it is not a widening of the validation contract, `IMPLEMENTER_ROLES` and the `tests_run` gate are unchanged, and an ad-hoc `forge invoke` completion is still ungated. As a blocker it is scoped **item-local**, never a shared campaign blocker: one agent's self-declared failure is a fact about that task.

**What this does and does not close.** It removes the environmental cause and the incentive: a lane that cannot load the project's real driver never starts, so there is nothing to work around. It does **not** detect an agent that *can* load the real driver and fabricates output anyway — engine-difference failures emit genuine `not ok` lines that are textually indistinguishable from a real regression. Enforcement is host-side and pre-dispatch precisely because an in-container assertion is unenforceable (agents have passwordless root in their own container), which is also why the probe is Forge's own container running a fixed command. Full reasoning, including the costs accepted: `learnings/decisions/2026-08-02_reviewer-dependency-environment.md` (FORGE-DEC-030), and invariant 19 in [invariants.md](invariants.md).

## Private writable Git for mutating agents

Under [workspace isolation](#workspace-isolation-worktree-mode) (the default since FG-345) a **mutating** agent — a workflow primary or a fan-out child — is not handed a linked worktree. It gets its own repository: a `git clone --shared` of the project, created at the task's **recorded base SHA**, checked out on the deterministic private branch `forge/<runId>/<taskId>`, at `~/.forge/worktrees/clones/<runId>/<taskId>`. The workspace path and the base are written to the task row in one write, *before* the container starts, so both survive a process restart and neither has to be inferred later. Non-mutating agents keep FG-559's read-only linked worktree unchanged (see [Worktree git access](#worktree-git-access)).

**Creation also anchors that branch in the parent's ref namespace**, at the base SHA, before the agent runs — so `forge/<runId>/<taskId>` is visible in the operator's own repo from dispatch rather than only after capture. It does three jobs the clone's own refs cannot: it pins the base against a parent `gc` (the clone's alternates point into that object store, and an unreferenced base is a collectable base), it makes the task's identity present in the repository the operator and `forge show` read, and it turns capture's fetch into a fast-forward of an existing ref rather than a creation, so even a partial capture is a ref movement the reaper can reason about. The agent cannot touch it — the parent's refs are not in its container. Creation is **create-only in both directions**: an existing clone directory *or* an existing anchor ref refuses (`parent-side ref … already exists at <sha> — a task workspace identity must be fresh`) rather than reusing or deleting either, because that ref may hold a prior attempt's captured work. Inspect it and delete it deliberately if it is genuinely stale.

The reason is that **a worktree isolates the working tree, not the repository.** Every worktree of a repo shares one `objects/`, one ref namespace and one `packed-refs`, so an agent with write access to any of them has write access to the parent's history and to every sibling worktree's basis — and a writable `objects/` is a *deletable* `objects/`. A clone has its own refs, index and object overlay, and reaches the parent's history only through `objects/info/alternates`, which forge mounts read-only into the container. The parent's unwritability is therefore a property of the **mount**, not a rule git is asked to enforce (FG-621; measured during FG-559 at ~0.1s and under 1MB per clone, full depth). What exactly gets mounted, what the alternates file has to prove before it is believed, and the dispatch refusals that follow when it cannot are under [The private-clone substrate](#the-private-clone-substrate-the-object-store-and-nothing-else-fg-621) — including two states an operator's *own* shared-clone project directory can be in that now refuse to dispatch.

**Commit authority is split.** The agent may commit freely on its own branch, in its own repository; those commits are untrusted checkpoints and transport artifacts. **Publication authority stays with Forge** — only Forge builds the candidate, runs the authoritative gates, and moves the target ref, and it may squash or replace the agent's commit boundaries (FG-345, FG-340).

**The clone is also where the no-AI-attribution commit-msg hook has to live (FG-685), and it gets there by a different mechanism than the operator's own checkout.** `forge init`'s installer (above, under [Slash commands](#slash-commands)) symlinks *through* `$FORGE_HOME/current`, which is right for a checkout that lives on the host next to `$FORGE_HOME` — but `$FORGE_HOME` is not mounted inside the agent container, so that same symlink inside a clone would dangle, and a dangling `commit-msg` hook is git reporting no hook at all, with no diagnostic. `createTaskClone` instead installs the hook as a **self-contained regular file** — the bundled bytes copied in, executable, no symlink, no host path — at the moment it creates the clone, before the container can reach the workspace, and pins `core.hooksPath` in the clone's own `--local` git config (`.git/hooks`, workspace-relative, since an absolute pin would name a path that does not exist inside the container) so the effective hooks directory is decided by the workspace itself rather than by whatever global or system git config the agent image happens to carry. Ownership of an existing target is then decided by **content identity, not link identity** — bytes equal to the bundled hook are Forge's to repair (e.g. a stripped executable bit); a foreign regular file, any symlink, or an unreadable target is someone else's and is left untouched. Before handing the clone to the container, `assertWorkspaceHookEnforceable` *proves* rather than assumes the hook will run — regular file, executable, bundled bytes, and the pinned `core.hooksPath` resolving to the directory it was installed into — and provisioning refuses the clone by name (`CommitMsgHookUnenforceableError`) rather than silently handing an agent a workspace whose guard never fires. This is a **layered mitigation, not an adversarial boundary**, and is scoped deliberately no wider than that: it defends against the *default* attribution behavior of a *cooperating* agent — the FG-610 failure this closes, where a `Co-Authored-By` trailer reached the publish HEAD and the only remedy, a history rewrite, orphaned the run's task-branch lineage. It defends against nothing adversarial — `git commit --no-verify` bypasses it, same as it bypasses the operator's own hook, and the agent is root in its own container — the same posture the [private-clone substrate](#the-private-clone-substrate-the-object-store-and-nothing-else-fg-621)'s read-only mount takes as an actual boundary, which this is not.

**The base is a receipt, not a guess.** A task's clone is created from the last accepted publication receipt for its run (`publication_attempts.published_sha`), falling back to the resolved `projectDir` `HEAD` only when the run has published nothing yet. So a sequential task starts from the exact candidate its predecessor published — including on a `remote:` target, which never advances local `HEAD` — and every sibling of one **concurrently-dispatched group** records the same base, resolved once before any of them is spawned. For an unordered fan-out the whole wave is one group, so that is the same rule it has always been; for an [ordered fan-out](#ordered-fanout-dependency-aware-build-execution) FG-584 narrowed it exactly as far as ordering requires and no further — a dependent's base is the run's candidate *after* its prerequisites integrated, which necessarily differs from theirs, while two items that can run at the same time still start from the same commit (resolving per-child would let a concurrent publication move the base underneath a live group).

**Capture is a fixed ordering**, and it is a contract rather than an implementation detail. On completion Forge: safety-commits whatever the agent left dirty, **tracked and untracked** (untracked output exists nowhere else) — through `git commit --no-verify`, deliberately bypassing the FG-685 hook above, because the safety-commit is Forge-authored with a fixed message, not agent-authored, and routing a known-clean string through the hook would only risk turning an unrelated hook failure (a userland regex quirk, a missing interpreter, an agent that replaced the hook) into a lost capture, since capture fails **before** the branch is fetched into the parent; resolves the clone's branch tip; fetches that branch into the parent's ref namespace under the same deterministic name — a real durable ref, never `FETCH_HEAD`, because it is simultaneously the gc anchor, the publisher's input and the reaper's reachability input; verifies the fetched ref **equals** the resolved tip; and only then hands it to the [integration publisher](#integration-publisher). Nothing commits into the clone afterwards: the candidate source is handed over *without* a workspace path, so the publisher's auto-commit cannot reach it. That omission is what makes the rule structural — the clone and the parent are different repositories, so a post-fetch commit in the clone would advance only the clone's ref and be silently absent from the published candidate. A capture that fails at any step fails the task and **retains** the clone; nothing is disposed of on an unverified capture.

Disposal is covered under [Reaping an orphaned workspace](#reaping-an-orphaned-workspace) — a clone is removed only against an ownership proof based on its alternates target, a clean tree including ignored files, and reachability from Forge-owned state.

**The boundary claim above is proven live, by hand, not by a test tier.** No test tier in this repo can run a real Docker container, and a skip-capable check is not acceptable as the proof of a security boundary — a green skip *is* a false security proof. `scripts/fg621-clone-boundary-smoke.sh` is that proof: an operator-run script that starts a real container with the exact mount shape above and shows the parent repository is genuinely unwritable from inside it. See [How-to: writing and running tests → Proving the private-clone container boundary](how-to-testing.md#proving-the-private-clone-container-boundary-fg-621) for how to run it and when it must be re-run.

## Worktree git access

A linked git worktree's `.git` is not a directory — it is a one-line pointer file (`gitdir: <absolute path>`) into the **parent** repo's `.git`, which forge did not mount. Inside the container that pointer dangled and every git command failed with `fatal: not a git repository`, so an agent that would have consulted history silently worked from the file tree instead and reported success anyway (FG-559 — caught when a review-loop reviewer could not run the diff it was reviewing). Forge now bind-mounts the pointer's target **read-only, at its own host absolute path**, whenever the project being dispatched against is a linked worktree — so the absolute `gitdir:` hop and the relative `commondir` hop (`../..`, into the common `.git`) both land on mounted storage. Mounting at the host path is what makes this work with no environment variables: `GIT_DIR`/`GIT_WORK_TREE` are global to the container's process tree, and would make an unrelated repo inside the container report the *worktree's* status — a silent wrong answer in place of a loud failure. The mount is computed in `buildDockerArgs` rather than declared in runtime YAML (a YAML mount would only reach dispatches running a regenerated seed generation, leaving older generations quietly broken), and it is `:ro` for **every** agent class, blue and red alike; `PROJECT_MODE` — read-write for blues, read-only for reds — is a separate mount and is unchanged. It applies to any dispatch whose project directory is a linked worktree: one you created by hand and passed to `--project` just as much as a forge-managed worktree under [workspace isolation](#workspace-isolation-worktree-mode), which since FG-345 is the default. What this widens for reds, and what it deliberately does not promise, is under [Red agent](#red-agent).

**Scope: absolute `gitdir:` pointers.** The bind reproduces one host absolute path inside the container, which covers what `git worktree add` writes by default. It does **not** cover a *relative* pointer — every git submodule (`gitdir: ../.git/modules/<name>`), and worktrees created with `git worktree add --relative-paths` on git 2.48+. Those resolve in-container against `/project`, to a path the bind never covers, so such a project still has no working git; it fails loudly at the probe below rather than being fixed. Tracked as FG-622. The accepted cost of the whole approach is that container paths stop being hermetic under a worktree mount — a host absolute path is reproduced inside the container — which is the price of not setting `GIT_DIR`.

**Two host-side refusals, before any container starts.** `assertWorktreeGitMountPlanned` asks a question about the argv being handed to docker, not about the filesystem: `PROJECT_DIR/.git` points outside `PROJECT_DIR` — is that path in the mount list? The host *cannot* catch this by resolving the path, since host-side the pointer resolves perfectly, which is exactly why the defect stayed silent. It refuses rather than warns, and unconditionally (`FG-559: … Refusing to dispatch`): a non-git project has no `.git`, a plain clone has a `.git` directory, and a correctly-mounted worktree passes, so the check can only fire on the broken state. Separately, `preflightProjectMount` no longer reads the mere existence of `.git` as evidence of a healthy repo — the bare `existsSync` it replaced returned true for the pointer file, and that single line is the seam the defect walked through. A pointer whose target is gone host-side (a pruned worktree, or a parent repo moved or deleted) now fails preflight with `project mount preflight failed: … does not exist on the host`, instead of dispatching an agent that cannot run git. That preflight runs against the path actually mounted at `/project` — under worktree mode the task worktree, not the run's `--project` root.

**What the pointer is allowed to name.** The `gitdir:` line lives in the project tree, so a project — or a prior writable agent — can rewrite it. Forge never binds what it says on trust: the target must be a real worktree admin dir of the shape `git worktree add` writes (`<common>/.git/worktrees/<name>`, carrying git's own `commondir` and `gitdir` files), its `commondir` must resolve back to that same parent `.git`, and that parent must actually be a git dir (`HEAD`, `objects`, `refs`). Anything else — a pointer at `$HOME`, at a hand-built look-alike, or at a submodule's `.git/modules/<name>` — is refused (`FG-559: … refusing to mount an unverified host path`) rather than reproduced inside every later container.

**Sentinel exit code 122, after the container starts.** The entrypoint probes `git rev-parse --git-dir` in the project mount before exec'ing the agent command (skipped entirely when the project has no `.git`), and exits **122** if git does not resolve — the peer of the 123 dependency-provisioning sentinel above, and deliberately not 124, which is `timeout(1)`'s conventional code. The host assertion proves the mount was *planned*; this proves it *resolved*, and the two fail for different reasons. `invoke.ts`/`runNext.ts` classify 122 as `failure_kind: "verification_environment_unavailable"` and record a `container.git_unavailable` event (container name, exit code, and container causal evidence when available); the operator-facing error is `verification_environment_unavailable: git is unusable in the project mount — <stderr tail>`, and the container's own stderr names the failing git error and points at FG-559. On a macOS worktree dispatch with an unpopulated dependency cache the **provisioner** container (above) is the first one to run that entrypoint, so 122 surfaces there rather than at the agent container — `runNext.ts` classifies a provisioner's 122 the same way, recording `container.git_unavailable` naming the `forge-provision-<cacheKey>` container and never `container.dependency_provisioning_failed` (which would send the operator hunting npm for a git fault). Reconcile inherits that classification when the watcher dies inside the window — an already-recorded `container.git_unavailable` event, or a docker-reported exit 122 on a container that is gone, lands the same kind (reason `container_git_unavailable`) rather than a generic orphan, but never ahead of a recoverable result: persisted work is accounted for first. That inheritance needs one of those two signals, which bounds it to the **agent** container's window. A host death inside the *provisioning* sub-window leaves neither: the provisioner runs `--rm`, so its 122 exit leaves no container to inspect, and the branch that would have recorded the event never ran. Such a task lands in FG-437's mid-provisioning branch ([Orphaned task recovery](#orphaned-task-recovery)) instead — the same `verification_environment_unavailable` kind, but reason `provisioning_phase_crash` and that branch's generic "retry — dependencies may already be cached" advice rather than this section's "fix the mount first". Accepted, not fixed: the kind is right, the suggested retry re-fails loudly at the same probe rather than dispatching a history-blind agent, and reclassifying it would mean reconcile parsing a provisioner stderr log for a git string. Like 123 it is an environment failure, not a container crash and not a task failure — nothing about the agent's work is implicated. `forge retry` is therefore pointless until the mount is fixed; the usual fixes are dispatching against the parent repo instead of the worktree, or restoring the parent repo the worktree points at. **The refusal is unconditional — there is no bypass.** No env var, runtime `env:` block, or flag dispatches a deliberately history-blind agent past an unresolved `.git`: an escape hatch here is precisely the silent degradation FG-559 exists to eliminate, so a broken mount is fixed, never waved through.

### The private-clone substrate: the object store, and nothing else (FG-621)

A mutating agent's workspace ([Private writable Git for mutating agents](#private-writable-git-for-mutating-agents)) is a `git clone --shared`, whose `.git` is a real *directory* — so none of the pointer-file handling above applies to it, and the linked-worktree path is unchanged. Its git is nonetheless broken inside the container for the same underlying reason: the clone's `objects/info/alternates` names the parent repository's object store by **host absolute path**, which forge did not mount. The same `buildDockerArgs` loop therefore identity-binds that object store `:ro` — the parent's `objects` directory **alone**, never the parent `.git`. The narrowness *is* the boundary: the parent's refs, index, `HEAD` and `packed-refs` are then **absent** from the container rather than merely unwritable, so parent ref creation and `main`/`origin/*`/packed-ref updates fail by absence, and object-store deletion by the kernel-enforced `:ro` bind. The provisioner container (which starts first) gets exactly the same view, so the checks below have to hold there too, not only at the agent container.

**Derive, then verify — never trust-then-bind.** `objects/info/alternates` lives *inside* the workspace forge mounts read-write, so it is agent-writable — strictly more so than the `gitdir:` pointer above — and it is never read as an instruction to mount a path. It is a value that must survive four proofs:

1. **Shape** — exactly one entry, absolute and unquoted, resolving to a real `objects` directory of a real git dir (the same `HEAD`/`objects`/`refs` marker triple a worktree's common dir must show), and never the workspace's own object store.
2. **Identity, mandatory** — the resolved path must be one of the object stores that forge's own record of the project reaches. That record is `CANONICAL_PROJECT_DIR` (always the run's `projectDir`, derived through `git rev-parse --git-path objects` rather than by assuming a `<dir>/.git/objects` layout, so a `projectDir` that is itself a linked worktree still answers). This is the only proof that says "*this* project's parent" rather than "some git repository" — an agent that repoints its alternates at any other repo on the host satisfies every other check. A missing canonical dir is therefore itself a refusal, never a skip and never a fallback to the other proofs: a verification that silently does not run is not a verification.
3. **Base-commit containment** — that store must actually hold the workspace's checked-out commit.
4. **The whole chain** — a parent that itself borrows serves part of the history from a store one hop further out, so every hop is proved the same way and mounted, bounded at 8 hops with a cycle guard. Mounting only the first hop would hand the container an incomplete object graph, which is the FG-559 history-blind failure in a new place.

Every failure **refuses to dispatch**, before any container starts, with `FG-621: <dir> is a git repository whose object store … refusing to mount an unverified host path`. Never a warning and never a fallback to "no mount": a silent no-mount here is the FG-559 defect twin — a container whose `git log` and `git commit` both fail, and whose agent then works from the file tree and reports success.

**This is checked for every project directory, not only forge-created clones**, because the planner classifies whatever is being dispatched against. An ordinary checkout and an ordinary (non-shared) clone have no `objects/info/alternates`, so they are unaffected — that is the overwhelmingly common dispatch and it costs one `stat`. But a project directory that is *itself* a `git clone --shared` or `--reference` goes through the same proofs, and two states an operator can genuinely be in now refuse where they previously dispatched:

- **A shared clone carrying a commit its source repo does not have.** Any local-only commit fails proof 3: `… does not contain this workspace's checked-out commit <sha> — it is not the parent repository this clone was made from`. Fix it by getting that commit into the source repo (`git push`/`fetch` between them), or by making the checkout self-contained — `git repack -a -d` copies the borrowed objects in, after which deleting `.git/objects/info/alternates` leaves a full-history repo that plans no mount at all. Deleting the alternates file *without* repacking is not a fix; it breaks the repository host-side too.
- **A shared clone whose alternates file was deleted** — it owns no objects of its own, yet `HEAD` still resolves. That is an unverifiable state, not an ordinary repo, and it is refused rather than dispatched history-blind.

**The refusal is unconditional — there is no bypass**, for the same reason the FG-559 refusals have none. `FORGE_WORKTREES` is irrelevant to it: the check is on the project directory being mounted, not on isolation mode.

## Agent-container settings isolation

Forge agents run with on-disk Claude Code settings discovery suppressed. Claude Code normally auto-discovers configuration from `<cwd>/.claude/settings*.json` (user, project, and local layers). Because the agent's working directory is the project bind mount, the orchestrator's own `.claude/settings.json` and `.claude/settings.local.json` — written for the host environment — are visible from inside the container. Without isolation those settings leak: an `env.CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK` entry flips the agent's auth mode, a `model` field swaps the model forge selected, and `hooks` blocks reference host-only paths that crash on execution inside the container.

Forge passes `--setting-sources ""` to every claude-code runtime invocation, which suppresses all three discovery layers. Agents receive configuration only through what forge passes explicitly: environment variables injected by the runtime YAML, the `--model` flag, and `--append-system-prompt`. The flag is scoped to claude-code runtimes; codex and pi CLIs do not accept it and are not affected.

Example: if a project's `.claude/settings.local.json` contains `{ "env": { "CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK": "1" } }` for the orchestrator's Bedrock session, without isolation every agent spawned for that project's runs would switch to Bedrock auth regardless of the runtime's declared auth mode — crashing silently if Bedrock credentials are absent inside the container, or silently switching auth if they're present.

## Docs impact

A run-level assessment of whether changes to operator-visible behavior have been reflected in durable docs. Three integrated signals:

1. **Contract flag.** A task's contract may declare `operator_behavior_changed: true` explicitly. When the orchestrator omits it, forge infers the value from the task's `files_modified`: if any path touches a defined operator surface, the flag is inferred as `true`. The surface list is project-configurable: add `<project>/.forge/docs-surfaces.yml` (shape: `{ surfaces: [<path-prefix>, ...] }`) to fully replace forge's built-in defaults for that project. Without that file, forge's own defaults apply (CLI commands, workflow seeds, runtime configs, the notify layer, the contract schema, etc.).

2. **`forge show` suggestion.** When displaying a completed task whose `files_modified` touched operator surfaces, `forge show` prints a suggestion line: `docs impact: operator surfaces changed (...) — durable docs may be stale; consider: forge invoke documentation-maintainer`. Advisory only — not a gate.

3. **Ship-time warning.** `forge notify milestone --kind shipped` assesses the full run before dispatching. If any task changed operator behavior and no task in the run reported `docs_updated` or a `docs_not_updated_reason`, the notification body includes an advisory warning naming the surfaces and suggesting `forge invoke documentation-maintainer --run <id>`. The warning never blocks shipping.

Resolution: the docs impact is considered resolved when a task in the run reports a non-empty `docs_updated` array (the `documentation-maintainer` ran and made changes) or a non-empty `docs_not_updated_reason` (a principled deferral was recorded). Recording a reason is equivalent to resolution — it makes the decision explicit and auditable.

Example: after the engineer modified `src/cli/commands/show.ts`, `forge show` printed a docs impact suggestion; the orchestrator chained `forge invoke documentation-maintainer --run <id>` and the maintainer updated this glossary.

## Host verification readiness

**This is not [Readiness](#readiness) below** — the two share a word and nothing else. That one is the ticket-level preflight (`forge readiness <ticket-id>`) asking whether a *backlog item* is refined enough to implement. This one asks whether a *workspace* is executable: before Forge runs a verification it owns, on the host, in a directory it created moments ago, are the dependencies and the interpreter actually there. Do not conflate them.

The defect it exists to eliminate (FG-566) is one shape appearing on two paths: a Forge-owned verification runs in a freshly-created workspace whose `node_modules` is correctly absent, and the resulting failure is reported as a verdict on the reviewed **code**. On the review-loop path a fresh standalone clone fails `typecheck` and `test` before either can examine the implementation, the loop converts that into fixer findings, and a review round is spent inviting an agent to fix passing code. On the publication path the [integration gate](#integration-publisher) runs against the candidate worktree `createCandidateWorktree` just built — which has no `node_modules` either — dies with `ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND`, and the task is recorded `integration_failed`. Both are environment faults wearing a code verdict.

**The primitive is prepare-or-refuse.** Before any Forge-owned host-side verification runs, readiness either establishes an execution-ready environment bound to that exact workspace, or it stops once with a classified environment outcome. It takes a workspace directory, that workspace's tree identity (the clone's `HEAD`, or the publisher's already-recorded `candidateSha`), the setup command, and the intended verification runtime as an explicit interpreter plus pinned `PATH`. That interpreter is only half the runtime the verification actually gets: the other half is the *environment* Forge constructs around it, which inherits the operator's general environment but withholds Forge's own `FORGE_*` namespace in full — see **The workspace supplies data; the operator supplies the command** below. It returns `ready` with a durable evidence record — `{workspace, treeSha, lockfileDigest, nodeVersion, abi, requiredAbi, interpreter, setupCommand, coveredCommandSet, reused}`, where `requiredAbi` records *what the ABI was checked against and where that requirement came from* — or `not_required`, or exactly one classified refusal carrying `{reason, workspace, command, exitStatus, stderrTail, message}`. The orchestrator never hand-installs dependencies to make a Forge-owned verification runnable; if Forge cannot identify a safe setup contract it fails *before* verification rather than guessing.

**Readiness asserts exactly three things, and it is not a promise that anything will pass.** A `ready` result says: (1) the declared dependency setup **completed** — every step exited zero; (2) the interpreter the verification will run under **matches the ABI** the workspace's bindings require; (3) preparation **did not modify the Git tree** under test. That is the whole claim. Readiness never runs a verification command, so it cannot promise that one will succeed, and it must not be read — or implemented — as if it could. Two consequences follow, and both are load-bearing:

- **A native build failure during setup is a readiness failure.** `npm ci` runs the install lifecycle scripts that build a native dependency's bindings; when that build exits non-zero, setup failed, and the outcome is `setup_failed` — a named refusal that publishes nothing and moves no ref. This case only became reachable when lifecycle-script suppression was dropped (below). While they were suppressed the build simply never happened: `npm ci` exited zero over a `node_modules/better-sqlite3/` with `binding.gyp`, `deps`, `lib`, `src` and **no `build/` directory**, readiness certified the workspace ready, and every DB-backed test then died on `Error: Could not locate the bindings file` — an environment fault wearing a code verdict, which is the very thing this section exists to eliminate.
- **A test failure after a successful setup is a code verdict.** Unchanged, and stated again below under *Readiness is a precondition*: `integration_failed` on the publication path, an ordinary verification failure reaching the fixer on the review-loop path.

**Refusal vocabulary** (one classified reason, never a bare throw):

| Reason | Meaning | What an operator does about it |
|---|---|---|
| `no_setup_contract` | No declared way to make this project runnable. Forge refuses rather than guessing an install command. | Declare one host-side: `hostVerificationSetup` in `~/.forge/config.json`, or `FORGE_HOST_VERIFICATION_SETUP` for a single invocation. A project that commits a `package-lock.json` gets the derived `npm ci` and needs neither. |
| `self_host_workspace` | The workspace overlaps the Forge source root the running process is executing from. An install there deletes the native bindings Forge itself is loaded from. No override. | **Intended, not a bug.** Point the command at a checkout outside the Forge source root — `forge review-loop … --project <clone>`. See **Forge never prepares its own checkout** below. |
| `runtime_unresolved` | The intended verification interpreter could not be resolved on the pinned `PATH`, or the workspace's ABI requirement could not be established. | When no `node` was found at all the refusal names the pinned `PATH` it searched; when one was found but could not be probed it names the interpreter. Either way, put a working `node` on the control toolchain (see [Durable launch](#durable-launch), FG-555) — Forge will not prepare or verify under an interpreter it cannot identify. |
| `runtime_abi_mismatch` | The resolved interpreter's ABI does not match what the installed tree requires (`checkAbi`, `src/cli/node-abi.ts` — one ABI checker, never a second). | The refusal names the requirement *and where it came from*. Switch the control toolchain to the Node major that source demands. If the source is a stale prior `ready` record — the workspace was installed under an interpreter you have since retired — delete that workspace's record under `~/.forge/host-readiness/` (the files are hashed by workspace path; the path is in each record's `workspace` field) and let the next attempt reinstall. |
| `ambiguous_setup_contract` | The declared setup contract cannot be read as argv — it contains a shell operator (`&&`, `\|\|`, `;`, `\|`, a redirect, a backtick, `$()`) or a quote character, or its JSON is not an argv array. Forge refuses rather than splitting it on whitespace and mis-executing it. | Say what you meant as argv: `["npm","ci"]`, or a sequence for multiple steps — `[["npm","ci"],["npm","run","build:native"]]`. See **The setup contract is structured argv** below. |
| `setup_failed` | A setup step ran and exited non-zero — **including a native dependency's build**. | Read the `stderrTail` on the refusal — it is the *tail*, where npm (or node-gyp) names the failure. Nothing was verified, so this is not a verdict on the code. Run the same argv in that workspace by hand to reproduce. A multi-step contract names which step failed and stops there. |
| `setup_timed_out` | The setup contract exceeded its ceiling. The ceiling bounds the **whole contract**, not each step: every step draws on one shared budget, so a later step gets only what the earlier ones left. A multi-step contract names which step was running when it ran out. | Raise `FORGE_HOST_READINESS_SETUP_TIMEOUT_MS` (see the knobs below) — size it for the contract end to end, not for its slowest step — or warm the npm cache. |
| `setup_contended` | Another host process holds readiness for this key and did not release it in time. | Another `forge` is preparing the same workspace; let it finish and retry. The lock self-heals — it is released when the holding pid dies, and there is a timeout backstop for a recycled pid — so it should never need clearing by hand. |
| `workspace_dirtied_by_setup` | Setup changed the workspace's `git status` — see the tree-identity invariant below. | The declared setup command writes into the tree (an `npm install` that rewrites the lockfile is the usual cause). Declare one that does not; `npm ci` does not. |

**`not_required` is the fourth outcome, and it is never a refusal** — readiness must not newly block a path that was already runnable. It is returned, and nothing is installed, when there are no verification commands to cover; when the workspace has no readable `package.json`, so there is nothing npm-shaped to establish; when its `package.json` declares no dependencies at all (a scripts-only manifest has no dependency graph to build); or when the workspace already carries a **non-empty** `node_modules` that Forge did not provision — no readiness record exists for it, so it is the operator's own tree and `npm ci` deleting and rebuilding it as remediation is forbidden outright. That last arm keys on the *absence of any record*, not the absence of a matching one: a record left in `preparing` by a failed or crashed install means the workspace **is** Forge's to re-prepare, and must never be mistaken for a foreign tree. "Non-empty" matters because forge pre-creates empty `node_modules` mountpoint directories (FG-627, and since FG-628 in any tree about to be bound at a container's project path — including a live checkout); an existence check alone would read one of those as already provisioned and skip the install, reintroducing this very defect.

**One contract, two legitimately different mechanics.** A persistent review clone may reuse a warm install; a disposable publication worktree may materialize a throwaway one. What is **shared** is the part that decides truth: the fidelity checks (tree identity, lockfile digest, runtime/ABI), the durable evidence record, and the classification vocabulary above. What legitimately **differs** is materialization, and the projection of one classified result into each consumer's own outcome space — the review loop stops with its own stop reason, the publisher returns a publication outcome distinct from validation failure. Two consumers, one classifier; a second readiness notion would drift, which is why this is one contract rather than two fixes.

**The projections, and what each consumer promises.** On the review-loop path a refusal stops the loop with its own distinct stop reason — `verification_environment_unavailable`, not an ordinary `verification_failed`. **No round record is written for it**: environment preparation is not a review round, so a refusal consumes zero rounds and dispatches neither reviewer nor fixer. A refusal at the loop's first verification therefore returns with `rounds` empty (the deterministic `self_host_workspace` case always lands here); one that first appears on a later round — a lock that became contended, say — leaves exactly the rounds that genuinely ran and adds none. Both the rendered run note and the command's own stdout carry the literal token `verification_environment_unavailable` plus the refusal reason, and the terminal line states outright that no verification ran and that this is not a verdict on the reviewed code. Readiness is consulted only inside the local-fallback and dirty-tree arms — **CI reuse stays first-class**, so trusted covering evidence ([Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate](#evidence-reuse-for-the-deterministic-gate-fg-474)) returns before any local provisioning is attempted. On the publication path a refusal is a distinct outcome that **publishes nothing and moves no ref**, and classifies as `failure_kind: "verification_environment_unavailable"` — the *existing* kind FG-376 already raises for a container-side install failure ([Agent worktree dependency parity](#agent-worktree-dependency-parity)), carrying a reason discriminant, never a third incompatible notion of dependency readiness. It is explicitly **not** `integration_failed`. Readiness also inherits the gate's existing skip: a project with no `test:unit` script has nothing to prepare for and is not newly blocked. On the ordered fan-out's mid-wave D1 gate the projection is the same as the publication path's — a refusal moves no ref (the gated-candidate ref simply does not advance) and reports `verification_environment_unavailable` — except that nothing publishes there either way, since the mid-wave gate never touches the publish target: the wave still lets independent ready work finish before the caller fails the phase (see [Fail-phase timing changed, deliberately (D3)](#fail-phase-timing-changed-deliberately-d3)).

**Readiness is a precondition, never a post-hoc reinterpretation of gate output.** It is established *before* the verification runs, from facts about the workspace, and it never looks at the verification's exit status afterwards to decide the failure was environmental. That ordering is what makes the inverse defect structurally impossible: a genuine test failure *after* a successful preparation keeps existing behavior on both paths, and a real red is never laundered into an infrastructure outcome. Say it in the positive, because it is the half readers most need to trust: a candidate whose environment was prepared successfully and whose `test:unit` then genuinely fails is still **`integration_failed`**, with the gate's own classification and its fail-closed default ([Integration publisher](#integration-publisher)) untouched; and on the review-loop path a local run that genuinely fails after a successful preparation is still an ordinary failed verification that feeds the fixer and consumes its round. Only a refusal — produced *before* anything ran — is an environment outcome. Laundering a real failure is the more dangerous direction of this bug, not the lesser one.

**The workspace supplies data; the operator supplies the command.** Everything read *from* the workspace under test is a fact to be measured — `package.json` presence, the lockfile digest, the Node major the project pins in `.nvmrc` / `engines.node`. The setup **contract** is never taken from the workspace, nor from any project checkout: it comes from `FORGE_HOST_VERIFICATION_SETUP` or `hostVerificationSetup` in the **host-level** `~/.forge/config.json`, and otherwise from the fixed lockfile-derived `npm ci`. A `<project>/.forge/config.json` is deliberately not consulted — it sits inside the tree under review, and these argv reach `execFileSync` on the host with no container boundary, so a reviewed change that could name them would choose a binary Forge runs under its own identity. The setup child gets an explicitly constructed **minimal environment** (a pinned `PATH` plus only what a package manager needs — never Forge's process environment); that is hygiene, not a boundary — see the trust model below. The runtime is likewise explicit — the interpreter resolved off the pinned `PATH`, never whichever `node` an ambient login shell resolves, and the same pinned `PATH` covers the verification readiness certified, not just the install; see the control-toolchain contract under [Durable launch](#durable-launch) (FG-555).

**The verification child keeps the operator's environment and loses Forge's.** It is not a second setup child: a verification is running the operator's own test suite, *after* preparation rather than as an install-time lifecycle hook, so it legitimately needs HOME, npm config, TMPDIR, locale and the rest, and it inherits them. The one exception is Forge's own reserved namespace. **Every `FORGE_*` variable is withheld** — the whole namespace, `FORGE_HOME` included, with nothing exempted by name — and the pinned `PATH` is then applied over what remains (`pinnedVerificationEnv`, `src/v2/host-readiness.ts`). The reason is that `FORGE_*` are **control-plane switches**: inheriting them lets the orchestrator's own configuration reconfigure the very code under verification. FG-636 is what that costs. A wave launched as `env FORGE_WORKTREES=1 forge next …` spread the switch into every unit-test child, worktree mode came on inside a suite that dispatches against `.git`-less fixture directories, dispatch aborted before writing its task manifest, and the gate returned nine test failures on a candidate that contained **no code change at all**. The gate's job is to answer the same question CI answers, and CI runs the tier with no `FORGE_*` switch set — which is precisely why a leaked switch produced a verdict CI could never reproduce. Since FG-345 made isolation [default-on](#workspace-isolation-worktree-mode), *withholding* `FORGE_WORKTREES` no longer means "off" — an unset value resolves to the platform default. What keeps the suite host-independent is forge's own test preload (`src/test-setup.ts`), which pins `FORGE_WORKTREES="0"` explicitly; the denylist here removes the leak, and the pin decides the value. This is a **denylist on Forge's own namespace, deliberately not the setup child's allowlist**: it removes exactly the variables Forge is responsible for and cannot break an arbitrary project's suite, which an allowlist over someone else's tests would. It is hygiene in the same sense the setup child's minimal environment is hygiene — it eliminates a source of false verdicts, not a class of attack; see the trust model below. All three Forge-owned host-verification callers get it — the publish-time [integration gate](#integration-publisher), the ordered fan-out's mid-wave D1 gate (FG-584, see [Ordered fanout](#ordered-fanout-dependency-aware-build-execution)), and review-loop host verification — and the last matters twice over because its result is persisted as covering evidence ([Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate](#evidence-reuse-for-the-deterministic-gate-fg-474)), so a false verdict there outlives the run that produced it. Test-harness inputs in the namespace are withheld too, `FORGE_TEST_PRINT_CMD` among them — a no-op today, since it is unset in an orchestrator environment, but a host that ever came to rely on one for extended verification would not get it here.

**The setup contract is structured argv, and Forge never invokes a shell.** The contract is a **sequence of argv arrays**, executed in order, each one a direct `execFile`. The default is exactly `[["npm","ci"]]`.

```
setup     := step | [ step, step, ... ]     # one argv array, or a sequence of them
step      := [ executable, arg, ... ]       # JSON array of strings, non-empty
shorthand := "npm ci"                       # one step, split on whitespace
```

All three of these declare the default: `"npm ci"`, `["npm","ci"]`, `[["npm","ci"]]`. A multi-step contract looks like `[["npm","ci"],["npm","run","build:native"]]` — the steps run in order and the **first failure stops the sequence**, refusing `setup_failed` with that step named. Each element of a step is one argv element passed **verbatim**, so an argument containing spaces (`["npm","ci","--cache","/tmp/a directory with spaces"]`) survives intact, which no whitespace split could manage. The same structure is accepted from `FORGE_HOST_VERIFICATION_SETUP` as JSON.

There is **no `sh -c`, no `bash -c`, and no metacharacter interpretation** anywhere on this path. The bare-string shorthand exists only because `npm ci` is unambiguous; the moment a configured value contains a shell operator (`&&`, `||`, `;`, `|`, a redirect, a backtick, `$()`) or a quote character, its intent is a *shell's* intent, and whitespace-splitting it would silently mis-execute it — `npm ci && npm run build` would run `npm` with the literal arguments `ci`, `&&`, `npm`, `run`, `build`, and the resulting failure would read like a broken project rather than a broken contract. So it is **refused** (`ambiguous_setup_contract`) with a message naming the source and showing the argv shape, instead.

**Trust model — host verification assumes candidate code is not actively malicious.** State this plainly, because the alternative reading has already cost a shipped defect. Install lifecycle scripts **run**: `npm_config_ignore_scripts` is not set, because a native dependency's `preinstall`/`install`/`postinstall` *is* how its bindings get built. Suppressing them was never a security boundary. Moments after readiness returns, Forge runs the candidate-controlled `npm run test:unit` on the host under the operator's general environment, and the candidate controls that script and every test file it names — so suppressing install scripts never prevented hostile candidate code from executing on the host; its only observable effect was that legitimate native dependencies could never work. **Withholding Forge's `FORGE_*` namespace from that child (above) changes nothing here.** It stops Forge's own configuration from reaching code Forge is trying to get an honest answer about; it narrows no attack surface, because the candidate's script still runs on the host, under the operator's own identity, with reach over everything that identity has. Read it as the correctness hygiene it is, never as a step toward containment. An **install-script allowlist was considered and rejected**: package names and lockfile identities are themselves candidate-controlled, native requirements vary by project, it grows into the dependency-policy system this contract deliberately fences out, and it still does not protect the host once verification begins. It must not be presented as if it could. If hostile-candidate isolation ever becomes a requirement, **installation and verification must both move into a sandbox** — nothing short of that is a boundary.

**The two other operator knobs.** Both are host-level, like the setup command — neither is readable from any project checkout.

| Knob | What it does | Default |
|---|---|---|
| `FORGE_HOST_READINESS_SETUP_TIMEOUT_MS` | The ceiling on the **entire setup contract**, in milliseconds — one budget shared by every step, not a per-step allowance. Each step is given whatever the ceiling has left after the steps before it, so a two-step bootstrap must be sized for both steps together. Exceeding it refuses `setup_timed_out` (whose message names the ceiling in seconds, and the step that was running) rather than letting an install hang the run. A value that is not a finite number greater than zero is ignored and the default applies. Bounding the contract rather than each step is deliberate: the lease covering the readiness span is composed from this ceiling, so a per-step ceiling would let an N-step contract outrun it and surface an environment fault as a lane takeover. The readiness lock's bounded wait likewise derives from this ceiling — it is the ceiling plus 60s — so raising the ceiling can never silently make the lock the thing that fails first. | `600000` (10 minutes) |
| `FORGE_HOST_READINESS_REQUIRE_ABI`, or `hostVerificationRequireAbi` in the **host-level** `~/.forge/config.json` | States the ABI the resolved interpreter must have, for a workspace whose own files do not say. It is the highest-precedence source of the requirement, ahead of everything derived from the workspace. The env variable wins over the config key when both are set. | unset — the requirement is derived from the workspace instead |

The requirement the resolved interpreter is checked against is taken from the first of these that applies: `FORGE_HOST_READINESS_REQUIRE_ABI`; `hostVerificationRequireAbi`; the exact Node major the workspace pins in `.nvmrc` or `engines.node` (`24`, `24.x`, `^24` — a *range* like `>=18` is not a pin and does not resolve to an ABI); the ABI recorded by a previous **completed** (`ready`) readiness assertion for this workspace, which is the ABI its installed bindings were actually built under; and finally the range itself, satisfied by membership rather than by ABI equality. A record still in `preparing` — a setup that failed or crashed — supplies no requirement: nothing was installed under that ABI, so it falls through as if the record were absent. When none applies there is no requirement, which is the honest answer for a workspace that pins no Node and has never been prepared.

**Forge never prepares its own checkout.** `forge review-loop --project` defaults to `process.cwd()`, which on a Forge session *is* the live checkout, and `npm ci` deletes `node_modules` before rebuilding it — including the `better-sqlite3` binding the running orchestrator and every concurrent `forge` on the host are loaded from. A workspace overlapping the Forge source root the process is executing from therefore refuses as `self_host_workspace`, **independently of worktree mode and with no acknowledged-override escape hatch**. [FG-612's dispatch guard](#self-host-dispatch-on-a-never-isolated-path) is the weaker one of the pair: it protects agent *writes*, which an isolated workspace does contain, so it stands down when the dispatch actually provisions one and it accepts `FORGE_NO_WORKTREES=1` as an acknowledged override. Neither concession is available here, because no isolation setting makes deleting a live dependency tree safe. (Note the two refuse a self-host `forge review-loop` at different points: this one when the loop reaches local verification, the dispatch guard when green CI evidence carries it past readiness to the reviewer dispatch.)

The refusal is **unconditional and evaluated first**, ahead of every `not_required` arm. That ordering is the guard, not an implementation detail: ranked below them, the common case — a live checkout that already has `node_modules` — short-circuited to `not_required` and never reached the refusal at all, so the destructive outcome was avoided only incidentally, and a *stale* self-host tree was certified as needing nothing rather than refused. Having its own installed `node_modules` therefore does **not** exempt the Forge checkout: the `not_required` arm for an already-provisioned tree Forge did not put there (above) is one of the arms this refusal is ordered ahead of, and it applies to every *other* workspace.

**What this means day to day:** `forge review-loop` run from inside the Forge checkout now stops with a `self_host_workspace` refusal instead of proceeding. That is the intended outcome, and it is not a verdict on the code — no reviewer or fixer ran and no round was consumed. For Forge-on-Forge review-loop runs, point the command at a clone outside the Forge source root: `forge review-loop <ticket-id> --project <clone>`. Everything else about Forge-on-Forge is unchanged — this is a constraint on the *verification workspace*, not on dispatching Forge against itself.

**Binding and invalidation are correctness, not caching.** Readiness is keyed on workspace path, tree identity, lockfile digest, and runtime ABI together, and it is **never inherited by a different candidate**. A changed lockfile invalidates it; a moved-base rebuild gets a fresh `~/.forge/worktrees/publications/<attemptId>-r<rebuild>` path and therefore cannot inherit the previous rebuild's assertion — asserted, not merely relied upon. The verification command set travels in the evidence record for audit, but is deliberately **not** a reuse key. Reuse is a side effect of the binding being exact; the binding does not exist to make things fast.

**Preparation may not move the tree.** The tree Forge publishes must be byte-for-byte the tree that passed the gate (FG-621 AC 6), so preparation is not allowed to alter the candidate git tree or the published commit — the workspace's `git status` is captured before setup and compared after, and a workspace whose status setup *changed* refuses as `workspace_dirtied_by_setup` rather than proceeding. Compared, not asserted absolutely clean: the review-loop's dirty-tree arm legitimately starts dirty, and what the invariant requires is that preparation does not **move** the tree, not that the tree was clean to begin with. For an already-clean workspace — every publication candidate — "unchanged" *is* "still porcelain-clean". Preparation likewise never mutates the live `main` checkout, its shared native bindings, the reviewed source or lockfile, or another clone's dependency tree as remediation.

**`~/.forge/host-readiness/` is a separate keyspace from FG-376's `~/.forge/dependency-cache/`, deliberately.** It reuses that store's discipline — temp-then-rename atomic markers, a narrow lock, liveness plus a bounded timeout backstop — by reading it, and is prohibited from reading or writing any FG-376 marker, volume or lock. The reason is an asymmetry in the keys: the container key is **ABI-free by design**, because the image pins the interpreter, while the host key **must** include the runtime and ABI, because the host interpreter varies between machines and between `.nvmrc` switches. Writing FG-376's key from the host would make container provisioning skip an install against an empty volume — a readiness lie in the other direction.

**Operator surfaces.** Readiness emits `host_readiness.*` events carrying the evidence record on success and the refusal payload on failure, and the review loop's `review_loop.verification_finished` payload gains `readiness: { outcome, reason? } | null`, where `outcome` is `prepared` | `reused` | `refused` | `not_required`. Older events carry no such field and render exactly as before. Both `host_readiness.*` events reach the dashboard task timeline even though the review loop emits them run-scoped with no `task_id`. The dashboard renders a refusal with its **own badge** — `status-environment_unavailable`, amber and dashed, labelled `… · environment` — not the red of a failed verification, plus an environment detail line naming the reason and stating that no reviewer or fixer ran and no round was consumed. The badge is where an operator scans; rendering a refusal in the failure badge would reproduce this very misclassification on the operator surface. Separately, a failed round-entry verification finding now names the command actually run, its tier, and a stderr tail, instead of the step name followed by an empty colon (the round-entry twin of the discarded-step-detail defect FG-625 owns on the post-fixer path).

**Deliberately out of scope.** *FG-627* owns Docker nested-volume mount mechanics for **container** verification — adjacent, merged, and not merged into this. *FG-376*'s provisioning internals (cache key, volume naming, lock, markers, prune) are read-only reference here; its `lockfileHash`/`safeLockfileHash` are imported, never forked. *FG-555* owns the runtime/environment contract for unattended verification — coordinated with, not contradicted. *FG-357 / FG-425* are the second **consumer**: readiness is integrated ahead of the gate they already run, and neither is redesigned. Also out of scope: package-manager unification, any dependency-system redesign, review-policy or round-limit changes, the orchestrator-policy surface, and the other Forge-owned host verification paths — `runAndRecordHostVerification` (`src/campaign/reconcile-collect.ts`) and `reconcile-outofband-collect`, which run against the live checkout rather than a freshly-created workspace and are not covered by this contract.

## Readiness

A mechanical preflight that classifies a backlog item as ready for implementation before any work starts. Run via `forge readiness <ticket-id> [--project <dir>] [--json]`. Read-only — does not write to the project.

**Outcomes:**

| Outcome | Criteria |
|---|---|
| `ready` | Ticket has a `## Problem` section, a `## Goal` section (or `## Expected behavior`), and an `## Acceptance Criteria` section with at least one bullet point. |
| `needs_refinement` | One or more of the above sections is missing, or Acceptance Criteria exists but has no bullet points. The command emits a concrete `refinementProposal` rather than leaving the operator to guess what to add. |
| `blocked` | Ticket `status` is `blocked`. |
| `exploratory` | Ticket type is `idea`, or the title or body contains any of: spike, research, explore, exploratory, exploration, investigation. Lighter criteria apply — at least one of Problem or Goal must be present; Acceptance Criteria are not required.
