If both Problem and Goal are absent the outcome is still `exploratory`, but `gaps` and `refinementProposal` are populated with guidance. |

This is a **cheap structural checklist** — it checks whether the required sections exist and contain content. It is not an LLM reviewer and does not assess whether the content is correct or complete. The mechanical done audit — which checks shipping evidence such as git state, closed commit, and host verification — is a separate evaluator; see [Done audit (mechanical)](#done-audit-mechanical) below. Semantic quality review is the Shipping Reviewer (FG-384) — a separate opt-in agent; see [Shipping Reviewer](#shipping-reviewer) below.

**Scope boundary:** the evaluator checks structural completeness only. Whether the latest operator instruction has been reflected in the ticket body is the **orchestrator's responsibility** — the preflight has no access to the operator conversation and cannot verify that reconciliation.

With `--json` the output is a stable object:

```json
{
  "ticketId": "FG-42",
  "outcome": "needs_refinement",
  "gaps": ["Missing Goal section (or Expected behavior)", "Acceptance Criteria section has no bullet points"],
  "refinementProposal": "Add a ## Goal section (or ## Expected behavior) describing what success looks like. Add an ## Acceptance Criteria section with testable bullet points."
}
```

`refinementProposal` is `null` when outcome is `ready`, or when outcome is `exploratory` and at least one of Problem or Goal is present. For `blocked` and `needs_refinement` it is always non-null; for `exploratory` with both sections absent it is also non-null.

**Campaign integration (FG-413).** The readiness evaluator is wired into the campaign runner as a pre-dispatch gate. Before dispatching each pending item the runner evaluates the ticket; if the outcome is `needs_refinement` or `blocked`, the item is held without dispatching any implementation work — no engineer tokens are spent on an unready ticket. The item is recorded with `outcome: held`, `blockerKind: readiness`, `reason: "held because not ready: <gaps>"`, and `requestedHumanAction: "refine <ticketId> then resume — <refinementProposal>"`. Tickets that evaluate as `ready` or `exploratory` proceed to dispatch normally.

On `forge campaign resume`, items held with `blockerKind: readiness` are re-evaluated against the **current** ticket body. A ticket refined to `ready` or `exploratory` is released and dispatched; a ticket still not ready stays held and the campaign stays paused.

Example: `forge readiness FG-42` on a story that has a Problem section but no Acceptance Criteria returns `outcome: needs_refinement` with a `refinementProposal` explaining what to add.

## Done audit (mechanical)

A pure mechanical evaluator that checks whether a campaign item can truthfully be treated as shipped and done. Run automatically by `forge campaign report` for each campaign item (best-effort; `doneAuditState` is `null` only if collection throws or `projectDir` is missing). Not the LLM Shipping Reviewer (FG-384) — makes no subjective judgment; all evidence arrives as structured input.

**Result shape:**

```json
{
  "outcome": "pass" | "fail" | "unknown",
  "checks": [{ "name": "...", "status": "pass" | "fail" | "unknown", "detail": "..." }],
  "gaps": ["..."],
  "requestedAction": "..." | null
}
```

**Checks** (run in this order):

| Check | What it verifies |
|---|---|
| `ticket_closed` | Ticket `status` is `done` |
| `closed_commit_present` | Ticket has a `closed_commit` field |
| `commit_exists` | The closed commit exists in the repository |
| `clean_git` | Working tree is clean, ignoring host-local operational noise (`git status --porcelain` empty after filtering out `backlog/notes.md` and `.forge-scratch/`) |
| `pushed` | The closed commit is reachable from a remote branch |
| `container_verification` | Sum of `tests_run` across the campaign item's run tasks (INFORMATIONAL — excluded from outcome aggregation) |
| `host_verification` | Required gate passed and recorded — a real host run, or a green required CI check reused as evidence (FG-474) |
| `deferral_linked` | If the ticket body declares deferred scope, a follow-up ticket is linked |

**Aggregation semantics.** `container_verification` is informational — present in `checks` but excluded from outcome aggregation. All other checks are required.

- `pass` — every required check is `pass`
- `fail` — at least one required check is a definite `fail`
- `unknown` — no required check is `fail`, but at least one required check is `unknown`

Missing evidence is always `unknown`, never `pass`. The `pushed` check follows this same principle when no remote is configured: `git remote` is checked first; if no remote exists, `pushed` resolves to `unknown` (not `fail`) — the commit's push status is unknowable without a remote. `container_verification` is populated from the campaign item's run task results — it sums `tests_run` across those tasks: `pass` when the sum is greater than zero, `fail` when the sum is exactly zero (zero container tests recorded), and `unknown` when the item has no run or no task recorded a numeric `tests_run`. It is informational and does **not** satisfy `host_verification`. `host_verification` resolves from the host-verification store by applying the same **per-member passing-row** aggregation as `forge campaign reconcile` (FG-453, aligned with FG-440) to **every member of the derived gate list** — `deriveRequiredGateList(projectDir, requiredGate)`, shared with the reconcile collectors via `resolveGateCoverage`/`evaluateGateListCoverage` (`src/campaign/reconcile-collect.ts`); see [Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate](#evidence-reuse-for-the-deterministic-gate-fg-474) below for how the list is derived (single-gate/custom-gate projects derive a one-element list, so behavior there is unchanged). For each list member: `pass` when one or more covering rows exist for it and *at least one* of them has `exit_code 0`, even if earlier covering rows for that member failed — a covering pass ships that member regardless of earlier covering failures; `fail` when covering rows exist for it but *none* of them passed; `unknown` when no covering row exists for it. The overall check is `pass` when *every* list member resolves `pass`, **or** when the list's primary (first) member has a `source: 'ci'` passing row — `runAndRecordHostVerification` only ever writes `source: 'ci'` when every required CI job is green, so a ci-sourced primary pass is whole-workflow evidence covering the rest of the list on its own; `fail` when any member resolves `fail` and the ci shortcut doesn't apply — a real, provable gap on one member is never laundered into a pass by another member's success (FG-453); otherwise `unknown` when no member has a proven failure but at least one is still uncovered. Earlier covering failures are not discarded when a later covering row passes — they're retained as visible audit history: the check's `detail` line appends `earlier_covering_failures: N` (summed across list members) when the verdict is pass-with-prior-failures. Matching requires exact `ticketId`, `projectDir`, and `gateName` (each list member matched under its own gate name) **and a recorded `command` equal to that same gate-list member** (FG-510), plus commit **coverage** rather than exact equality — `closedCommit` must be a git ancestor of a row's tested commit, and that tested commit must itself be reachable on the configured base branch (the gate always runs at `projectDir`'s current HEAD, never a checkout of `closedCommit`, so exact-sha matching would never find a real capture); evidence for a different gate, project, or ticket, a row whose `command` is not the gate it names, or a commit that fails either half of the coverage check, is treated as absent for that member. The `command`-equality half is what makes a row recorded as `--command "echo ok" --gate "npm run test:all"` inert: it is not a covering row **at all**, so it neither passes its member nor counts as a covering failure, and that member stays `unknown` exactly as if the row had never been written (fail-closed). This is the same command-equality check the reuse predicate `findCoveringGateEvidence` has always applied, so reconcile's pre-check, the out-of-band lane, done-audit, and review-loop reuse now share one definition of a covering row. A covering row's `source` (`'host'`, the default, or `'ci'` — FG-474) does not change per-member aggregation: a CI-sourced passing row satisfies its member exactly like a host-run one (and, via the ci shortcut above, a ci-sourced primary-member pass satisfies the whole list on its own). See [Recording host-verification evidence](#recording-host-verification-evidence) below and [Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate](#evidence-reuse-for-the-deterministic-gate-fg-474) for how `'ci'` rows get written in the first place.

**Effective scope of a single row.** Coverage matching means one recorded row vouches for more than the single commit it names: within the same `ticketId` + `projectDir` + `gateName` — and, since FG-510, only when the row's `command` equals that gate — it satisfies *every* `closedCommit` that is an ancestor of the row's tested commit and reachable on the base branch — its effective trust scope is a commit **range**, not a single sha. This is intentional, not a gap: the gate always runs at `projectDir`'s current HEAD, never a checkout of `closedCommit`, so a row could never cover an exact-sha match in the first place. But it's a caveat an operator should keep in mind when recording evidence manually with `forge record-host-verification` — a row recorded against one ticket's commit can also silently satisfy an earlier `closedCommit` for the same ticket/project/gate, without a second `record-host-verification` call.

**`requestedAction`** is `null` only when `outcome: pass`. Otherwise it names the concrete operator step(s) for each non-pass required check, joined with `"; "` — for example: `"run host typecheck + full suite, record the result, then re-audit"`, `"commit or revert the working tree"`, `"push <commit>"` (or `"no remote configured; push/PR unavailable"` when the `pushed` check is `unknown` due to no remote being configured), `"file a follow-up ticket and link it"`.

Example: `forge campaign report camp-abc123 --json` — the `doneAuditState` field on each item contains the result of running all eight checks against that item's ticket and git state.

### Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate (FG-474)

`host_verifications` rows carry two more columns beyond ticket/project/commit/gate/command/exit-code: `source` (`'host'`, the default — a real host command execution; or `'ci'` — sourced from a green required CI check instead) and `ci_url` (the CI check's details URL, set only for `source: 'ci'` rows). This is a **separate, narrower** lookup from the ancestry-based `host_verification` coverage described above — that coverage question is "has *any* row, at *any* covered commit, satisfied this ticket's shipped evidence"; this one is "has the canonical deterministic gate already run, for real, against *this exact* commit" — asked by callers deciding whether to re-run the gate at all, before they'd otherwise execute it.

**"The gate" is a derived list, not always one command (FG-500).** `deriveRequiredGateList(projectDir, requiredGate)` (`src/store/host-verifications.ts`) is the single source of truth every consumer below shares for what counts as covering evidence: `[requiredGate]`, unless `requiredGate` is still the project default (`npm run test:all`) **and** the project's `package.json` defines a `test:extended` script — in which case the extended tier joins the list, `[requiredGate, "npm run test:extended"]`. A project with a custom `requiredHostGate`, or no `test:extended` script, still derives a one-element list, so single-gate/custom-gate projects behave exactly as before.

`findCoveringGateEvidence(ticketId, projectDir, sha, command)` (`src/store/host-verifications.ts`) answers, for an exact `(projectDir, sha, command)` pair, whether covering evidence already exists for **every member** of `deriveRequiredGateList(projectDir, command)` — not `command` alone. Command match per member is **exact** — every row is written with one canonical command string as both `gate_name` and `command`, so a row recorded under a narrower command (e.g. `npm run test` vs. the required `npm run test:all`) never satisfies its member. It checks, in order:

1. an existing **passing** `host_verifications` row, at the exact sha, for every list member — stops at the first uncovered member, so a fast-tier-only row can never stand in for a project's full tiered set;
2. else, only once this project's own CI is verified to actually run the required command **and** every job in its matched CI workflow is green at the exact sha: the required CI check (`CI / test`) is still the pairing that proves the command is backed by real CI content, but a green paired job alone is no longer sufficient — any completed failure on that sha, on any job in the matched workflow, disqualifies it outright (a same-sha pass/fail disagreement across re-runs is treated as a flake signal, never covering evidence), and a still-running or check-run-absent sibling job is likewise not covering, never a synthetic pass. This whole-workflow-green evidence covers every list member at once (it already proves every job passed), so no separate CI-sourced row is needed per extended-tier member.

Step 2's CI check is never trusted on its name alone. Because forge is host-global, `opts.projectDir` is an arbitrary managed project — a green `CI / test` there proves nothing about what that project's own workflow actually runs (the FG-419 gate_name-spoofing vector, narrowed but not closed by exact-command matching alone). `findCoveringGateEvidence` closes this by calling `projectCiRunsCommand(projectDir, checkContext, command)` (`src/store/host-verifications.ts`) **before** the check-runs provider is ever consulted: it reads `<projectDir>/.github/workflows/*.y{a,}ml` fresh, finds the workflow/job named by `checkContext` (`"CI / test"` → workflow `"CI"`, job `"test"`), and returns `true` only if one of that job's steps has a `run:` string that (trimmed) exactly equals `command`. A missing workflows dir, unparseable YAML, or no exactly-matching run step all fail closed — the check-runs provider is never called, so a same-named check on a project whose CI doesn't actually run the required command can never cover.

**Whole-workflow requirement (FG-495 review finding).** Pairing only proves that *one* job of the matched workflow runs the required command — it does not prove the whole gate passed if a sibling job is red, pending, or has no check run at all at that sha. Post-FG-495, forge's own CI workflow (and any project that adopts the same split) spreads the suite across sibling required jobs, e.g. `test` + `test-extended`; a green `CI / test` no longer means the gate is covered if `test-extended` is red or hasn't reported at that sha. `findCoveringGateEvidence` closes this with `projectCiWorkflowJobIds(projectDir, workflowName)` (`src/store/host-verifications.ts`), which shares the same workflow-YAML parsing as `projectCiRunsCommand` to enumerate every job id defined under the matched workflow. Before minting `source: 'ci'` evidence, every enumerated job id must show a green check run at the exact sha — a red, pending, or absent sibling job means no CI coverage. Enumeration itself fails closed: a missing workflows dir, no workflow matching `checkContext`'s workflow name, or a workflow with no jobs all make `projectCiWorkflowJobIds` return `null`, and `findCoveringGateEvidence` returns `null` in turn without the check-runs provider ever being consulted.

**Fails closed:** no evidence, a different sha, a different command, an uncovered member of the derived gate list, a project whose own CI content doesn't demonstrably run the required command, a job-enumeration failure, a red/pending/absent sibling job in the matched workflow, or a pending/failing/disagreeing check all return nothing, so the caller falls back to actually running the gate — never a synthetic pass.

Two consumers consult this before running the deterministic suite themselves:

- **`forge review-loop`'s verification phase** (`buildReviewLoopDeps.verify` in `src/cli/commands/review-loop.ts`) — only when the worktree is clean (a dirty tree never reuses, since a dirty diff under review wouldn't match what any recorded/CI evidence actually covers; a dirty tree runs `runVerification` locally right away). On a clean tree with no covering evidence yet, the loop does **not** immediately duplicate CI by running locally (FG-501): it probes the required CI gate's live status (`probeCiGateStatus`, `src/store/host-verifications.ts`) under the same fail-closed pairing/whole-workflow-green trust chain as evidence lookup. Pending checks are polled (default every 30s, up to a 20-minute timeout — `FORGE_CI_POLL_SECONDS`/`FORGE_CI_WAIT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`), logging the sha, check contexts, state, elapsed time, and URL on every poll; once the checks go green it re-checks for covering evidence and reuses it (`ciOutcome: "reused_after_wait"`). A failing required check stops the loop directly as verification failed, citing the failing check's name and URL (`ciOutcome: "ci_failed"`) — no local run. Only a genuinely unavailable/unqueryable CI setup, or a wait that times out, falls back to a real local run (`ciOutcome: "local_fallback"`, with the operator-facing reason recorded). That fallback runs a fast-tier-only `typecheck` + `test` by default — `test:extended` is delegated to CI — restoring the full tier (equivalent to `scriptsForVerification()`, itself still gated by `deriveRequiredGateList` per FG-500) only when the operator passes `--local-extended`. Either way, the run note records what happened: reused evidence, the failing CI check, or which local tier ran and why.
- **`forge campaign reconcile`'s host-verification capture** (`runAndRecordHostVerification` in `src/campaign/reconcile-collect.ts`) — see [Automatic host-gate capture](#reconcile) below; since FG-500 its real-exec fallback runs and records every member of the derived gate list, not `requiredHostGate` alone.

A third consumer applies the same derived list but skips this exact-sha reuse check entirely: `forge campaign report`'s done-audit collector (`src/done-audit/collect.ts`) resolves the ancestry-based `host_verification` coverage described in [Done audit (mechanical)](#done-audit-mechanical) independently for **every** list member — a pass requires either a covering passing row for every member, or a single ci-sourced passing row on the list's primary (first) member, since whole-workflow CI evidence already proves every job and would otherwise double-charge CI once per list member.

### Recording host-verification evidence

`forge record-host-verification --ticket <id> --project-dir <path> --commit <sha> --command <cmd> --exit-code <n>` records a real host-command result in the host-verification store (`~/.forge/forge.db`). The collector reads this store when assembling done-audit input for `forge campaign report`. Because matching is ancestry-based, the row you record here covers more than just `--commit` — see [Effective scope of a single row](#done-audit-mechanical) above.

Required flags:

| Flag | Description |
|---|---|
| `--ticket <ticketId>` | Ticket ID (e.g. `FG-419`) |
| `--project-dir <path>` | Absolute path to the project directory |
| `--commit <sha>` | Commit SHA that was verified — typically the tested HEAD; it satisfies a ticket's `closedCommit` when `closedCommit` is an ancestor of it and it is reachable on the base branch (exact match not required) |
| `--command <cmd>` | The exact command that was run |
| `--exit-code <n>` | Exit code of the command (`0` = pass, non-zero = fail) |

Optional: `--gate <name>` overrides the `gate_name` recorded for this evidence row; when omitted, `gate_name` defaults to the `--command` value. It is an **audit label only** — since FG-510 it does not make the row satisfy the gate it names (see **`--gate` labels a row; it never covers a gate** below). `--run-id <id>` associates the record with a forge run.

**Gate labeling and `host_verification`.** Two distinct defaults — do not conflate them. The **CLI `--gate` default** is the `--command` string. The **collector's required host gate default** is `"npm run test:all"` (per-project override via `.forge/config.json` `requiredHostGate`); `host_verification` passes only when rows whose `gate_name` *and* `command` are both a given member cover every member of `deriveRequiredGateList(projectDir, requiredGate)` (`src/store/host-verifications.ts`) — see [Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate](#evidence-reuse-for-the-deterministic-gate-fg-474) above for how that list is derived. On a project with a custom `requiredHostGate`, or no `test:extended` script in `package.json`, the derived list has a single member and behavior is unchanged: `--command "npm run test:all"` without `--gate` records `gate_name = "npm run test:all"` and alone satisfies `host_verification`. On a **tiered** project — the default gate plus a `package.json` `test:extended` script — the derived list has two members, `["npm run test:all", "npm run test:extended"]` (FG-500); a single fast-tier row no longer satisfies `host_verification` by itself. The operator must also record a passing `--command "npm run test:extended"` row — or leave both to `forge campaign reconcile`'s real-exec capture, or to a whole-workflow-green CI check, which covers the full list at once (see [Evidence reuse for the deterministic gate](#evidence-reuse-for-the-deterministic-gate-fg-474)). Until every list member is covered, `host_verification` resolves to `unknown`, not `pass` (`src/done-audit/collect.ts`). A weaker command (e.g. `--command "npm run typecheck"`) without `--gate` records `gate_name = "npm run typecheck"` — supporting evidence only, which does not satisfy `host_verification`.

**`--gate` labels a row; it never covers a gate (FG-510).** Passing `--gate "npm run test:all"` alongside a different `--command` does *not* make the row satisfy the required gate. A covering row must have `command` equal to the gate-list member it covers, so a labeled-but-mismatched row is inert for coverage: it neither passes the member nor counts as a covering failure, and `host_verification` stays `unknown` rather than `pass` — the same result as if the row had never been recorded (fail-closed). This closes the labeling half of the FG-419 spoofing vector: earlier the required gate could be satisfied by *labeling* any command as it, which is exactly the claim an evidence row must not be able to make about work it never did. The only way to cover the required gate is to run it. The row itself still persists with both columns and stays visible in evidence views — the FG-419 glass-box audit trail is unchanged; the row is simply not evidence *for that gate*. Canonical rows, where `command` and `gate_name` are the same string, are unaffected — that covers every row forge writes automatically (`forge campaign reconcile`'s real-exec capture, its CI-sourced rows, and review-loop evidence reuse all set `command == gate_name`), plus any `--command "npm run test:all"` recorded without `--gate`. `forge record-host-verification --gate` remains the one writer that can produce a mismatched pair, and that pair is precisely the inert row described above.

**Trust model.** Evidence is an audit trail in a trusted-operator context, not tamper-proof: `--command` and `--exit-code` record what was run and what it returned rather than an unverifiable bare assertion. The threat boundary is the operator — they control the host and could supply any exit code, but the recorder requires an explicit real-command invocation.

**Matching semantics.** Every consumer of this store (`forge campaign reconcile`'s shape-1 and shape-2 lanes, and `forge campaign report`'s done-audit `host_verification` check) applies the same rule: `ticketId`, `projectDir`, and `gateName` must match exactly, the row's `command` must equal the gate it is matched against (FG-510), and the commit dimension is **coverage**, not exact equality — the ticket's `closedCommit` must be a git ancestor of a row's recorded commit, and that recorded commit must itself be reachable on the configured base branch. `projectDir` is canonicalized (`path.resolve`) on both insert and lookup (FG-431), so a row recorded with an equivalent-but-not-identical path string (relative vs. absolute, a trailing slash) is still found — this closes matching, it does not loosen it: `ticketId`, `gateName`, and `commitSha` still require exact equality. This lets a row recorded against a later base-branch commit still satisfy an earlier `closedCommit`, since the gate always runs at `projectDir`'s current HEAD, never a checkout of `closedCommit` itself. Evidence for a different gate, project, or ticket, a row whose `command` is not the gate it names, or a commit that fails either half of the coverage check, does not satisfy `host_verification` — stale, mislabeled, or non-covering evidence resolves to `unknown`, never `pass`.

Example: after `npm run test:all` exits 0 on commit `abc1234` for ticket `FG-419`:

```shell
forge record-host-verification \
  --ticket FG-419 \
  --project-dir /code/forge \
  --commit abc1234 \
  --command "npm run test:all" \
  --exit-code 0
```

On a tiered project (`test:extended` present in `package.json`, `requiredHostGate` still the default), this single call is not enough — repeat it with `--command "npm run test:extended"` at the same commit to cover the rest of the derived gate list, or skip manual recording for the extended tier and let `forge campaign reconcile` or a whole-workflow-green CI run capture it.

## Review ledger

The durable record of what forge **decided** about a candidate's review findings, as distinct from a [Verdict](#verdict), which records what a reviewer **reported**. Two tables (`reviews`, `review_findings`) plus four lifecycle events; the rows carry current state and the events carry how it got there, because a row alone cannot answer "when did this become a `rejected_premise` and who said so" and an event stream alone cannot answer "what is open right now" without a replay. Shipped by FG-638 as Change 1 of `docs/prds/evidence-led-review-lifecycle.md`. Full column contract: [SCHEMA-CONTRACT](SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md#reviews--review_findings-tables-fg-638-dashboard-read-path).

**The `verdicts` table is immutable provenance.** Ingestion reads it and records what it read; it never edits or deletes a verdict row. A ledger finding points back at the verdicts that produced it, and every one of them is kept — the source count is provenance, never an "independent review count".

**Forge assigns the ids.** A review is `review-<suffix>`; a finding is `<review-id>/RF-n` globally, and `RF-n` is what an operator types (bare, it is only unambiguous within one review, so a bare ref matching several reviews reports the ambiguity rather than picking one). A reviewing model may arrive carrying an id it invented for itself; that id is preserved as provenance inside the finding's source record and is never the finding's identity.

**Review mode: declared by the workflow, stamped on the run.** Every run carries `review_mode` — `legacy_verdict` (the verdict/[blocked-by-red](#blocked-by-red) model), `legacy_review_loop`, or `evidence_led` — `NOT NULL` with a `legacy_verdict` default, so a run created before the ledger existed resolves to the legacy model rather than to nothing. Since FG-640 the value is **declared in the workflow file** and stamped onto the run row at creation by `startRun`; the run row is the durable per-run record and the reconciliation anchor below, and which model actually settles a gated step is read from the **workflow** — see [`review_disposition` gate](#review_disposition-gate). Exactly one mode per run: a run still carrying that default and owning no review **adopts** its first review's mode atomically at insert (that is how an evidence-led review opens on a run whose workflow never declared one), and a run already marked **refuses** a conflicting mode rather than letting the run and its ledger disagree. A review's own `review_mode` is a copy of its run's, written from the run's authoritative value rather than from what the caller passed.

**Disposition is the decision; resolution is the proof.** Every ingested finding starts `untriaged` and must receive exactly one disposition — `fix_now`, `accepted_risk`, `deferred`, `rejected_premise`, `duplicate`, or `architecture_question` — recorded with who decided, when, against which candidate sha, and why. `resolution` is a separate field answering whether an accepted fix is *proven complete*; collapsing the two is how "we decided to fix it" silently reads as "it is fixed". A review's **unsettled count** is therefore not just its untriaged findings: an open `architecture_question`, and a `fix_now` whose resolution is not `resolved`, are unsettled too.

Each disposition carries its own preconditions, enforced at the write and refusing with nothing written:

- **All** require `--rationale`. A disposition that does not record why is not a decision.
- `fix_now` and `architecture_question` require nothing further.
- `accepted_risk` requires `--operator` when accepting the risk would move a stated threat model, protected invariant, acceptance criterion, security promise, or data-integrity guarantee. Whether it does is **derived mechanically from the finding's own durable fields** — it cites an invariant or an acceptance criterion, arrived through the `security` risk lens, or is typed `data_integrity` — never from a caller-supplied claim that the decision is routine.
- `rejected_premise` requires candidate-bound disproving evidence, not a rationale alone: `--evidence <json> --evidence-kind <kind>`, and the review must have a candidate sha to bind it to. The three kinds are **structural**, each defined by the fields that make it proof rather than restated opinion — `replayed_command` `{command, output}`, `deterministic_reproduction` `{reproduction, result}`, `anchored_contradiction` `{file, line, fact}` (`line` is a line number, the rest non-empty text). What is checked here is that the payload parses and carries every field its kind is defined by: a replay without its output is only a claim that it was run. Whether the output actually contradicts *this* finding is a semantic judgement and belongs to the FG-639 coordinator. What is stored is the structured payload plus its kind and candidate sha, so a later reader never re-guesses what the text meant.
- `deferred` requires `--ticket` naming the durable destination the finding survives in. A destination the store already knows is accepted; creating a *new* one is an operator decision and needs `--operator`.
- `duplicate` requires `--duplicate-of <finding-id>` citing a canonical finding that exists, is not itself, and belongs to the same review. The canonical row then **absorbs** the duplicate's sources, which is the whole reason closing a duplicate is safe: no reviewer's report is lost by closing it.

`decided_by` is `operator` or `orchestrator`. Under the single-user trust model the flagged invocation *is* the operator act, exactly as a `forge gate` human decision is — an explicit confirmation, **not** authenticated identity (the FG-597 caveat).

**Read surfaces.** `forge review show <review-id> [--json]` renders one review's summary and findings. `forge show <run-id>` gains a `review:` line naming the run's mode and, when the run owns ledger reviews, a `Review ledger:` block per review with its state, candidate sha, finding counts by disposition, and the `forge review show` command to drill in — distinct from the AWN-5 block above it, which clusters raw verdict findings rather than decisions about them. The dashboard's **reviews** tab renders the same summary-plus-findings shape read-only, including each review's single next required action; disposition controls stay on the CLI until that surface is proven. The human and `--json` CLI renders are held to each other by an exhaustive field classification, so a field wired into one and not the other fails a test rather than quietly giving an operator two different answers; the dashboard, though, computes its own copy of the projection in its own SQL and is **not** joined to the CLI's, so [SCHEMA-CONTRACT](SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md#reviews--review_findings-tables-fg-638-dashboard-read-path)'s standing dashboard drift caveat applies here too. One acknowledged gap in both surfaces today: a finding's own `evidence` and `hypothesis` fields are carried by `forge review show --json` and rendered by nothing. A dashboard pointed at a store whose last *writable* open predates FG-638 has no ledger tables at all — `/api/reviews` reports that by name (`no such table: reviews`) with an empty ledger and keeps the page up, because a read-only open never migrates a store it has no authority over.

**What each change added, and where the lifecycle stands.** FG-638 persisted the 11 states (`confirming_contract`, `discovering`, `awaiting_disposition`, `fixing`, `documenting`, `verifying`, `rechecking`, `shipping_review`, `settled`, `blocked_environment`, `failed`) and emitted an event per transition, but nothing moved between them. FG-639 shipped the stage machine and the `forge review start` / `forge review continue` verbs that drive it, as a pilot: no workflow was migrated and gate authority had not moved. FG-640 (Change 3) closed both gaps. The `feature` workflow declares `review_mode: evidence_led`, its six build reds are advisory, and its build gate is settled by this ledger through the [`review_disposition` gate](#review_disposition-gate) rather than by [verdict](#verdict) aggregation. The interim Change-0 policy, which asked an orchestrator to keep an ephemeral finding ledger in its own session notes, is **retired**: the ledger is these tables. Unmigrated workflows are untouched — they keep verdict aggregation and [blocked_by_red](#blocked-by-red) exactly as before.

**Forge still never opens a review of its own accord**, on a migrated workflow or otherwise: `forge review start` is the only thing that inserts a row. What the migration changed is that on `feature` the ledger is no longer optional — the build step parks at `awaiting_gate` and the gate refuses `review_absent` until an operator opens one with `forge review start <ticket-id> --run <run-id>`. An absent ledger is unestablished evidence, not a clean one.

Example: a review with two findings — `RF-1` dispositioned `fix_now` but not yet resolved, `RF-2` dispositioned `duplicate` of `RF-1` — reports `dispositions: duplicate 1, fix_now 1`, `resolutions: unresolved 2`, and `unsettled findings: 1`, and `RF-1` now carries both findings' sources.

## Review coordinator

The stage machine that drives a [review ledger](#review-ledger) row through the evidence-led lifecycle, shipped by FG-639 as Change 2 of `docs/prds/evidence-led-review-lifecycle.md` and taken off pilot by FG-640 (Change 3). It is driven through the explicit `forge review start` / `forge review continue` verbs; what FG-640 added around it is that the `feature` workflow is migrated, so the ledger the coordinator maintains is what settles that workflow's build gate ([`review_disposition` gate](#review_disposition-gate)), and that [`forge review-loop`](#review-loop-reviewer) is deprecated in its favour. FG-649 moved two authorities into it: the coordinator now **commits the fix cycle** and records the post-fix candidate itself, and the **review row** — not cwd — names the workspace its stages act on. FG-655 finished the first of those, giving the **docs stage the same commit authority**: Stage 6 commits the documentation-maintainer's declared files and advances the candidate to the commit it authored, so neither of the two candidate-moving stages reads HEAD any more.

```shell
forge review start <ticket-id> --contract <file> [--project <dir>] [--run <run-id>] [--since <sha>] [--evaluated-no-drift <statement>] [--shard-budget <n>]
forge review continue <review-id> [--project <dir>] [--dry-run] [--all] [--acceptance <file>] [--docs-closeout <file>] [--retry-shard <lens:index>] [--shard-budget <n>]
forge review show <review-id> [--json]
forge review accept-lens <review-id> <lens> --operator --missing-evidence <text> --rationale <text> [--shard <n>]
```

**The contract is an input, never a reconstruction.** `--contract <file>` is **required** — without it `start` refuses and writes nothing, because the review contract is approved by the plan gate and is not something to infer from prompts after the fact. The JSON must carry all six fields (`threat_model`, `protected_invariants`, `acceptance_refs`, `risk_lenses`, `non_goals`, `lens_scopes`); anything else is a refusal with the missing field named. `risk_lenses` draws on a **fixed five-name vocabulary** — `wide`, `narrow`, `frontend`, `backend`, `security` — each mapping to one red role (`red-wide`, `red-narrow`, `red-frontend`, `red-backend`, `red-security`). Five names and a role map are deliberately the whole mechanism: the PRD forbids shipping a general conditional-workflow language to express lens selection.

**`lens_scopes` says which paths each selected lens owns (FG-689).** It is a map from a selected lens to a non-empty list of **authored** path patterns — a literal repo-relative path, or a directory prefix ending in `/`:

```json
"risk_lenses": ["backend", "security"],
"lens_scopes": {
  "backend": ["src/store/", "src/v2/review-run.ts"],
  "security": ["src/util/creds.ts", "src/v2/review-gate.ts"]
}
```

It exists so a reviewer can be handed its own risk surface rather than the whole diff, which is what makes a large change reviewable at all. Every selected lens must have an entry and no entry may name a lens the contract did not select; either way round is a refusal by name. **Overlap between lenses is allowed and expected** — two reviewers looking at one file is coverage, not a conflict.

Three things it deliberately is not:

- **It is not a path classifier.** Forge never derives a scope from a filename, a directory name or an extension. The direction of inference is the whole point: an authored scope is a human saying "security owns these paths"; a classifier would be forge saying "this path looks like security". Only the first is a contract.
- **There is no negation or exclusion form.** A pattern beginning `!` is refused. An exclusion list ("everything under `src/` except the generated bits") is a path classifier by another name, so generated and vendored artifacts get an owner like any other path, and the cost in reviewer attention is accepted.
- **It is not narrowable by the coordinator.** A scope may be **widened** with recorded diff evidence, following the same rule that lets the coordinator add a lens. **Narrowing** one — removing a pattern, or dropping a lens's entry — returns to the contract's approving authority, because narrowing a scope to nothing removes a lens without saying so.

**`lens_scopes` is change-controlled per scope, which is a third rule (FG-689).** Every contract field belongs to exactly one change-control class, and the classes differ in which direction the coordinator may move the field on its own:

| Class | Fields | What the coordinator may do alone |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `boundary` | `threat_model`, `protected_invariants`, `acceptance_refs`, `non_goals` | **Nothing.** Any change, in either direction, returns to the approving authority |
| `lens_list` | `risk_lenses` | **Add** a lens with a recorded reason and non-empty diff evidence. Removing one returns to the approving authority. Whole-list granularity |
| `lens_scopes` | `lens_scopes` | **Widen** a lens's scope with a recorded reason and diff evidence. **Narrowing** returns to the approving authority — **per lens**, not per contract |

The per-lens granularity is what neither of the other two classes can express: `security` may gain a path in the same proposal where `backend` loses one, and a rule that answers per contract cannot say that. A scope that both gained and lost patterns is **neither** — it is an unrecorded rewrite, and it is refused by name stating the lens, what it added and what it removed, rather than being rounded to whichever half forge could justify. Re-propose the addition as a widening with its evidence and take the removal to the approving authority.

Two consequences worth stating plainly. A widening that arrives **unclaimed** — the scope grew but no `--add-lens` names that lens — is refused for being unevidenced, exactly as an unclaimed added lens is; broadening what forge reviews is autonomous, but never silent. And **narrowing a scope to nothing is the one that matters most**: it removes a lens without ever touching `risk_lenses`, leaving the lens selected, owning no path, and landing in the [intentionally-skipped](#review-coordinator) state the gate legitimately accepts — the same end state a lens removal reaches, through a door nobody was watching. That is why narrowing goes where a removal goes.

The classification is a map over the contract's own fields rather than a list of the controlled ones, so a field added later cannot fail open by nobody remembering to add it: an unclassified field is a typecheck error, and the runtime half is asserted against `ReviewContractSchema`'s shape in `fg689-scope-change-control.test.ts`.

**A contract approved before `lens_scopes` existed is refused BY NAME, not as a parse error.** It reports that it *predates lens scopes*, names the lenses that still need one, and states the remedy: re-approval by its approving authority. That distinction matters operationally, because the plan-step prompt that teaches agents to author the field ships in `seeds/workflows/feature.yml` and only reaches a host through `forge upgrade` — so a host can legitimately be running new code against contracts authored under the old prompt, and "invalid input" would be both true and useless.

**Nine stages, and the next one is always a read.** `nextTransition` is a pure function of the persisted review, its findings, and its fix batches, so "what happens next" lives in SQLite rather than in the process that started the review — which is what makes `continue` after an orchestrator crash a lookup instead of a guess:

| # | Stage (`stage_evidence_json` key) | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `verified_entry` | deterministic verification at the opening candidate, reusing the existing covering-evidence and host-readiness machinery |
| 2 | `contract_confirmed` | confirm the approved contract against the final implementation diff; freeze `contract_confirmed_sha` |
| 3 | `discovery` | parallel read-only lens dispatch against that one frozen sha, then normalization/dedup |
| 4 | *(no stage record)* | the disposition stop — findings are settled through `forge review disposition` |
| 5 | `fix` | ONE fixer (role `engineer`) for the whole UNRESOLVED `fix_now` set, via an immutable [FixBatch](SCHEMA-CONTRACT.md#fix_batches--fix_batch_results-tables-fg-639-fixbatch-delivery); the coordinator then commits the cycle itself and advances the candidate to the commit it authored |
| 6 | `docs` | guaranteed docs reconciliation (role `documentation-maintainer`) **before** final verification, since it moves the candidate itself: the coordinator commits the agent's declared docs and advances to the commit it authored |
| 7 | `verified_final` | deterministic verification at the final candidate |
| 8 | `recheck` | exact recheck of known finding ids + bounded review of the post-discovery delta, by [review-rechecker](#review-rechecker) |
| 9 | `shipping` | the eight shipping checks |

**`start` opens and stops; `continue` steps.** `start` drives stages 1–3 only and then stops, printing the pending transition and the `forge review continue` command that would drive it — it never fixes anything. `continue` drives exactly **one** transition by default (`--all` keeps going while each advances), and `--dry-run` reports the one valid next transition and exits without dispatching. `--dry-run` reads the same `nextTransition` the real run does *and* goes through the same preconditions — an unknown review id and a malformed `--add-lens` or `--acceptance` refuse identically in both — so the answer it prints cannot drift from the act it previews.

**Stage completion is recorded per sha, which is what removes the reset flags.** Every stage record in `stage_evidence_json` carries the sha it completed against, and the coordinator checks them three different ways. `contract_confirmed` and `discovery` are checked against the **frozen** `contract_confirmed_sha`, which is why a fixer or docs commit can never re-open discovery. `docs`, `verified_final`, `recheck`, and `shipping` are checked against the **current** candidate, so a commit from any stage automatically re-opens the ones after it. `verified_entry` is checked on **existence alone** — it is the entry gate, it runs exactly once per review, and remediation moving the candidate does not un-verify it (the candidate that exists now is Stage 7's job).

`recheck` carries one more term: it is complete for the candidate **and** for `meta.fixCycleKey`, the batch revisions present when the review consumed its one remediation cycle. The key remains useful for crash recovery, replacement revisions created before Stage 5 completes, and reviews written by older Forge versions. It counts `ingested` **and** `superseded` batches so it can only grow. A crash-resume at an unchanged cycle therefore reads as complete and is never repeated. It is not permission for another completed remediation cycle: once the `fix` stage record exists, a changed rationale or late finding stops at disposition and cannot create revision n+1.

A new fix cycle also clears the resolutions of the findings it carried, for the same reason a moved candidate does — a verdict recorded before that cycle is a claim about pre-fix code. It clears them only for the findings that are **still `fix_now` after the cycle's results are applied**, though: a finding the fixer reports as scope-changing becomes an architecture question and is thereafter absent from every recheck's expected set, so wiping a resolution an *earlier* cycle proved would leave it NULL with nothing mechanical to re-establish it.

`fix` is the one stage whose record is checked by neither sha rule. The stage itself moves the candidate, so its recorded sha is deliberately the *pre*-fix one and comparing it would always look stale. Before completion, coverage is decided **per finding**: a `fix_now` finding is covered when an ingested batch carried it under the decision it carries now, and an interrupted cycle resumes from that durable state. Once the `fix` stage record exists, it is also the review-wide cardinality boundary. If no fix stage was needed, the completed `recheck` record closes the same remediation window so a late finding cannot become a post-recheck “first” batch. A finding newly added to `fix_now`, or re-decided with a new rationale, returns to disposition for explicit follow-up; it does not earn another fixer or recheck inside the review.

**The coordinator commits the fix cycle, and nobody else does (FG-649).** Stage 5 does not end at ingestion: it commits the fixer's work itself and then advances the candidate to the commit it just authored, automatically. The docs stage was not the precedent for that — it was left on the bare-HEAD read this replaced, and adopted the same discipline only at FG-655 (below). Commit authority for a review fix cycle used to sit with the orchestrator, and that is exactly what broke — the coordinator read HEAD immediately after ingestion, so when the orchestrator committed *after* the process had exited, the read was a guaranteed no-op and the fix stage recorded the PRE-fix candidate. Everything downstream then anchored to a tree the fixes were not in: the rechecker examined pre-fix code and honestly reported `still_present`, an unresolved `fix_now` is itself a disposition blocker, and no verb could move the candidate, so the review looped until someone re-anchored the row by hand. Owning the commit is what makes the post-fix sha *known* rather than inferred, and it is the whole fix: `advanceCandidate` stays the one place the candidate moves, so the existing per-sha stage rules do the rest with no new key — resolutions proven at the pre-fix sha are invalidated (the `review.resolutions_invalidated` event names each one), and `docs`, `verified_final`, `recheck` and `shipping` re-open against the sha the fixes are actually in.

**Only the fixer's OWN declared files are committed.** The expected change set is the fixer's own claim, read back from each result's `files_changed`, and the worktree is reconciled against it — an independent ledger, not `git add -A` with a denylist behind it. The commit is anchored before it is authored (a head that is not the candidate refuses `candidate_not_checked_out`, the same name verification uses) and the paths are passed to `git commit` itself, not just `git add`, so a concurrent process staging something else in a shared checkout cannot have it carried in. Four named refusals, each of which leaves Stage 5 open with **nothing recorded** and the review row not parked mid-stage, because a stage records itself only on success:

- `fix_cycle_declared_changes_absent` — the results declare changed files but the worktree is clean. The fixer's own ledger contradicts the tree; deliberately kept distinct from the legitimate resolved-without-a-code-change case.
- `fix_cycle_tree_dirty_outside_declared_scope` — paths moved that no result declared. Named, never swept into the commit.
- `fix_cycle_commit_failed` — the commit itself did not land.
- `fix_cycle_commit_raced` — the commit landed but not as authored: read back off the commit that actually exists, HEAD is still the candidate right after the cycle authored a commit (git reported one that is not there — a writer rewound the checkout), it does not have exactly one parent, that parent is not the candidate (for a recovered commit, the commit itself may be — see below), or it carries a path no result declared. Something else wrote the checkout mid-commit. This one does not un-commit — it refuses to **adopt**, which is the decision that matters: the sha never becomes the candidate and nothing is recorded. **The refusal is durable**: re-running `continue` without the reset it asks for hits the crash-recovery arm, which applies this same test, so the commit is refused again rather than adopted by the other door.

A cycle whose results declare no files and whose worktree is clean is a legitimate `no_change`: the candidate does not move, and `meta.fixCycleKey` still makes that cycle earn its own recheck. Any of the refusals means **the fix cycle did not complete** — re-run `forge review continue` once the named condition is resolved.

**The reconciliation runs in both directions, and only one of them is a stop.** `fix_cycle_tree_dirty_outside_declared_scope` catches a tree that moved *beyond* the declaration; the other direction is a declaration that reaches beyond the tree, and that is deliberately not the same outcome. If **nothing** moved, the claim is wholly unsupported and the cycle refuses `fix_cycle_declared_changes_absent`. If *something* moved but some declared path did not, the cycle **commits what moved and names what did not** — `committed ⊆ declared` still holds, so the commit-only-declared-paths invariant is intact, and the discrepancy rides the outcome into `meta.fixCommit.declaredNotMoved` and the operator's line rather than passing in silence. Refusing there instead would dead-end a converging review: a second cycle that re-writes a file it already committed declares it honestly and moves nothing, and no re-entry could ever change that. The recheck is what adjudicates the claim.

**The fix stage record's own sha is still the PRE-fix candidate**, because coverage is decided per finding rather than by `stageCompleteAt`; that has not changed. What changed is `meta.candidateAfter`: it carries the sha the coordinator created (with `meta.fixCommit` recording the kind, the committed paths, the fixer's declared set, whether the commit was `recognized` rather than authored on this pass, and any `declaredNotMoved` paths), where before it recorded a HEAD read that could not have moved.

**The commit subject is an interface, not a log line — and a per-revision idempotency key.** It is `fix(review): fix batch <batch-id> revision <n>`, and it deliberately does **not** reference the ticket, because a later review's comparison base is inferred from the oldest commit whose *subject* references the ticket, and a review with no base can never pass Stage 2. It is written and matched in one place, since a subject authored in one spot and recognized in another is a key that silently stops matching.

**Stage 5 never dispatches a second fixer for a revision it already ingested.** A crash between ingestion and the commit resumes at the commit: `continue` sees the batch already `ingested`, skips materialization and dispatch entirely, re-derives the results from the batch rows, and goes straight to commit → advance → record.

**A crash between the commit and the ledger writes is recovered, not re-authored.** The git commit is an irreversible external write, and the three ledger writes that record it — candidate advance, resolution invalidation, stage record — are separate transactions after it; nothing can make those atomic with git, so the only way a crash in between is recoverable is for the retry to **recognize** the commit it already made. It is identified by two terms together, never either alone: the subject names this batch revision, **and** the commit sits on the tree under review. Both crash windows land on an anchored HEAD, which is what makes that second term work: a crash *before* the candidate advance leaves HEAD's **parent** at the candidate, and a crash *after* it leaves HEAD **at** the candidate. The subject alone would adopt any commit carrying that text; the anchor alone would adopt any commit sitting on the candidate. **Recognizing a commit is adopting it, so it is decided by exactly the predicate the post-commit check applies** (RF-7): one parent, that parent (or the commit itself, once the candidate advance has landed) the candidate, and no path outside the declared set — otherwise it refuses `fix_cycle_commit_raced` under that name. Subject and anchor say *which* commit this is; they say nothing about whether it landed as this coordinator would have authored it, and a weaker recovery test is a refusal one `continue` away from being reversed. A recognized commit is reported as recovered rather than authored and carries `meta.fixCommit.recognized`, because "I committed this now" and "I recovered the commit I made before the crash" are the same sha but not the same event. Without this, the pre-advance window refused `candidate_not_checked_out` forever (HEAD is not the candidate) and the post-advance window `fix_cycle_declared_changes_absent` forever (the tree is clean, because the fixes are already committed) — the FG-649 stuck loop again, whose only exits were hand re-anchoring or re-running a fixer over already-fixed code.

**A fix batch carries the UNRESOLVED `fix_now` findings only (RF-8).** The predicate that selects the initial `batch_fix` transition and the filter that builds the payload are one definition — `fix_now`, and not already `resolved` at a NAMED candidate sha rather than "now", since the fix stage itself moves the candidate. So a `fix_now` already resolved at the current candidate is not re-dispatched, and its resolution is **preserved** — preserved *by non-membership*, since fix-cycle invalidation is scoped to batch membership. There is deliberately no per-finding "preserve" flag and no third invalidation authority: resolution invalidation stays exactly the candidate advance and the fix cycle. The narrowing is applied to what goes INTO a payload and never as a filter at ingestion, so the delivered scope and the judged scope cannot disagree. Replacement revisions remain available for a batch that has not completed Stage 5, including infrastructure recovery, but a completed stage cannot mint another revision. An empty unresolved set is a named refusal before anything is written, not an exception thrown out of batch creation.

**The coordinator commits the docs cycle too, and nobody else does (FG-655).** Stage 6 does not end when the documentation-maintainer returns: the coordinator commits the paths that agent DECLARED and advances the candidate to the commit it just authored, on exactly the Stage 5 discipline above. Before FG-655 the docs stage read HEAD and adopted whatever it found — the bare-HEAD read the fix stage's own rules forbid — so a docs agent that edited files and did not commit them left the stage reporting "candidate unchanged", a statement that was true about HEAD and false about the work. The edits stayed in the workspace, and everything after them was wrong in a way nothing named: final verification silently degraded to a dirty-tree LOCAL run and lost its CI-evidence reuse, and committing the stranded edits by hand moved HEAD while the ledger candidate stayed put, so the next stage refused `candidate_not_checked_out` and the review could only proceed by DISCARDING the docs work. It was observed nine or more times across three different docs agents; one occurrence shipped documentation asserting the opposite of shipped behaviour, and one stranded fragment was lost outright. Owning the commit is what makes the post-docs sha *known* rather than read, and `advanceCandidate` stays the one place the candidate moves, so the per-sha stage rules re-open `verified_final`, `recheck` and `shipping` against the sha the docs are actually in with no new key.

**Only the docs agent's OWN declared files are committed.** The expected change set is that agent's own `docs_updated` claim, read back off its durable task result — the task record stays authoritative and the ledger keeps an index of the dispatch's identity only, so a disagreement between them is stated rather than reconciled. The worktree is reconciled against that declaration and never the other way round: there is no `git add -A` and no scope derived from `git status`, in any path including crash recovery. The commit is anchored before it is authored, and the declared paths are passed to `git commit` itself rather than merely staged, because the index is shared with whatever else is running in the operator's checkout. The reconciliation is two-directional on the fix cycle's terms, and only one direction is a stop: a tree that moved *beyond* the declaration refuses, while a declaration that reaches beyond the tree **commits what moved and names what did not**. Six named refusals, each of which leaves Stage 6 open with **nothing recorded** — no stage evidence, no candidate movement — and each of which names a concrete action that returns the review to a state where `forge review continue` makes progress. All but one also leave the dispatch binding live, so re-entry starts no second docs agent; the exception is `docs_cycle_declared_changes_absent`, and what makes it safe is stated in its own entry:

- `docs_cycle_tree_dirty_outside_declared_scope` — paths moved that the agent's result did not declare. They are named individually (the first few, then `+N more`), never swept into the commit. Clear it by reverting the undeclared paths, or by moving them out of the workspace, then re-run `continue`.
- `docs_cycle_declared_changes_absent` — the result declares docs it changed and the worktree is clean. The agent's own ledger contradicts the tree, which is deliberately not the same as the legitimate no-op below. This refusal is reachable only with the head **at** the candidate and the tree **wholly clean**, which is provable evidence that nothing of that delivery is present in the workspace to strand — so it is the one refusal that **retires the spent dispatch binding itself**, and `continue` then runs exactly one more docs agent. It has to: the declaration is read off the immutable task record on every pass, so a refusal that left the binding live here would reproduce itself forever and no operator action could clear it. If the agent committed the edits itself, the head is not the candidate and you get the last refusal in this list instead.
- `docs_dispatch_in_flight` — the bound docs task has not reached a terminal state (a `running` row a CLI killed mid-dispatch leaves behind, for instance). A clean tree proves a *dead* delivery left nothing behind; it cannot tell that apart from a live agent that has not written yet, so this is deliberately not folded into the dead-delivery case below and **never** retires the binding. Confirm what the task is doing with `forge show <task-id>`; if its container is gone, `forge cancel <task-id>` marks it failed, and a failed delivery over a clean tree at the candidate is what lets the next `continue` retire the dead dispatch and run one more docs agent.
- `docs_cycle_commit_failed` — the commit itself did not land. Nothing is adopted, and the agent's edits are still in the worktree, so nothing is lost. The remedy has two arms and both terminate: resolve what git reported (staging the paths yourself is enough — the commit is taken from the index) and re-run `continue`; or, if those edits cannot be made committable at all, stash or discard them so the tree is **clean at the candidate**, which is what lets `docs_cycle_declared_changes_absent` retire the spent dispatch and run one more docs agent. A *staged* rename is deliberately not this refusal: `git mv` removes the original from the worktree and the index, so no `git add` can match it, and the commit carries it whole off the index instead. The tolerance is only for a path the index already fully represents — a path git does not know at all still refuses here, by name.
- `docs_cycle_commit_raced` — the commit landed but not as authored: read back off the commit that actually exists, it does not have exactly one parent, that parent is not the candidate (for a recovered commit, the commit itself may be), or it carries a path the result did not declare. Something else wrote the checkout mid-commit. Like its fix-cycle twin this does not un-commit — it refuses to **adopt**, so the sha never becomes the candidate, and the refusal is durable because the crash-recovery arm applies the same test rather than adopting through the other door. Return the checkout to the candidate with the foreign commit resolved however it deserves, then re-run `continue`.
- `candidate_not_checked_out` — the workspace head is not the candidate. This is also what a docs agent that committed its OWN work looks like, and FG-649 already pinned that case under this name. Its commit is left alone: soft-reset it back into the worktree (`git reset --soft <candidate-sha>`) so the coordinator authors the commit itself from the declaration when `continue` re-enters.

**A docs stage that genuinely changed nothing is a first-class outcome, and is deliberately distinguishable from a stranded one.** A delivery that declares nothing and leaves a **clean** tree records candidate-unchanged: the candidate does not move, the stage completes, and the tree is clean at it. A delivery that declares nothing and leaves the tree **dirty** is precisely the FG-655 stranding shape, so it refuses `docs_cycle_tree_dirty_outside_declared_scope` — every moved path is outside an empty declaration — rather than being read as the same claim. "Nothing changed" is adjudicated against the tree, never accepted on its own. The two outcomes are different records and different states, which is the whole point: "the docs really were already correct" must not render identically to "the docs work is sitting uncommitted in your workspace". A delivery that is **unreadable, absent, or terminally failed** is the third case and is likewise a refusal that records nothing and moves nothing; its remedy is restoring the checkout to the candidate, because a clean tree there is what proves the dead delivery's edits are gone and is what authorizes dispatching once more — there is no new operator verb for it. A result whose `docs_updated` field is **missing entirely, not an array, or an array carrying a non-string member** belongs to that case rather than to the no-op: the documentation-maintainer contract requires a list of paths, so a field that cannot be read as one — including a single malformed member such as `docs_updated: [42]` — is a contract violation and never the agent's claim that it changed nothing. Filtering the bad member away instead of refusing on it would silently turn that violation into the legitimate no-op, which is precisely the failure this refusal exists to prevent. A delivery that is merely **not terminal yet** is a fourth case with its own refusal (`docs_dispatch_in_flight` above), because a live agent and a dead one are not the same fact and a clean tree cannot tell them apart.

**Stage 6 never dispatches a second docs agent for a candidate it already dispatched one for.** The dispatch is bound durably before any container write — a row naming the review, the candidate sha it was dispatched for, the host-minted task id and the run — and the re-entry check consults that binding **before** it decides to dispatch, the same position the fix stage's pending-batch check occupies. A crash before the commit resumes at the commit; a crash between the commit and the candidate advance, or between the advance and the stage record, resumes by **recognizing** the commit already authored rather than authoring a second one. Recognition is identity, never permission: the commit is identified by a stage-scoped idempotency key in its subject **and** an anchored head together (the candidate is its sole parent, or is the commit itself once the advance landed), and it is then adopted only on the same predicate the post-commit check applies. The subject deliberately does not reference the ticket, for the same reason the fix cycle's does not — a later review infers its comparison base from the oldest commit whose subject does — and it is written and matched in one place.

**The docs stage record's sha is the POST-advance one**, which is where it differs from the fix stage's: Stage 6 completeness is checked against the *current* candidate, so a record written at the pre-docs sha would leave the stage incomplete the moment the docs commit moved it, and the review would loop on its own docs stage. The fix stage is exempt only because its completeness is decided per finding by ingested-batch membership; docs has no per-item ledger to appeal to.

**`blocked_environment` is a stop, not a gravestone.** When the candidate is not runnable, verification stops in the `blocked_environment` state and **dispatches nothing** — no reviewer, no fixer, no review cycle consumed. Both deterministic verification stages run the same code, so either Stage 1 or Stage 7 can stop this way. **Since FG-664 Stage 8 can too**, on two conditions that are the same fact seen from either side of the container: the rechecker's dispatch was *refused* by the [dependency-environment gate](#the-dependency-environment-gate-fg-664-fg-678) (`verification_environment_unavailable` — no container ever started), or the rechecker ran and *declared* it could not execute the coverage it cited (`blocked_environment` coverage on any ingested entry). Both stop with **nothing written** — no resolution, no new finding, no stage record — because a lane that could not run is not evidence that a finding is present *or* absent; the refusal is deliberately not the `refused` arm, which re-enters and re-dispatches and would burn rounds on a failure no code change can fix. It is deliberately a review *state* and not a transition kind: the next transition out of it re-enters **the stage that blocked**, so once the environment can execute the required verification the *same* review continues from where it stopped. Because `verified_entry` is checked on existence, a review blocked at Stage 7 resumes at Stage 7 rather than back at the entry gate. The row keeps reading `blocked_environment` until it clears, so `forge review show` reports the stop even between attempts. The most common concrete refusal is `candidate_not_checked_out` — the workspace head is not the candidate the coordinator asked about, so verification there would be an answer about a different tree; it names both shas and tells you to check the candidate out or point `--project` at the workspace that has it.

**A dirty tree left by a prior stage is a named refusal too, not a silent degradation (FG-655).** Being at the right head is no longer enough to verify: the workspace must be at the candidate **and** clean, because a verification computed over uncommitted edits is an answer about a tree nobody reviewed and nothing downstream can tell it apart from an answer about the candidate. The check sits at the two readers inside the review lifecycle — the coordinator's verification seam, which Stages 1 and 7 share, and the **Stage 9 shipping reader**, which calls loop verification directly and would otherwise bypass that seam entirely even though its result is the verification block operators read as authoritative. It stops the way `candidate_not_checked_out` does, in `blocked_environment` with nothing dispatched and nothing recorded, and it names the same three things that refusal names: the candidate, the modified paths, and the workspace they are in. Clear it by committing or reverting them — or, if they are a stranded docs stage, by letting Stage 6 commit them, which is now what Stage 6 does. It is deliberately **not** installed inside `forge review-loop`: that loop's dirty-tree local-verification arm is intended behaviour for a standalone operator with uncommitted work in the tree, and refusing there would change behaviour for every non-coordinator caller in order to enforce a review-lifecycle invariant.

**A deterministic verification failure is never laundered into a finding.** A verification that *runs* and fails stops deterministically and records no stage completion, so fixing the code and re-running `continue` re-enters the verification stage that failed. (If the fix moved the candidate, the `docs` record no longer matches it either, so docs reconciliation re-runs first — the stages after a candidate change re-open in order.) A failing test is not a reviewer's opinion, and putting it through a disposition gate built for judgement calls would let it be `accepted_risk`-ed.

**Candidate-bound evidence is invalidated when the coordinator itself moves the candidate.** The fix and docs stages are the only two that advance it — each to the commit it authored itself — and advancing is the single place that clears resolutions proven at the old sha; the `review.resolutions_invalidated` event names every finding affected. A candidate change made *outside* the coordinator is not detected this way, because a review's `candidate_sha` is never re-read from HEAD once set; it is caught at the other end instead, as a `candidate_not_checked_out` verification refusal and by the `identity_continuity` shipping check.

**The candidate is never adopted from a bare HEAD read**, and moving commit authority into the coordinator did not weaken that — FG-655 is what made it true of the code as well as of the intent. It moves only through the coordinator's own advance, and only to a sha the coordinator itself **authored or recognized**: the Stage 5 fix commit, and since FG-655 the Stage 6 docs commit, which until then was the one surviving HEAD read and the one place a sha nobody in the review had authored could become the candidate. Both candidate-moving stages now answer to the same rule, so `candidate_not_checked_out` and `identity_continuity` remain real detectors rather than tautologies the coordinator satisfies by construction. There is no convenience or migration path that re-derives it from HEAD; changing that would be a change to the lifecycle's stated semantics, decided by the authority that approved the PRD.

**The review row names the workspace its stages act on — never cwd (FG-649).** `forge review start` persists the resolved `--project` (or the cwd it defaulted to) on the review, and every later `continue` dispatches against **that** directory, so an invocation from the wrong cwd is simply irrelevant. Under the moved commit authority this stopped being a convenience: a wrong workspace used to be a wrong READ, surfaced as the `candidate_not_checked_out` stop, and would now be a WRITE into a different repository. `--project` on `continue` still overrides — and is **recorded** on the review before it is used, so the next invocation is bound to it and an operator who legitimately moved the checkout supplies the new path once rather than every time. A legacy row with no persisted workspace adopts its run's project dir when the review names a run, and records what it adopted. `--dry-run` is the one exception, and only to the *write*: a preview resolves the workspace and refuses by the same names, but binds nothing. Rebinding which repository every later stage — including the coordinator's own `git commit` — writes into is a durable change, and a preview that reports the next transition without running it may not make it. There is deliberately no silent cwd fallback; three refusals, each of which writes nothing:

- `review_workspace_unbound` — no persisted workspace and none adoptable. Names `--project`.
- `review_workspace_unusable` — the persisted path is absent, is not the root of a git worktree, or its filesystem identity could not be established at all (FG-693: neither the path itself nor the worktree root it names resolves, so it cannot be proven to BE that root). Names `--project`.
- `review_workspace_identity_mismatch` — the directory resolves to a different repository than the one the review's run recorded a project identity for. Identity, not string equality, is what is compared where the ledger can.

**Discovery is complete only when every lens authored an outcome.** Each selected lens must return a schema-valid, reviewer-authored `pass` / `fail` / `inconclusive`. An authored `inconclusive` normalizes into an untriaged `lens_inconclusive` finding — visible, not silently absent. A crash, timeout, OOM, missing, malformed, or synthesized output is **not** completion, carrying FG-628's fail-closed artifact invariant into this model. Since FG-654 a lens can also fail to complete for a reason that is not a container outcome at all: `stale_protocol`, the dispatch REFUSED before the container started because that red's [Forge-owned protocol](how-to-upgrade.md#agent-seeds-and-the-forge-owned-protocol) could not be established as current — no generation is published on this host, the published generation carries no protocol for that role, its bytes are torn (they do not match the generation's own provenance manifest), or they are BEHIND the forge that is executing. Two further cases are outside the published generation altogether: the operator's own `~/.forge/agents/<role>/CLAUDE.md` still carries an embedded copy of the protocol from before FG-654, and the forge that is EXECUTING carries no `seeds/agent-protocols/<role>.md` for that role at all, so there is no baseline left to measure the published generation's currency against. Six states, one word, because the review-side fact is identical; the refusal names which one it was. The four generation-side states clear on a bare `forge upgrade`; **the last two do not** — the embedded copy is a hand deletion, because forge does not write that file, and a release missing a covered role's protocol is repaired by reinstalling the release, since publication refuses that same missing file. Its own word in the incomplete-reason vocabulary rather than `not_dispatched`, because it is neither a wiring bug nor clearable the way the others are — see below.

**Unknown ROOT keys are tolerated and recorded; nested strictness is untouched (FG-650).** A lens output whose root carries keys the discovery schema does not name — `status`, `verdict`, `confidence`, `notes` — is no longer refused whole. The extra keys are stripped from the validated value and their names recorded, sorted, as `toleratedRootKeys`: on the lens outcome in `lens_outcomes_json`, and in the `discovery` stage record as `meta.toleratedRootKeys`, a per-lens list naming only the lenses that carried any. The rechecker's root is stripped and recorded the same way into the `recheck` stage record — it already tolerated extras, and what is new there is that they are **named**. Root strictness was refusing outcomes over a key the reviewer had no choice about — the harness output contract every agent composes requires `status` in `result.json` — so an honest review stopped at `discovery is INCOMPLETE … malformed_output: Unrecognized key` with nothing wrong in it. The tolerance is deliberately **visible**: a stripped key is named in the durable record, never silently swallowed. What did not change: every required root field is still required and still strictly typed, and the nested shapes are still closed — an unknown key inside a finding still invalidates the whole lens output (recorded as `malformed_output`, i.e. that lens produced no review), and an unknown key inside a per-id recheck result still refuses the whole recheck. A discovery finding accepts only the fields the dispatch prompt lists (plus optional `finding_type` and `hypothesis`), so a verdict-path enrichment carried onto a finding — `confidence`, `affected_files`, `recommended_fix`, `disposition` — still costs the whole lens.

**An absent lens clears three ways, and the third is a named operator decision.** Retry the lens; amend the contract through the authority that approved it; or accept the missing evidence with `forge review accept-lens <review-id> <lens>` (FG-640). Dispositioning some other finding is never one of them. The acceptance requires `--operator` without exception — narrowing the discovery coverage the approved contract states is a move of the review's own risk surface — plus `--missing-evidence "..."` naming **what was not reviewed** and `--rationale "..."` saying why that is acceptable here; an acceptance that names nothing is a blanket waiver, which is what `--force` already is. It refuses and writes nothing for a lens the contract did not select, for a review with no confirmed candidate to bind to, and for a review whose contract cannot be read. What is recorded sits in `lens_outcomes_json` **beside** the reviewer-authored outcomes but in its own shape, so nothing that reads outcomes can mistake it for a review that happened: `forge review show` renders it as its own `lens accepted:` line, the discovery stage record lists the accepted lenses separately from the ones that authored an outcome, and the `review.lens_accepted` event carries the same fields. It is bound to the confirmed candidate, so a re-confirmation at a new sha retires it with the outcomes beside it — a superseded acceptance is named in the next refusal rather than dropped.

**A lens absent for `stale_protocol` is the one absence an acceptance cannot clear (FG-654).** Completeness checks that reason *before* it consults any acceptance, so `forge review accept-lens` is not a route out of it and the gate refusal names the real remedy instead.
